It also covers no Nordic, Dutch, French, Italian, Spanish or Polish vocabulary, despite the product being aimed at European lead generation.

**Failure.** A Norwegian employer's sitemap lists 400 postings under `https://x.no/ledige-stillinger/...` and a Dutch one under `https://y.nl/vacatures/...`. `fetch_sitemap_urls` retrieves them correctly and `filter_job_urls` throws every one away, so the discovery path reports zero jobs for both companies and the operator concludes the sitemap is empty.

**Fix.** Extend the alternation with `jobb|stilling(er)?|ledige-stillinger|vacature(s)?|vacancies|emploi|offre(s)?-d-emploi|lavoro|offerte|empleo|praca|oferty|arbeit|karriere|karriar|rekruttering`, allow an end-of-string boundary (`(?:/|$)`), and make the token list configurable per region alongside the existing region configs.

### _is_junk_url rejects any URL whose path contains /home/ or /index/, and any URL whose path is just "/"

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1420` · data-quality

**What happens.** `NON_JOB_PATH_RX` (extract.py:1386) includes `home` and `index` in its alternation and matches anywhere in the path. `/home/` is an extremely common path segment on German and Swiss TYPO3/Drupal sites (`/de/home/karriere/...`), and `/index/` appears on plenty of legacy CMSes. `_is_junk_url` also returns True whenever `p.path in ("", "/")`, which rejects a perfectly valid board URL that carries its job id in the query (`https://jobs.firma.de/?gh_jid=4005`). When `_finalize` rejects a candidate it silently falls through to the next one and ultimately to `page_url`, so the wrong URL is exported without any warning. Separately, the `search\?` alternative is dead code: `_is_junk_url` matches against `p.path`, and urlparse never leaves a `?` in the path, so the intent of filtering search-result pages is unmet.

**Failure.** A Swiss company runs TYPO3 with URLs like https://firma.ch/de/home/karriere/senior-entwickler. JSON-LD gives that exact URL as the posting's `url`. `_finalize` calls `_is_junk_url`, gets True because of `/home/`, discards it, and falls through to `page_url` - which on a listing-page extraction is the listing index, not the posting. Every row for that company exports a job_url pointing at the careers index.

**Fix.** Anchor the pattern to the *first* path segment rather than searching anywhere (`^/(account|profile|...)(/|$)`), drop `home` and `index` or require them to be the sole segment, and allow `p.path in ('', '/')` when a query string is present. Delete or fix the dead `search\?` alternative by testing against the full URL if search-result filtering is actually wanted.

### baseSalary given as a bare number or string is silently dropped; unitText is exported uppercase while heuristics export lowercase

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:243` · data-quality

**What happens.** `sal = ld.get("baseSalary"); if isinstance(sal, dict): ...` with no else branch — a scalar baseSalary yields nothing. Within the dict branch, when the QuantitativeValue carries only `value` (a single figure, not a range) it is written to salary_max with salary_min left empty, so the `salary` property renders a maximum-only figure. And unitText is stored verbatim ('YEAR'/'MONTH'), whereas _apply_heuristics writes 'year'/'month'/'hour' — the column mixes two casings.

**Failure.** A posting with `"baseSalary": 62000` exports no salary at all. A posting with a single-value QuantitativeValue exports salary_max only, so the `salary` property reads '62000 EUR' while salary_min is blank and any min-salary filter excludes the row. Consumers grouping on salary_period see both 'YEAR' and 'year'.

**Fix.** Add an else branch parsing a scalar baseSalary through SALARY_RX/_normalize_amount; when only `value` is present set both min and max; and lowercase/normalise unitText to the same {year,month,hour,day,week} vocabulary the heuristics use.

### SENIORITY is matched against page text in list order and never against the title, so "Junior" postings come out "staff"

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:1056` · data-quality

**What happens.** _apply_heuristics iterates SENIORITY in declaration order (principal, staff, senior, lead, mid, junior, intern, ...) over the full page/description text and takes the first regex that fires anywhere. 'staff' and 'lead' are ordinary English words that appear in almost any company blurb ('our staff', 'you will lead'), and they are tested before junior/intern. The title — the single most reliable seniority signal — is not consulted at all here (universal_extract does check the title, so the two modules disagree).

**Failure.** An internship ('Praktikant (m/w/d)') whose page mentions 'Senior Management' is exported as seniority=senior. A buyer filtering for senior engineers receives interns.

**Fix.** Test the title first and only fall back to body text; order the list junior/intern before staff/lead; and require stronger context for the bare words 'staff'/'lead' (e.g. 'staff engineer', 'lead developer', 'team lead').

### _extract_education_experience scans 50 KB of whole-page text, so company-age and benefits boilerplate becomes experience_years

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:601` · data-quality

**What happens.** EXPERIENCE_RX's first alternative is `(\d+)\s?\+?\s?(?:-\s?\d+\s?)?(years?|jahre?n?|ans|anni)` — any number followed by a year word, with no requirement that it relate to a requirement. The function is called on the full body text (extract.py:1273, text[:50000]), where the About blurb and the benefits list usually precede the requirements section. EDUCATION_RX likewise accepts bare 'degree', 'diploma' and the two-letter 'MA'/'BA' forms.

**Failure.** A German Mittelstand posting opening with '...seit 40 Jahren...' exports experience_years=40 for a graduate role. Filtering on experience_years <= 3 drops it; showing it to a buyer looks like an error.

**Fix.** Prefer the requirements/qualifications section text over whole-page text; require a proximity qualifier (Berufserfahrung / experience / Erfahrung im Bereich / mind.) near the number; clamp accepted values to 0-20; and drop bare 'degree'/'diploma'/two-letter forms from EDUCATION_RX unless followed by a field of study.

### Every JobPosting on a multi-posting page gets the same apply_url and job_url, so all applications point at one job

`src/job_scraper/extract.py:44` · correctness

**What happens.** When a page carries N JSON-LD JobPosting blocks, extract_job_postings loops over them but calls _apply_microdata, _apply_opengraph, _apply_heuristics, _apply_recruiter and _finalize against the SAME soup for each. _find_apply_url returns the first ATS anchor on the page and _finalize falls job_url back to page_url, so all N rows share them. The heuristics likewise attribute the whole page's tech stack, emails and phones to every row.

**Failure.** A small employer's /karriere page lists three openings in one JSON-LD array. All three exported rows carry the same apply_url (job 111) and the same page URL, so two of the three leads route applicants to the wrong vacancy, and the Mechatroniker row claims a React/Kubernetes stack.

**Fix.** When len(job_postings) > 1, skip the page-wide heuristic/recruiter passes (or scope them to the ld's own description), and derive apply_url/job_url per posting from the ld's `url`/`potentialAction` only, leaving them empty rather than sharing a page-level guess.

### get_json sends navigation headers with a JSON Accept — an impossible combination that every ATS API call carries

`src/job_scraper/http.py:601` · anti-bot

**What happens.** `get_json` merges only `Accept: application/json` over the full `_stealth_headers` set, so the request goes out with `Sec-Fetch-Dest: document`, `Sec-Fetch-Mode: navigate`, `Sec-Fetch-User: ?1` and `Upgrade-Insecure-Requests: 1` alongside a JSON Accept. No browser ever produces that: a fetch/XHR sends `Sec-Fetch-Dest: empty`, `Sec-Fetch-Mode: cors`, no `Sec-Fetch-User`, and no `Upgrade-Insecure-Requests`. This is a one-rule detection. Every ATS integration routes through it — greenhouse, lever, ashby, workable, recruitee, smartrecruiters, workday, jobsch, arbeitsagentur, plus all of discovery.py — so the seed stage of every run carries the tell.

**Failure.** A SmartRecruiters or Workday API endpoint behind bot protection receives Accept: application/json together with Sec-Fetch-Mode: navigate and Upgrade-Insecure-Requests: 1. The edge returns 403. get_json returns None, the adapter reports zero listings, and the operator sees an empty seed stage with no indication that the request was malformed rather than the board empty.

**Fix.** Give get_json its own header profile: Accept: application/json, Sec-Fetch-Dest: empty, Sec-Fetch-Mode: cors, Sec-Fetch-Site: same-origin (APIs are usually same-origin with the page that calls them), an Origin and Referer matching the site, X-Requested-With where the real SPA sends it, and no Upgrade-Insecure-Requests / Sec-Fetch-User.

### head() bypasses the proxy pool and the global pacer entirely — an unconditional real-IP leak with a naked header set

`src/job_scraper/http.py:583` · security

**What happens.** `head()` calls `self._session.head(...)` with no `proxies=` argument at all, so it always issues a direct connection regardless of how many proxies are configured. It also skips `_global_pace()`, and it sends only a rotated `User-Agent` plus requests' defaults (`Accept: */*`, `Connection: keep-alive`, `Accept-Encoding: gzip, deflate, br, zstd`) — no Accept-Language, no Sec-Fetch-*, no client hints — making it a far more obvious bot request than get(). Its throttle key `f"{host}|"` also collides with the direct-connection key used by get() when the pool is exhausted, so the two share a bucket. Currently latent (no callers found outside http.py), but it is public API on a class whose entire purpose is anonymised egress, and any future caller silently de-anonymises the run.

**Failure.** A developer adds a cheap liveness probe using http.head() to filter dead company domains before the expensive crawl. Every probe goes out from the employee's real IP, unpaced, with a bare header set — so the very first contact with each of several thousand domains is a direct, obviously-automated request, priming the CDN's reputation system against the IP before the proxied crawl even starts.

**Fix.** Route head() through the same acquire/proxies/jar/stealth-header path as get(), including _global_pace and a distinct throttle key. Or delete the method, since it has no callers.

### post_json claims a Chrome UA while sending only three headers, uses no cookie jar, and applies none of the stealth profile

`src/job_scraper/http.py:633` · anti-bot

**What happens.** `post_json` builds its headers from scratch — `User-Agent` (a rotated browser UA), `Accept: application/json`, `Content-Type: application/json` — and never calls `_stealth_headers`. The request therefore has no Accept-Language, no Sec-Fetch-*, no client hints, no Origin and no Referer, while claiming to be Chrome; and requests fills in `Connection: keep-alive` and `Accept: */*`-style defaults for anything unset. It also omits `cookies=jar`, so unlike get() it does not even attempt per-proxy session continuity, meaning a POST that should carry the session established by a preceding GET carries only whatever leaked into the shared session jar. It does use the proxy (unlike head()), but its fingerprint is markedly worse than get()'s.

**Failure.** adapters/workday.py posts a CXS search body to a Workday tenant. The request declares Chrome on Windows but carries no Accept-Language, no Origin, no Referer, no Sec-Fetch-Site and no session cookie from the preceding GET of the careers page. Workday's bot protection rejects it; post_json returns None and the adapter yields zero listings with no explanation.

**Fix.** Build post_json's headers from a fetch/XHR variant of _stealth_headers (Sec-Fetch-Dest: empty, Sec-Fetch-Mode: cors, Sec-Fetch-Site: same-origin, plus Origin and Referer derived from the target URL), pass the per-proxy cookie jar, and keep the UA stable with the session that fetched the page.

### Cache key ignores the negotiated representation: get() and get_json() share one row per URL, so an HTML body can be served to a JSON caller for the whole TTL

`src/job_scraper/http.py:296` · correctness

**What happens.** `_ResponseCache` is keyed on the raw URL only (`url TEXT PRIMARY KEY`). Keying out the proxy and UA is correct — including them would collapse the hit rate — but the Accept header materially changes what the server returns, and it is excluded too. `get()` sends an HTML-first Accept; `get_json()` sends `Accept: application/json`; both write to and read from the same row. For any endpoint that content-negotiates, whichever call ran first wins for the whole TTL. The lead configs set `cache_ttl_seconds: 604800`, so a single mis-negotiated fetch poisons that URL for a week, and `json.loads` in get_json then fails silently (`logging.debug`, return None). The class docstring also claims the key is a "canonical URL", but no canonicalisation is performed: `https://x.de/a`, `https://x.de/a/`, `https://x.de/a#team` and differing query orders all occupy separate rows even when they are the same request on the wire.

**Failure.** discovery.py:52 calls http.get('https://boards.company.com/api/jobs', allow_404=True) which caches an HTML error page. Ten minutes later an adapter calls http.get_json on the same URL; the cache returns the HTML body, json.loads raises, get_json returns None, and that board yields zero listings for seven days. No log line above debug level records it.

**Fix.** Include the negotiated representation in the cache key — e.g. key on (canonicalised_url, sha1(Accept)) or simply prefix the key with 'html:' / 'json:'. Canonicalise before keying: strip the fragment, lowercase scheme+host, sort query parameters, normalise the trailing slash. Keep proxy and UA out of the key.

### Expired cache rows are never deleted on read and purge_expired has zero callers, so the SQLite file grows without bound

`src/job_scraper/http.py:285` · perf

**What happens.** `_ResponseCache.get` compares `time.time() - fetched_at > self.ttl` and returns None for an expired row, but leaves the row in place. A later `put` for the same URL does `INSERT OR REPLACE`, which reclaims the space only for URLs that are re-fetched. URLs visited once and never again — the overwhelming majority in a lead crawl over thousands of one-off company pages — accumulate forever. `purge_expired()` exists to fix this but nothing calls it: `grep -rn purge_expired` across src/, tools/ and tests/ returns only the definition. With a week-long TTL and compressed HTML bodies averaging tens of KB, the cache file grows by hundreds of MB per run on an employee machine, and `get` slows as the B-tree grows.

**Failure.** An employee runs the Europe lead harvest daily for a month. Each run caches roughly 20,000 pages that are never requested again. After 30 days .cache/http_cache.sqlite holds ~600,000 dead rows and several GB; nothing ever deletes them, and every cache lookup pays the cost of the larger index.

**Fix.** Delete the row when get() finds it expired, and call purge_expired() once at client construction (or on close()) and run VACUUM periodically. Alternatively add a size ceiling with LRU eviction on fetched_at.

### SQLite errors are unhandled and escape get(), and deep_scrape's fut.result() is unguarded — one 'database is locked' aborts an entire run

`src/job_scraper/http.py:302` · correctness

**What happens.** `_ResponseCache.get/put/delete/purge_expired` call `self._conn.execute` with no try/except (only the zlib call in put is guarded) and the connection is opened with no `busy_timeout` pragma. Within one process, `self._lock` plus `check_same_thread=False` makes that safe; across processes it is not. The tool is explicitly deployed to multiple employee machines and the repo ships run_leads.py, run.py, several tools/ scripts and a Makefile, so two concurrent runs sharing `cache_path` — or a run plus tools/extract_from_cache.py (which builds its own HttpClient at line 169) — will produce `sqlite3.OperationalError: database is locked`. That exception is raised inside get()'s try block, whose only handler is `finally` (it re-raises after releasing the slot) and inside `_cache.get()` which sits entirely outside any handler. deep_scrape then calls `fut.result()` with no try/except at line 239, so the exception propagates out of the `as_completed` loop and kills the whole deep-scrape, discarding every enrichment already completed.

**Failure.** An operator leaves a scheduled harvest running and opens a second terminal to run tools/extract_from_cache.py against the same .cache/http_cache.sqlite. The second process holds a write lock; the first process's put() raises OperationalError after the default 5s timeout; that exception escapes get(), escapes _scrape_one, and fut.result() re-raises it, terminating the ThreadPoolExecutor loop. Hours of enrichment are lost and the traceback names sqlite, not the real cause.

**Fix.** Wrap every _conn.execute in try/except sqlite3.Error and degrade to a cache miss rather than raising — the cache is an optimisation, never a correctness dependency. Add `PRAGMA busy_timeout=5000`. Guard deep_scrape's fut.result() with try/except so one worker's exception cannot abort the pool.

### Per-host politeness is multiplied by the proxy count, and main.py raises concurrency further as the pool grows

`src/job_scraper/http.py:513` · anti-bot

**What happens.** The throttle key is `f"{host}|{proxy_url}"`, so `max_inflight` and `min_delay` are enforced per (host, proxy) pair, not per host. With N proxies a single host can receive N x per_host_concurrency simultaneous requests, each pair independently satisfying its own min-delay — there is no host-global ceiling anywhere in the client. main.py then compounds it: when proxies are present it raises `deep_per_host_concurrency` to `min(8, len(proxies))` and `deep_concurrency` to `min(16, len(proxies)*2)`. The module docstring presents this as safe, but many rate limits and bot heuristics are applied per-host or per-ASN rather than per-IP (request rate to one origin, and datacenter ASN concentration), so adding proxies makes blocking *more* likely on exactly the hosts that matter. It also means the throttle silently changes meaning the moment the pool exhausts: the key collapses to `host|` and all workers pile into one bucket.

**Failure.** The operator loads 20 proxies (the fix the user is asking for) with deep_per_host_concurrency: 2. A large job board serving the whole seed then receives up to 40 concurrent requests, each (host, proxy) bucket independently honouring its 0.5s delay — roughly 80 requests/second to one origin from 20 datacenter IPs. The host rate-limits or blocks all 20 IPs at once, and because 429/403 are charged to proxy health, the entire pool cools down. Adding proxies made the run strictly worse.

**Fix.** Keep the per-(host, proxy) bucket for IP-level fairness but add a second, host-global token bucket that every request must also pass, sized independently of the pool. Remove or bound the automatic concurrency escalation in main.py. Make the host-global rate adaptive: halve it on the first 429/403 from a host and recover slowly.

### _global_pace sleeps while holding a global lock, serialising every request across all hosts and all proxies

`src/job_scraper/http.py:445` · perf

**What happens.** `_global_pace` acquires `self._global_lock` and calls `time.sleep(wait + random.uniform(0, 0.25))` *inside* the `with` block. Every thread in the process must take that lock before every GET and POST, so the pacer is a strict global serialiser: total throughput is capped at roughly `1 / (delay_seconds + 0.125)` requests per second regardless of thread count, proxy count, or how many distinct hosts are involved. `delay_seconds` defaults to 1.0 (config.py:36), which is what job_scraper/main.py uses, so a run with deep_concurrency=6 and 20 proxies still moves at about 0.9 requests per second. The lead configs set `delay_seconds: 0.0`, which sidesteps the sleep but leaves the lock as a per-request serialisation point on the hot path. This directly contradicts the module docstring's claim that proxies let you scale parallelism.

**Failure.** A job_scraper run uses the default delay_seconds: 1.0 with deep_concurrency: 6 and 10 proxies. All six workers queue on _global_lock; each sleeps ~1.1s while holding it. Six workers and ten proxies deliver 0.9 requests/second — the same as one worker with no proxies. A 20,000-URL deep scrape takes 6+ hours of pure sleeping, which reads to the operator as 'the scraper is slow/not working'.

**Fix.** Compute the next-slot timestamp under the lock, release it, then sleep outside — a classic reservation pacer. Better, make the global pacer optional and let the per-host token bucket do the pacing (which is what both lead configs already assume by setting delay_seconds: 0.0), since a global rate makes no sense once traffic is spread over thousands of distinct hosts and N egress IPs.

### robots.txt is evaluated for a User-Agent that is never sent, and RobotFileParser truncates a browser UA to the token 'mozilla'

`src/job_scraper/http.py:443` · correctness

**What happens.** `_robots_allow` passes `self.user_agent` — the single configured UA — to `RobotsCache.is_allowed`, while the actual request is sent with a UA drawn at random from `DEFAULT_UA_POOL` by `_pick_ua()`. The permission decision and the identity presented therefore do not match, which is both an ethical problem (the site's rules for the UA you claim to be are not the rules you checked) and a functional one. Compounding it, `urllib.robotparser.RobotFileParser.can_fetch` normalises the agent with `useragent.split("/")[0].lower()`, so every entry in the browser pool — and any configured browser-style UA — is matched against robots.txt as the literal token `mozilla`. Host-specific rules and, notably, `Crawl-delay` directives aimed at named crawlers are therefore never applied, and a robots.txt that happens to carry a `User-agent: Mozilla` block would be matched by accident.

**Failure.** A host publishes `User-agent: *  Disallow: /team/` and `User-agent: GoogleBot  Allow: /team/`. The client checks with the configured UA, which RobotFileParser reduces to 'mozilla', matches the `*` group, and refuses. It then never sends a request — yet if it had, it would have presented a Chrome UA. Conversely a host that sets Crawl-delay for browser-like agents has that directive silently ignored, so the client crawls faster than the host asked and earns a 429.

**Fix.** Evaluate robots against the exact UA that will be sent for that request (pick the UA before the robots check and thread it through), and fetch robots.txt with that same UA. If a stable crawler identity is wanted, use one honest product token consistently for both robots evaluation and the request, and stop rotating UAs on hosts whose robots.txt names a specific agent. Also read and honour Crawl-delay, which is currently parsed and discarded.

### Every requests.RequestException is charged to the proxy, so client-side faults cool down healthy proxies

`src/job_scraper/http.py:536` · correctness

**What happens.** The `except requests.RequestException` handler in get() (and the identical one in post_json) calls `report_failure(proxy_entry)` unconditionally. RequestException is the root of requests' exception tree, so this catches faults that have nothing to do with the proxy: `MissingSchema` and `InvalidURL` from a malformed URL in the frontier, `TooManyRedirects` from a redirect loop on the target, `ContentDecodingError` from the brotli problem above, `SSLError` from the target's own expired certificate, and `ChunkedEncodingError` from a truncated response. Each of these charges a strike against a proxy that behaved correctly, and three of them in sequence cool it for 300 seconds. Since leadgen's frontier is built from links scraped off arbitrary company sites, malformed URLs and redirect loops are routine.

**Failure.** cascade.py harvests links from a company homepage and produces three URLs with a mailto: or javascript: scheme that slipped past the guard, or three company domains with expired TLS certificates. Each raises a RequestException, each charges a strike, and after the third the proxy currently in rotation is cooled for 300 seconds — on evidence that says only 'the URL was bad' or 'the target's certificate expired'.

**Fix.** Only charge the proxy for exceptions that implicate the transport it provides: requests.exceptions.ProxyError, ConnectTimeout, and ConnectionError whose cause is a proxy CONNECT failure. Treat SSLError against the target, TooManyRedirects, ContentDecodingError and the URL-validity errors as target/client faults. Ideally validate and normalise URLs before they reach get().

### Retry(backoff_max=...) is urllib3 2.x only while nothing pins urllib3, so a clean install can crash at HttpClient construction

`src/job_scraper/http.py:378` · correctness

**What happens.** `backoff_max` was added as a `Retry.__init__` keyword in urllib3 2.0; in urllib3 1.26.x the equivalent is only the class attribute `Retry.BACKOFF_MAX`, and `Retry.__init__` declares explicit keyword parameters with no **kwargs, so passing `backoff_max=` raises `TypeError: __init__() got an unexpected keyword argument 'backoff_max'`. requirements.txt and pyproject.toml pin only `requests>=2.31.0`, and requests 2.31/2.32 permit `urllib3>=1.21.1,<3` — so a resolver that lands on urllib3 1.26 (common when another dependency caps it, e.g. older boto3/botocore in the same environment) produces an immediate TypeError inside `HttpClient.__init__`, before any request. Every entry point (run.py, run_leads.py, tools/) constructs an HttpClient, so the whole product fails to start with a stack trace that points at urllib3 rather than at a version conflict.

**Failure.** An employee installs HarvestKit into an existing Python environment that already contains a package capping urllib3 below 2 (older botocore, requests-toolbelt pins, corporate wheels). pip resolves urllib3 1.26.x. The first `python run_leads.py ...` dies with TypeError inside HttpClient.__init__ and the operator has no way to connect that message to a version constraint.

**Fix.** Pin `urllib3>=2.0` in requirements.txt and pyproject dependencies. If 1.26 must be supported, set the ceiling via the class attribute (`Retry.BACKOFF_MAX`) or build the Retry kwargs conditionally on urllib3.__version__. Ship a constraints/lock file so employee machines get a reproducible dependency set.

### get() collapses robots-deny, 403, 429, WAF-block, network error and genuinely-empty into a single None, so the operator cannot tell a bot wall from an empty result

`src/job_scraper/http.py:495` · silent-failure

**What happens.** Every failure mode in get() returns the same bare `None`: robots disallow (504), transport exception (539), 429 (544), 403 (548), any other 4xx/5xx (551), and a positive block heuristic (562). Callers therefore cannot distinguish 'this host walls us — escalate to a browser or a residential proxy' from 'this page genuinely has no people on it'. `robots_allows()` was added as a public method for exactly this reason ('so a caller can tell a blocked host apart from a broken one; those need different fixes and reporting the wrong one sends the operator chasing a phantom') — but no equivalent exists for the 403/block case, which is the dominant failure in practice. cascade.py:65 does `if not response: return` and logs nothing above debug. The result is a run that reports '0 leads' with no diagnosis, which is precisely the false green light in docs/RUNBOOK-leads.md (established fact 7).

**Failure.** A Europe run processes 800 companies and writes 40 leads. 600 of those companies returned 403 from a bot wall, 100 were dropped by robots disallow_all, 30 had genuinely no named people, and 30 hit the brotli garbage path. The operator sees one number — 40 — and no way to learn that the fix is proxies and browser fingerprinting rather than better extraction selectors. The RUNBOOK's reachability check reports every one of those hosts as healthy because 403 counts as reachable.

**Fix.** Return a small result object (or (result, reason) tuple) with a typed reason enum: OK, ROBOTS_DENIED, BLOCKED_403, RATE_LIMITED_429, BLOCK_HEURISTIC, TRANSPORT_ERROR, HTTP_ERROR, EMPTY. Have the lead pipeline tally reasons per run and print the histogram at the end. Change the runbook's health check to require a 200 with parseable HTML, not merely a non-000 status.

### The Anthropic client is created with no timeout and no retry cap — one call can park a deep-scrape worker for 30 minutes

`src/job_scraper/llm_adapter.py:226` · perf

**What happens.** `client = Anthropic(api_key=api_key)` then `client.messages.create(model=..., max_tokens=2000, messages=[...])` with no `timeout` and no `max_retries`. The SDK defaults are a 10-minute request timeout and 2 automatic retries, and timeouts are themselves retried, so worst-case wall clock is timeout × (max_retries + 1) = 30 minutes. `llm_enrich` runs inline inside `_scrape_one`, i.e. on a deep-scrape worker thread, while the host's `_LEARNING` entry is held so no other worker can learn that host either.

**Failure.** The API is degraded during a nightly run with `llm_fallback_enabled: true` and `deep_concurrency: 6`. Six unknown hosts trigger six concurrent calls; each hangs for 30 minutes; the entire pool is blocked and the run makes no progress for half an hour with nothing logged above DEBUG.

**Fix.** `Anthropic(api_key=api_key, timeout=30.0, max_retries=1)` (or `client.with_options(timeout=30.0)` per call), and log a warning when a call exceeds a few seconds so the stall is visible.

### The monthly budget cap is computed with hardcoded Haiku prices while `llm_model` is operator-configurable

`src/job_scraper/llm_adapter.py:67` · correctness

**What happens.** `HAIKU_PRICE_IN_PER_M = 1.0` / `HAIKU_PRICE_OUT_PER_M = 5.0` are module constants used by `_record_usage` and by the log line in `llm_enrich`, but the model is a free-form config field (`RunConfig.llm_model`) threaded through `DeepScrapeConfig.llm_model`. Nothing checks that the configured model is actually Haiku. The `llm_monthly_budget_usd` "hard cap" therefore tracks a fiction whenever the model is changed. (The default id `claude-haiku-4-5-20251001` is itself valid and current, and $1/$5 per MTok is the correct Haiku 4.5 rate — the bug is the coupling, not the numbers.)

**Failure.** An operator sets `llm_model: claude-opus-5` to improve selector quality. Opus 5 is $5/$25 per MTok — 5× the assumed rate — so the tracked spend reaches the $5.00 cap only after $25.00 of real usage, and the budget guard silently fails to guard.

**Fix.** Keep a `{model_id_prefix: (in_price, out_price)}` table, look the configured model up in it, and refuse to run (or log a loud warning and assume the most expensive tier) when the model is unknown. Also record usage before checking the cap on the next call so concurrent workers cannot each pass a check that only one should.

### A host whose cached selector map underperforms is re-learned on every page, burning the monthly budget on one host

`src/job_scraper/llm_adapter.py:314` · correctness

**What happens.** On a cache hit, the map is applied and an early return happens only if `_count_filled(j) >= min_fields`. Otherwise control falls through to the paid learning path, which buys a fresh map and `_save_map`s it over the previous one. Nothing records "this host was learned recently and the map is as good as it gets", so a host whose pages are heterogeneous (some rich, some sparse) pays for a new map on every sparse page, capped only by the `_LEARNING` one-at-a-time guard and the budget.

**Failure.** A large board whose postings vary (some with salary and recruiter blocks, most without) is scraped with `llm_min_fields: 5`. The first page yields a good map; every sparse page after it still falls below 5 filled fields, so each one buys another ~$0.02 map for the same host. The $5 monthly budget is exhausted on one host within a single run, and every other unknown host gets nothing for the rest of the month.

**Fix.** Add a `learned_at` / `attempts` column and skip re-learning a host for N days (or after K failed improvements). Return early after a cache hit unless the cached map added literally zero fields, and only overwrite the stored map when the new one fills strictly more fields than the old one did.

### canonicalize_url strips the fragment, collapsing every job on a hash-routed career board to the board root

`src/job_scraper/models.py:59` · data-quality

**What happens.** `parsed._replace(fragment="", ...)` unconditionally discards the fragment. That is correct for tracking anchors but wrong for the many SPA career sites (Angular/React boards, several white-label ATS front-ends) that route with `#/`. Because every adapter writes `apply_url=canonicalize_url(job_url)` and `job_url=canonicalize_url(job_url)`, the truncation is what lands in the CSV - so the exported apply link points at the board index, not the posting, and is useless to whoever works the leads. It also feeds `fingerprint()` (models.py:270), so two different postings on such a board that share a title, company and location collapse into one row.

**Failure.** A crawl of a Swiss company running a hash-routed careers SPA extracts 40 postings, each with job_url https://firma.ch/karriere#/job/<id>. All 40 canonicalise to https://firma.ch/karriere. Distinct titles keep them from fully deduping, but every exported apply_url and job_url is the same dead index URL, so the entire company's rows are unactionable.

**Fix.** Keep the fragment when it begins with `/` or contains `=` (the SPA-routing shapes) and strip it otherwise; or strip only fragments matching a known tracking-anchor allowlist. Strip the fragment for the *fingerprint* only if you also keep the full URL in the exported field.

### canonicalize_url does not normalise host case or default ports, so the same posting can export twice under two ids

`src/job_scraper/models.py:55` · data-quality

**What happens.** `canonicalize_url` normalises scheme-case only as far as urlparse does, leaves `netloc` exactly as given, and leaves an explicit `:443`/`:80` in place. `fingerprint()` then lowercases the whole canonical string, which fixes host case for the dedupe key but not for the exported `apply_url`/`job_url` columns, and does not fix the port at all. Two adapters reaching the same posting by different link spellings therefore produce two ids.

**Failure.** The generic adapter's sitemap pass yields https://firma.de:443/stellen/dev-9 (some sitemap generators emit the explicit port) while the BFS crawl yields https://firma.de/stellen/dev-9. `seen_urls` in generic.py:69 does not match them, both are appended, and the CSV carries the same job twice with two different `id` values - which also double-counts against `stop_after` quotas.

**Fix.** In canonicalize_url, lowercase the hostname, drop `:443` for https and `:80` for http, and collapse a repeated path separator before the final `rstrip('/')`.

### robots.txt is fetched with urllib directly, bypassing the proxy pool and leaking the operator's real IP on every new host

`src/job_scraper/robots.py:61` · anti-bot

**What happens.** RobotsCache._fetch calls urllib.request.urlopen with no proxy handling, while every other request in the client goes through _ProxyPool (http.py:511-533). Since _robots_allow runs before any GET, the very first contact with each host comes from the machine's own IP regardless of proxy configuration - which defeats the premise of deploying this on employee devices behind proxies, and gives a WAF a clean correlation between the direct robots fetch and the proxied traffic that follows.

**Failure.** An employee runs a lead crawl of 3,000 company domains through a proxy pool; 3,000 robots.txt requests still originate from the employee's real IP, which is the IP that gets blocked - and the operator sees proxy stats showing healthy proxies while the run still fails.

**Fix.** Fetch robots.txt through the same requests.Session with the same proxy entry and UA rotation as the request that triggered it, honouring the same timeout and cache.

### Email placeholders pass the junk filter while two major Norwegian employers' real domains are blocked

`src/job_scraper/universal.py:55` · data-quality

**What happens.** `JUNK_EMAIL_HOSTS` blocks `example.com` but not `email.com`, so the literal Norwegian template placeholder `fornavn.etternavn@email.com` ("firstname.lastname@email.com") is accepted as a real contact. The same list blanket-blocks `@finn.no` and `@nav.no`, which are not junk domains — NAV is one of Norway's largest employers of software engineers and posts its own roles on its own board with `@nav.no` contact addresses.

**Failure.** Rows are delivered with `application_email=fornavn.etternavn@email.com` — an address that looks plausible in a CSV and bounces on send. In the other direction, a NAV-posted NAV engineering role (live example: `Nyutdannet utvikler 2027`, Nav Teknologi) has its genuine `@nav.no` recruiter address stripped by the junk filter.

**Fix.** Add a placeholder-pattern check (local part matching `fornavn|etternavn|firstname|lastname|firstname\.lastname|name@|din\.epost|your\.email`, and hosts `email.com|domain.com|company.com|mail.com`), and replace the blanket `@nav.no`/`@finn.no` block with a rule that only rejects those domains when they are the *source board* of the posting, not when they are the hiring employer.

### jobbsafari overwrites the structured employer name with a fragment lifted out of the description

`src/job_scraper/universal.py:1015` · data-quality

**What happens.** `_jobbsafari_specific` distrusts `jobEntry.company.name` on the theory that it is often the poster rather than the employer, and unconditionally replaces it with whatever an `"Om stillingen" <Capitalised words>` regex captures out of the ad body. On a public-sector posting that phrase is followed by the *department*, not the employer, and the assignment at line 1015 has no guard. `merge`'s longest-wins rule then cements the longer, wrong value.

**Failure.** A lead row names "Fana og Ytrebygda hjemmebaserte tjenester" (a home-care district) as the company instead of Bergen Kommune. The leadgen domain guesser (commit 760dc2a, "guess domains from the trading name") is then handed a department name and will resolve no domain, or the wrong one — so the row yields no person at all.

**Fix.** Only prefer the description-derived employer when `company.name` is empty or matches a known staffing-agency/poster list; never overwrite a non-empty structured `hiringOrganization`/`company.name`. Keep the description-derived value in `extras.department_hint` instead.

### jobbsafari writes raw newlines into the location field

`src/job_scraper/universal.py:1035` · data-quality

**What happens.** `parts = [area.get("name",""), first.get("address",""), first.get("zipCode","")]` then `", ".join(...)` with no whitespace normalisation. jobbsafari's `locations[].address` is a multi-line postal block, so the newlines and the duplicated place name land verbatim in `JobListing.location`.

**Failure.** The CSV location cell contains embedded line breaks. Any consumer that splits on newlines (a spreadsheet import with the wrong quoting, a downstream `awk`/`cut`, the Slack export) sees a row break mid-record. `region` is also populated with the city rather than the county that is sitting right there in the same address block (Viken / Vestland).

**Fix.** Run each part through `_normalize` (which already collapses whitespace) before joining, dedupe repeated tokens, and map the address block's county segment to `region` instead of copying `city`.

### recruiter_name is the person's name concatenated with their job title, and can be paired with a different person's email

`src/job_scraper/universal.py:165` · data-quality

**What happens.** `_split_name_role` is applied to the whole contact-section head, and on every European board tested it fails to cut the role off the name. Worse, `recruiter_name` and `recruiter_email` are resolved by independent paths (section-head parsing vs. document-wide email mining), so when a posting lists two contacts the exported row pairs one person's name with the other person's address.

**Failure.** A mail-merge on {{first_name}} greets someone as "Contact" or addresses Aina Nordhagen as Suzanne. Neither the name nor the name/email pairing is usable for outreach, which is the entire product.

**Fix.** Extract name and role as a pair from the same DOM node and keep them in separate fields (`recruiter_name`, `recruiter_title`); strip leading label words (Contact / Kontakt / Kontaktperson) and trailing role nouns via a role lexicon. When a section yields both a name and an email, bind them together and never fill `recruiter_email` from a document-wide scan once a named contact block has been found.

### The same human produces several Leads, inflating every funnel counter and tripping the early-stop prematurely

`src/leadgen/assemble.py:164` · data-quality

**What happens.** `cascade.merge_hits` dedupes on the *raw* extracted name (cascade.py:38, `_key(hit.name)`), but `_clean_hits` normalises names afterwards — stripping leading titles and splitting multi-person lines. So two hits that merge to one person only after cleaning survive as two hits, and `build_leads` emits one Lead each. `build_leads` itself performs no dedupe. pipeline.py:85-91 then counts `people_found`, `with_email` and `target_role` from that inflated list, and pipeline.py:112 compares `with_email` against `stop_after`.

**Failure.** Verified: an Impressum yields 'Dr. Anna Mueller' and a team page yields 'Anna Mueller'. merge_hits keeps both (different raw keys). build_leads returns 2 Leads, both with anna.mueller@f.ch and both with fingerprint a0974f6406 — so the checkpoint stores one (INSERT OR REPLACE, arbitrary winner for role/source_url) while the funnel counts 2 people_found and 2 with_email. Across a run with German Impressum pages (where honorifics are the norm) this inflation is systematic, so `stop_after = target * overfetch` is reached well before that many distinct people are banked, and the crawl quits early — directly worsening the shortfall it was meant to prevent.

**Fix.** Run the cleaning/splitting normalisation *before* merge_hits (or re-merge after `_clean_hits` inside build_leads) so one human is one hit, and dedupe the returned leads by fingerprint in build_leads before pipeline counts them. Note the comment at scripts/daily.sh:107 already shows an operator working around this by setting --overfetch 0.

### Two real people sharing one mailbox collapse into a single row and one of them is deleted from the checkpoint

`src/leadgen/assemble.py:69` · data-quality

**What happens.** When `_clean_hits` splits a multi-person line, every split person inherits the same `hit.email`. `_address_echoes` can legitimately pass for more than one of them — a shared family-name mailbox echoes both siblings' names. Both Leads then get email_status 'published' with the identical address, hence the identical fingerprint (models.py:94), and `save_leads`' INSERT OR REPLACE (checkpoint.py:89) silently overwrites one with the other. The loss is in the checkpoint, not just in the cut, so no `--select-only` re-run can recover the discarded person.

**Failure.** Verified: a hit with name 'Anna Mueller und Peter Mueller' and email 'mueller@f.ch' produces two Leads, both with person_email='mueller@f.ch', both status 'published', both fingerprint b3823cdefa. `save_leads` writes one row; whichever of the two real executives was assembled second is the only one that survives. quota.py:106 would in any case drop the other as a `duplicate`.

**Fix.** When `split_people` returns more than one name, only the part whose name the address actually distinguishes may claim it — or, more safely, treat the shared address as un-attributable, fall through to the pattern branch for all split parts, and keep the raw address in evidence rather than in person_email.

### all_leads() loads the entire history unordered, making dedupe winners arbitrary and the daily run progressively slower

`src/leadgen/checkpoint.py:99` · perf

**What happens.** `SELECT payload FROM leads` has no ORDER BY, no LIMIT and no filter. Every run deserialises the complete lead history into memory and scores all of it. Because the order is whatever SQLite returns, the `seen_ids`/`seen_people` first-wins dedupe in quota.py:105-111 keeps a non-deterministic winner: for a person found at two companies (the same email under two domains), which company they are attributed to depends on physical row order, and it can change after any INSERT OR REPLACE rewrites a page. The `leads` table is also never pruned — superseded fingerprints (see the fingerprint-churn finding) accumulate forever.

**Failure.** After six months of daily --only-new runs the checkpoint holds ~250,000 lead rows, many of them stale fingerprints for people already delivered under a different derived address. Every morning the run JSON-decodes all 250,000, builds 250,000 Lead objects and scores them, before selecting 300 — several minutes of CPU and hundreds of MB, growing without bound. Separately, a re-run of `--select-only` on an unchanged checkpoint can attribute a duplicated person to a different company than the previous run did, so two "identical" re-cuts differ.

**Fix.** Add `ORDER BY id` (or a `scraped_at` column with an index) for determinism, and either stream with a cursor or push the `--only-new` and country filters into SQL (`WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT id FROM delivered)`) instead of materialising everything. Add a retention/compaction path that drops superseded rows for the same person.

### --countries accepts unvalidated garbage and silently yields zero or partial results

`src/leadgen/cli.py:93` · correctness

**What happens.** `_country_set` special-cases only the exact strings 'eu' and 'europe'; anything else is uppercased token-by-token and used as-is as an ISO-3166 alpha-2 code. Nothing validates the result against EU_COUNTRIES/EFTA_AND_UK or geo.COUNTRY_NAMES. A bogus code matches no lead at quota.py:85 and names no search location at geo.search_names, so the effect is silent under-delivery rather than an error.

**Failure.** Verified: `--countries uk` -> frozenset({'UK'}), and search_names({'UK'}) == [] — the UK's code is GB everywhere in geo.py. Every British lead is dropped as wrong_country and the geography-first search seed gets zero locations. `--countries eu,ch` -> frozenset({'EU','CH'}) — the 'eu' expansion only fires when it is the *whole* argument, so this asks for Switzerland plus a country that does not exist, quietly discarding the 27 EU states the operator asked for. `--countries Germany` -> frozenset({'GERMANY'}), zero rows. In all three cases the run completes and exits 2 (shortfall), which scripts/daily.sh:172 reports as "Done, but short".

**Fix.** In `_country_set`, expand 'eu'/'europe' per-token (so 'eu,ch' works), map each remaining token through geo.COUNTRY_NAMES first so 'uk' and 'Germany' resolve to GB/DE, and raise `parser.error(...)` listing the unrecognised tokens for anything still unknown. Fail at argument-parse time, before hours of crawling.

### --roles is case- and spelling-sensitive with no validation, so a typo empties the deliverable

`src/leadgen/cli.py:395` · correctness

**What happens.** `--roles` is split and stripped but never lower-cased or checked against the known families (roles.TARGET_FAMILIES + 'other'). `--roles any` is normalised with `.lower()` on line 397, but the family names themselves are not, so the argument is inconsistent with itself and with `--countries`, which does uppercase its tokens.

**Failure.** Verified with the real parser and `select`: `--roles HR` produces frozenset({'HR'}); every lead's person_role_family is 'hr', so all of them are dropped with `wrong_role` and the CSV contains only a header. Same for `--roles Hr` and `--roles tech-leadership` (hyphen instead of underscore — the natural typo, and the form the runbook's prose uses when it says "technical leadership"). The run exits 2 and daily.sh calls it "Done, but short of 300 rows", so a multi-hour harvest is thrown away over a capital letter.

**Fix.** Lower-case each token and validate against `set(TARGET_FAMILIES) | {'other'}`, calling `parser.error()` on anything unrecognised. Accept '-' as a synonym for '_'.

### _collect_listings swallows every seed failure, making a dead seed indistinguishable from a genuine shortfall

`src/leadgen/cli.py:82` · silent-failure

**What happens.** Every adapter exception is caught and downgraded to `log.warning`, and the per-target line at cli.py:80 is only logged on success. Nothing counts how many targets failed, and nothing propagates that to the exit code. Combined with the exit-2-on-shortfall convention (cli.py:429-431) and daily.sh:172's treatment of exit 2 as normal, a completely dead seed layer reports the same way as a merely thin day.

**Failure.** All five configured targets raise (expired credentials, moved API path, host-level robots 403). The log contains five `seed: X failed: ...` warnings buried in hours of DEBUG-adjacent output, then `seed: 0 listings total`. The run continues, exports whatever is in the checkpoint, and exits 2. daily.sh prints "Done, but short of 300 rows. See the SHORTFALL line above." Nobody investigates, because that is the documented normal state of a mature daily run.

**Fix.** Count failures in `_collect_listings` and return them alongside the listings; log an ERROR (not WARNING) summarising `N of M seed targets failed`, include it in the final printed summary, and return a distinct non-zero exit code when every configured seed failed.

### cli.py logs "skipping search seed" and then does not skip it, running SmartRecruiters globally with no geography

`src/leadgen/cli.py:332` · silent-failure

**What happens.** cli.py:332-333 reads `if keywords and not names: log.warning("seed: --search-keywords needs --countries; skipping search seed")`. Execution then falls straight through to :340-350, which calls `search_all(...)` regardless. Inside `search_all` (src/leadgen/seed/jobsearch.py:203-238), the Workable half maps over `countries=[]` and contributes nothing, but the block at :233-237 runs `search_smartrecruiters(http, keywords=smartrecruiters_keywords)` unconditionally — and as jobsearch.py's own docstring (:15-17) explains, SmartRecruiters accepts no geography parameter. docs/RUNBOOK-leads.md:60-62 repeats the wrong story: "without one there is nowhere to search and the seed returns nothing. The run warns rather than failing silently."

**Failure.** An operator runs with `--search-keywords-multilingual` but forgets `--countries`. The log says the search seed was skipped. It was not: SmartRecruiters returns employers worldwide, `_country_set("")` returns None (cli.py:95-97), so `companies_from_listings` applies no country filter and `select` applies no country filter — and the operator gets a "European" lead list with US and APAC companies in it, having been told the seed did not run.

**Fix.** Either actually skip (guard the `search_all` call on `names or multilingual`) or change the message to say what happens: the location-paired half is skipped and the multilingual half still runs globally. Fix RUNBOOK:60-62 to match.

### domain.py parses urlparse().netloc instead of .hostname, so ports, userinfo and trailing dots become part of the mail domain

`src/leadgen/company/domain.py:99` · correctness

**What happens.** Both is_company_site and resolve_domain read urlparse(url).netloc, which includes userinfo and the port, while net_guard correctly uses .hostname. The raw netloc is then returned as the company domain and used verbatim as the mail domain by apply_pattern, producing addresses that fail valid_syntax — so the company yields zero emails with no diagnostic. The userinfo form additionally defeats the ATS denylist in one direction: 'https://boards.greenhouse.io@acme.de/' has a netloc that neither equals nor ends with any denylisted host, and the URL actually resolves to acme.de.

**Failure.** Verified with a stub http client: 'https://acme.de:8443/' -> domain 'acme.de:8443'; 'https://acme.de./' -> domain 'acme.de.'; 'https://boards.greenhouse.io@acme.de/' -> domain 'boards.greenhouse.io@acme.de'. valid_syntax rejects 'a.b@x.de.' and every address built on those, so all leads for that company are silently discarded.

**Fix.** Use urlparse(url).hostname (which lowercases, strips userinfo and the port) in _registrable, and strip a trailing dot. Reject any candidate host that still contains '@', ':' or whitespace before returning it.

### A null MX record (RFC 7505) passes has_mx, so addresses are minted for domains that declare they accept no mail

`src/leadgen/email/validate.py:188` · correctness

**What happens.** has_mx returns bool(_resolve(domain,'MX')), which is True for a domain publishing the RFC 7505 null MX ('0 .') — an explicit declaration that the domain receives no mail. _mx_host then rstrips the '.' to '', is_catch_all returns None, and validate reports 'unknown', which assemble maps to inferred_high/inferred_medium. The strongest possible negative signal about deliverability is thus converted into a mid-tier lead.

**Failure.** Verified with a stubbed resolver returning preference=0, exchange='.': has_mx('nullmx.example')=True, _mx_host=''; validate returns EmailVerdict(status='unknown', reason='server would not answer the probe'), and the derived address is exported. example.com itself publishes '0 .' (live-checked), as do many brand-protection and redirect-only domains that parked-page guessing lands on.

**Fix.** In has_mx, treat a single MX whose exchange is '.' (or empty after rstrip) as no MX at all and return False. Do the same check in _mx_host so the SMTP probe is never attempted against a null MX.

### The SMTP catch-all probe has no per-MX dedupe, no rate limiting, a default HELO name and a static fingerprintable probe address

`src/leadgen/email/validate.py:220` · anti-bot

**What happens.** is_catch_all is cached per domain, not per MX host, and is called from build_leads inside the pipeline's ThreadPoolExecutor (default concurrency 8, cli.py:192). Thousands of customer domains share a handful of MX hosts — aspmx.l.google.com, *.mail.protection.outlook.com — so one run opens thousands of port-25 connections from one IP to the same few MXes, every one of them issuing MAIL FROM verify@example.com (null MX, spf -all) and RCPT to the same static local part zz-harvestkit-probe-9f3a1c. smtplib.SMTP is constructed without local_hostname, so the HELO name is socket.getfqdn() — typically a non-resolvable private hostname on an employee laptop, which many MTAs reject outright. Nothing here honours the 'politeness' the module docstring claims, and the pattern is the textbook shape that earns an IP block — feeding straight back into the 'everything is 403/blocked' symptom. Separately, outbound port 25 is filtered by most consumer ISPs and blocked by default on AWS/GCP/Azure, so on the deployment target described (employee devices) each new domain costs an 8-second connect stall in the hot path for a result that is always None.

**Failure.** A 2,000-company EU run where ~60% are on Google Workspace: ~1,200 connections to aspmx.l.google.com from one IP within the run, each RCPT'ing the same invented local part. Google rate-limits and then 421/554s the source, which per the finding above is read as 'ok' -> those leads ship as 'verified'. On a laptop with port 25 filtered, the same run stalls 8 s x (number of distinct domains) with nothing to show for it.

**Fix.** Key the cache on the MX host as well as the domain and skip the probe entirely for known big-provider MXes (their behaviour is already known). Add a per-MX token bucket and a global cap on probes per run. Pass an explicit local_hostname that matches a real forward-confirmed A record. Randomise the probe local part per domain. Detect a blocked port 25 once at startup (single connect test) and disable SMTP probing for the run instead of paying the timeout per domain.

### is_role_account rejects real people and misses common European shared mailboxes

`src/leadgen/email/validate.py:149` · data-quality

**What happens.** The stem heuristic splits the local part on [-_.+0-9] and tests the first fragment against ROLE_LOCALPARTS. That produces false positives whenever a person's first name or a name fragment happens to be a role word — 'post' is a common Dutch and German surname, 'team', 'pr' and 'personal' collide with real name tokens — causing a genuinely published personal address to be discarded (and the person's address to be re-derived or dropped). It also misses a long list of real European shared mailboxes, which then flow on as pattern anchors (see the anchor-poisoning finding) and as candidate 'published' addresses.

**Failure.** Verified false positives: post.mueller@x.de, team.mueller@x.de, pr.schmidt@x.de and personal.trainer@x.de all return True. Verified false negatives: stellen@, stellenangebote@, kundendienst@, verkauf@, zentrale@, empfang@, reception@, anfragen@, ausbildung@, praktikum@, apply@, applications@, cv@, talentacquisition@ and peoplemanagement@ all return False.

**Fix.** Only apply the stem rule when the remainder after the role word is a pure suffix (digits, a country code, a year) rather than another name-shaped token — i.e. require the local part to have no second alphabetic token, so 'info-de' is a role but 'post.mueller' is not. Extend ROLE_LOCALPARTS with the missing entries and match multi-word compounds (talentacquisition, peoplemanagement, stellenangebote, kundendienst) by substring against a small set of role stems.

### Unicode NFD input silently defeats country inference

`src/leadgen/geo.py:271` · correctness

**What happens.** COUNTRY_NAMES and CITY_NAMES are written in NFC (precomposed 'ü' as U+00FC), and country_from_location does no normalisation of the input. HTML from macOS-authored CMSs and several JSON APIs serve NFD (decomposed 'u' + U+0308), which is a different string, so neither the exact-match pass nor the \b regex pass finds it. The location then resolves to '' and jobboard drops the company from a geography-filtered run.

**Failure.** Verified: country_from_location(unicodedata.normalize('NFD','München')) returns '' while the NFC form returns 'DE'; the NFD form of 'España' returns '' while 'españa' returns 'ES'. Any seed whose location field arrives decomposed loses every company whose only geographic clue is an accented place name.

**Fix.** Normalise the input with unicodedata.normalize('NFC', text) at the top of _tokens (and normalise the table keys the same way at import), so both forms compare equal. A belt-and-braces alternative is to also index an accent-folded copy of both tables.

### resolve_people spends roughly 30 requests per company with no early exit and fetches several URLs twice

`src/leadgen/person/cascade.py:166` · perf

**What happens.** The docstring says 'Bounded on purpose: eight pages per company', but the actual budget is 8 candidate paths + 1 homepage + up to 6 navigation pages + up to 5 sitemap documents + up to 10 per-person pages — about 30 requests — and there is no early exit once people have been found. Two of those are pure waste on every DE company: BY_COUNTRY['DE'] contains both `/das-team` and `/team/` while UNIVERSAL contains `/team`, so `/team` and `/team/` are both fetched; and `seen_urls` is built from `f"https://{domain}{path}"` while `discover_people_pages` resolves hrefs against the *final* URL after redirects, so on any site that canonicalises to www the same page is fetched again under a different host spelling. With a per-host token bucket and global pacing this multiplies the wall-clock cost of a run by roughly 4x versus stopping as soon as a page yields targeted people.

**Failure.** A 5,000-company run at 8 workers with the default per-host pacing issues ~150,000 requests instead of the ~40,000 it needs, so the run does not finish overnight; the operator kills it partway and the checkpoint has recorded every company it touched, including the ones it only half-crawled.

**Fix.** Normalise candidate URLs (strip the trailing slash, lowercase the host, canonicalise www/bare against the homepage's final URL) into a single `seen_urls` set consulted by `_harvest`. Add an early exit: stop the cascade once N hits classify into a target family, and skip the sitemap stage entirely when the guessed paths already produced targeted people.

### 'Dipl.-Ing.' and 'Lastname, Firstname' forms fail the name gate and drop the person entirely

`src/leadgen/person/name.py:74` · correctness

**What happens.** Two distinct failures at the final gate in assemble._clean_hits. (1) `_TOKEN_RX` allows a dot only at the very end of a token, so 'Dipl.-Ing.' (an interior dot) fails and the whole name is rejected; `strip_leading_title` cannot save it because 'dipl.-ing' is not in `_NOT_A_NAME`, and `roles._TITLE_RX` cannot strip it because that pattern requires whitespace right after the optional dot ('dipl' + '.' + '-' breaks it). Dipl.-Ing. / Dipl.-Kfm. / Dipl.-Betriebsw. are standard on German and Austrian Impressums. (2) Surname-first order 'Müller, Hans' fails the token regex on the comma, and `split_people` deliberately refuses to split it, so the person is dropped; when such a name does survive elsewhere, `roles.split_name` returns ('Müller,', 'Hans') — first and last swapped, with a comma inside the first name, which `apply_pattern` then slugs into a wrong address.

**Failure.** An Austrian GmbH's Impressum reads 'Geschäftsführer: Dipl.-Ing. Hans Müller'. impressum.extract finds him, merge_hits keeps him, and assemble._clean_hits drops him because looks_like_person_name is False. The company is exported with no people at all. Separately, a Swiss team page listing 'Müller, Hans' loses him outright, and any path that keeps such a name produces 'mueller,.hans@' style garbage from apply_pattern.

**Fix.** Extend `_NOT_A_NAME`/`strip_leading_title` (or add a dedicated honorific stripper) covering `dipl`, `dipl.-ing`, `dipl.-kfm`, `mag`, `lic`, `med`, `bsc`, `msc`, `mba` with interior dots and hyphens; loosen `_TITLE_RX` in roles.py to `^(?:dr|prof|dipl|ing|…)[.\-\s]+`. Add a surname-first normaliser: when a name is exactly 'A, B' with both halves plausible single-token names, rewrite it to 'B A' before the shape test rather than dropping it.

### Norwegian 'Are' and Swedish 'My' are treated as leading stopwords, rejecting real people in configured Nordic markets

`src/leadgen/person/name.py:79` · correctness

**What happens.** `_LEADING_STOPWORDS` rejects any name whose first token is a determiner or pronoun. The list includes 'are', 'my', 'new', 'all', 'us', 'we', 'who', 'why'. 'Are' is a common Norwegian male given name and 'My' is a common Swedish female given name, and paths.py configures both NO and SE (and the seed layer reaches finn.no / jobbsafari.no). Any lead whose first name is Are or My is dropped at the final gate with no log above debug.

**Failure.** A Norwegian run finds 'Are Hansen, Personalsjef' on a team page. The name passes every extraction stage and is dropped by assemble._clean_hits because tokens[0].lower() == 'are'. The company reports no_person_found.

**Fix.** Only apply the leading-stopword test when the token is lowercase in the source text, or when the rest of the name also fails a positive signal. A determiner in a slogan is nearly always followed by more stopwords or by a `_NOT_A_NAME` token, so requiring a second piece of evidence before rejecting keeps 'Our Guiding Principles' out while letting 'Are Hansen' through. At minimum, drop 'are', 'my' and 'new' from the set and rely on the existing vocabulary and particle checks.

### looks_like_person_name caps names at four tokens, dropping standard Iberian full names

`src/leadgen/person/name.py:131` · correctness

**What happens.** `if not 2 <= len(tokens) <= 4: return False`. Spanish and Portuguese naming convention routinely produces five or more tokens (two given names plus paternal and maternal surnames, with a particle), and paths.py configures ES and PT. Those names are dropped at the final gate. impressum.py's own `_looks_like_a_person` allows up to 5 tokens, so the two gates disagree and a person that impressum.extract accepts is discarded by assemble._clean_hits.

**Failure.** A Spanish company's /quienes-somos names 'María del Carmen García López, Directora de Recursos Humanos'. team.extract accepts her (its regex allows up to 4 tokens... it also rejects her), impressum.extract would accept her, and assemble._clean_hits drops her for having five tokens. The ES market under-reports HR contacts systematically.

**Fix.** Raise the upper bound to 5 or 6 when the extra tokens are particles or short given names, and align the cap with impressum.py's. Count particles separately from name words: allow up to 4 non-particle tokens plus any number of particles.

### Names beginning with a nobiliary particle are dropped entirely from commercial-register results

`src/leadgen/person/register.py:286` · correctness

**What happens.** `_name_and_role` walks the comma-separated fields and stops collecting name fields as soon as one matches `_AFTER_NAME_RX`, whose first alternative is `^(?:von|de|da|di|del|della|dello|des|du|…)\b`. That test is meant to catch the origin clause ('von Zürich', 'de Genève') that follows a name, but it fires on the *first* field when the surname itself starts with a particle. Swiss registers are full of these: von Arx, von Grünigen, von Ballmoos, de Meuron, della Valle, du Pasquier. The function returns ('','') and the person is silently skipped. Because the register is the last-resort source for companies that publish nobody, these people are lost outright.

**Failure.** A Swiss AG whose board chair is 'von Arx, Beatrice' and whose only other registered person is the auditor (correctly filtered as a legal entity). people_from_register returns [], the company is recorded as no_person_found, and the one source that was guaranteed to name a decision maker produced nothing.

**Fix.** Only treat a particle as the start of an origin clause when it is not the first field of the entry, or when the token after it is a known place rather than a capitalised surname. Simpler and safer: never apply `_AFTER_NAME_RX` to `fields[0]` — the register always puts the name first — and only use it to terminate collection at `fields[1]` onwards.

### Rejected Zefix credentials are silently indistinguishable from 'no register entity matched'

`src/leadgen/person/register.py:334` · silent-failure

**What happens.** `people_from_register` guards only on `is_configured()`, which just checks that the two env vars are non-empty. If the credentials are wrong or expired, Zefix answers 401; `http.post_json` returns None for any status >= 400 (src/job_scraper/http.py:657-660) with no log above debug; `find_entity` iterates `_as_entities(None)` -> `[]` and returns None; `people_from_register` returns []. That is the exact same observable as 'this company is not in the register under that name', which the docstring explicitly calls intended behaviour. The CLI warns only when the variables are *absent* (cli.py:273). An operator with a typo'd password gets a clean-looking run with zero register rows and nothing in the log pointing at authentication.

**Failure.** Operator exports ZEFIX_PASSWORD with a trailing newline from a secrets file. `--register` reports no warning, every one of the ~20% of Swiss companies that publish no staff returns [], and the funnel shows register_companies=0. The operator concludes the register 'has no data on these companies' — which is what the code comment at cli.py:274 says must never happen.

**Fix.** Have the HTTP layer surface the status (or add a `post_json_status` / `last_status`) so `find_entity` can distinguish 401/403 from an empty result set. On the first authentication failure, log a warning naming the env vars and set a module flag so the rest of the run skips the register instead of issuing thousands of doomed requests.

### roles.py leaves common EU HR and executive titles unclassified, dropping them from the targeted set

`src/leadgen/person/roles.py:18` · correctness

**What happens.** Several high-frequency EU titles fall through to 'other', which removes them from TARGET_FAMILIES and from the `target_role` funnel count. Specific gaps confirmed by running classify_role: (a) French recruitment is spelled 'recrutement' — `recruit\w*` needs the letter i and `rekrut\w*` is the German stem, so 'Responsable du recrutement' and 'Chargée de recrutement' are unclassified; (b) `(?:vize)?pr[äa]sident(?:in)?` covers only the German spelling, so French 'Président' and Italian 'Presidente' / 'Presidente del Consiglio di Amministrazione' are unclassified; (c) Nordic HR titles 'Personalsjef' (NO), 'Personalansvarig' (SV) and Finnish 'Henkilöstöpäällikkö' are unclassified even though paths.py configures NO/SE/FI; (d) 'Head of People & Culture' — now the dominant modern HR title — matches neither the `people (?:operations|ops|team|partner|lead|manager|director)` list nor anything else; (e) there is no Polish vocabulary at all ('Dyrektor Personalny', 'Kierownik Działu Kadr', 'Prezes Zarządu'); (f) German 'Kaufmännischer Leiter' and 'Prokurist' are unclassified. Additionally 'Chief People Officer' classifies as executive rather than hr because `chief\s+\w+(?:\s+\w+)?\s+officer` in EXEC catches it before any HR pattern would.

**Failure.** press.py drops a hit outright when `classify_role(role) == 'other'`, so a French press release quoting 'Marie Dupont, Responsable du recrutement chez Acme' yields nothing. A Norwegian team page naming 'Kari Nordmann, Personalsjef' produces a lead whose person_role_family is 'other', so it is excluded from the target_role count and from any downstream target filter — the HR contact the buyer paid for is found and then discarded.

**Fix.** Add to HR_PATTERNS: `recrut\w*`, `personal(?:sjef|ansvarlig|ansvarig|direktor)`, `henkil[öo]st[öo]\w*`, `people\s*(?:&|and)?\s*culture`, `kadr\w*`, `dyrektor personaln\w*`. Add to EXECUTIVE_PATTERNS: `pr[éeäa]sident(?:e|in)?`, `consiglio d[i']\s*amministrazione`, `prezes(?:\s+zarz[ąa]du)?`, `prokurist(?:in)?`, `kaufm[äa]nnische[rn]? leiter(?:in)?`. Move a `chief\s+(?:people|human\s+resources|hr|talent)\s+officer` alternative into HR_PATTERNS so it is tested before the generic C-level catch-all.

### Sitemap mining and the same-site check let another company's pages be crawled and its people filed under this company's email domain

`src/leadgen/person/strategies/sitemap.py:124` · data-quality

**What happens.** `person_urls(domain, ...)` seeds the queue with the company's own sitemap paths, then does `queue.extend(nested)` with no check that the nested `<loc>` is on `domain` - a sitemap index can point anywhere. The page-URL filter is only `_looks_like_a_person_url(page) and page not in found and is_safe_url(page)` (line 126); `is_safe_url` only rejects internal address space, not other companies. The returned URLs go to `cascade._harvest`, and every hit is then handed to `assemble.build_leads` with `company.domain`, where `_resolve_email` mints `apply_pattern(pattern, first, last, company.domain)` (assemble.py:73). The same defect exists in `cascade.discover_people_pages`, whose same-site test is the substring `domain not in parsed.netloc` (cascade.py:148) rather than a suffix match, and in `_harvest`, which hands the post-redirect `final_url` to the extractors while still filing the results under the original company.

**Failure.** A marketing agency's sitemap index for kunde-a.de includes the sitemaps it also generates for kunde-b.de. `person_urls('kunde-a.de', ...)` follows the nested loc, finds https://kunde-b.de/team/anna-schmidt, harvests it, and `build_leads` emits anna.schmidt@kunde-a.de - a real person, a fabricated address at an unrelated company. It bounces, and enough of them damage the sending domain's reputation.

**Fix.** Restrict nested sitemap `<loc>` and selected page URLs to the registrable domain of the company being crawled (use tldextract, already a dependency). Replace `domain not in parsed.netloc` with `netloc == domain or netloc.endswith('.' + domain)`. In `_harvest`, verify `final_url`'s host still belongs to the company before running the extractors, since redirects can cross domains.

### Sitemap person-URL matching rejects .html and underscore slugs and omits the Swiss 'geschaeftsleitung' segment

`src/leadgen/person/strategies/sitemap.py:27` · correctness

**What happens.** `_PERSON_PATH_RX` anchors the slug with `(?P<slug>[a-z0-9][a-z0-9-]{2,60})/?$`, which rejects any file extension and any underscore. TYPO3, Contao and many other EU CMSs emit `/team/anna-schmidt.html`; WordPress sites configured with underscores emit `/team/anna_schmidt`. The path-segment alternation also lists `geschaeftsfuehrung` but not `geschaeftsleitung`, which is the Swiss word — and paths.py's CH list is built around exactly that word (`/geschaeftsleitung`, `/ueber-uns/management`). Separately, `person_urls` seeds the queue with three root sitemap candidates and caps total documents at `MAX_SITEMAP_DOCS = 5`, counting failed probes in `seen_docs`; on a site that only has /sitemap.xml, the two 404 probes burn two of the five slots, leaving just two nested sitemaps from what is often a 20-entry index.

**Failure.** A Swiss company runs TYPO3 and lists 40 per-person pages at /ueber-uns/geschaeftsleitung/<name>.html in its sitemap index. Every one is rejected by the slug regex and by the missing path segment, and the two 404 root probes mean only two nested sitemaps would have been read anyway. The strategy the docstring calls 'cheaper and far more precise than guessing' contributes nothing.

**Fix.** Allow an optional extension and underscores in the slug: `(?P<slug>[a-z0-9][a-z0-9_-]{2,60})(?:\.(?:html?|php|aspx))?/?$`, and strip the extension before the `_NOT_A_PERSON` check. Add `geschaeftsleitung`, `verwaltungsrat`, `direktion`, `ons-team`, `medewerkers` to the segment list. Count only successfully fetched documents against MAX_SITEMAP_DOCS, and read the `Sitemap:` directive from robots.txt before falling back to the three guessed paths.

### team.py loses the role whenever the name is split across inline elements inside the heading

`src/leadgen/person/strategies/team.py:99` · correctness

**What happens.** The role is found by locating the name as an exact line: `if name in lines: index = lines.index(name)`. `name` comes from `tag.get_text(" ", strip=True)` (single-space joined) while `lines` comes from `card.get_text("\n", strip=True).split("\n")` (newline separated). When the heading wraps the given name and surname in separate inline elements — `<h3><span class="first">Anna</span> <span class="last">Schmidt</span></h3>`, a very common pattern on styled team cards — the two renderings disagree ('Anna Schmidt' vs 'Anna' + 'Schmidt' on separate lines), the lookup misses, and role stays ''. The person is still exported but with person_role_family='other', so they drop out of the target set.

**Failure.** A team page renders each name as `<h3><span>Anna</span> <span>Schmidt</span></h3>` with the title in the following `<p>`. Every person on the page is exported with an empty role, classify_role returns 'other', and the entire page contributes zero rows to the target_role count even though half of them are HR and engineering leads.

**Fix.** Do not match on the flattened text. Find the role by walking the DOM forward from the heading element — the next sibling (or the next non-empty text node inside the card that is not itself a `_NAME_TAGS` element) — rather than by looking the name up in a list of newline-split strings. Fall back to whitespace-normalising both sides before comparing.

### 14% of arbeitnow rows carry a German town that geo cannot resolve, so those companies are dropped by the geography filter

`src/leadgen/seed/arbeitnow.py:59` · coverage

**What happens.** `_listing` sets `country=country_from_location(location)` and arbeitnow's `location` is usually a bare town name. `geo.CITY_NAMES` deliberately lists only large, unambiguous cities, so Mittelstand towns fall through to "". A single-ad company in such a town gets `country_of(group) == ""` and is removed by the `countries is not None` filter in jobboard.py before any crawling - despite arbeitnow being, by construction, the German-market seed that a `--countries europe` run most wants.

**Failure.** `--arbeitnow-pages 20` harvests ~5,000 rows; ~700 of them name a German town geo cannot resolve. Companies whose only ad is in Dresden are filtered out of a `--countries europe` run as 'not in the requested geography'.

**Fix.** Arbeitnow is a German-market feed: default its listings to country 'DE' when `country_from_location` returns nothing and the text is not an obviously foreign/remote string, or widen CITY_NAMES with the German towns the feed actually uses. Either way, log how many listings were dropped for an unresolvable location.

### guess_domains treats DNS resolution as proof of ownership, attributing strangers' staff to the wrong employer

`src/leadgen/seed/atsboards.py:182` · data-quality

**What happens.** `guess_domains` builds name-stem x TLD candidates and accepts any that `_resolves_to_public` says exists. Resolving is not owning: parked domains, squatters, and unrelated companies on a different TLD all resolve. The docstring says this was the Empa -> empa.com / Suva -> suva.de bug and that ordering the national TLD first fixed it, but ordering only changes which wrong answer wins when the right one does not exist - the function still returns up to MAX_GUESSES=4 domains and the caller crawls them all, filing whatever people it finds under the seed company.

**Failure.** A board slug 'empa' with no website hint produces empa.ch, empa.com, empa.io. The pipeline crawls all of them, finds an Impressum on a squatter's empa.com, extracts that registrant's name, and emits it as a lead for the Swiss research institute with an invented empa.ch address. The operator cannot tell it from a real lead.

**Fix.** Require corroboration before accepting a guessed domain: the fetched homepage's <title>/og:site_name or Impressum must contain the company's trading name, or an MX record must exist, before the domain is crawled. Return at most one domain, and mark leads derived from a guessed (rather than API-supplied) domain with a distinct, lower-confidence status so the quota selector ranks them below evidenced ones.

### Thirteen EU/EFTA countries have no national TLD anywhere in the guessing tables

`src/leadgen/seed/atsboards.py:57` · correctness

**What happens.** COUNTRY_TLD maps 19 countries. The EU/EFTA codes with no entry are BG, CY, EE, GR, HR, HU, IS, LT, LV, MT, RO, SI and SK — and DOMAIN_TLDS, the fallback list used when the country is unknown, contains none of those TLDs either (nor .be, .dk, .no, .cz, .pt, .ie or .uk). So for a Romanian, Greek, Hungarian, Bulgarian or Baltic company, domain guessing can only ever land on .com/.de/.ch/.io/.co/.fr/.nl/.at/.fi/.se/.eu/.ai/.es/.it/.pl.
